#10232. On-Device Speech Recognition and Text-to-Speech in Google AI Edge Gallery

Today, we are introducing on-device Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) and Text-to-Speech (TTS) capabilities in Google AI Edge Gallery. Voice is often the most natural way to interact with AI on phones and wearables. Recent advances in compact speech architectures now make it possible to run accurate, multilingual ASR and expressive TTS models entirely on device, all within a small memory footprint, with low latency, and completely offline. By pairing these lightweight speech models with on-device small language models (SLMs) like Gemini Nano and Gemma 4, developers can build apps that stream live transcriptions as the user speaks and synthesize natural spoken replies locally.

This release brings together major advancements across both Google’s system speech models and the open-weight ecosystem:

Below, we’ll explore how this local voice pipeline powers the new XR (Extended Reality) Language Teacher showcase, upgrades AI Chat in Google AI Edge Gallery, and how you can build with these APIs and models today.

Learning a language while traveling happens in the real world, often in underground stations, busy markets, or abroad where Wi-Fi and roaming data may not be available. To show how vision, language, and speech models can work together 100% locally on your phone with no network connection needed, we built the XR Language Teacher showcase in Google AI Edge Gallery. Simply point your camera at an unfamiliar object or menu, ask "What is that, and how do I ask for one?", and immediately hear an explanation in your language alongside authentic pronunciation in the local language.

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

With inference running locally on a paired phone, the same experience works standalone on a phone and extends naturally to hands-free smart glasses. To see how we adapted Language Teacher for intelligent glasses check out our recent talk at Android Dev Summit: Building Audio-First Experiences for Intelligent Eyewear with Gemini Nano.

Beyond the XR Language Teacher showcase, we also built ASR and TTS directly into Google AI Edge Gallery’s main AI Chat feature, giving on-device SLMs like Gemini Nano and Gemma 4 a natural, offline voice interface while on the go.

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

While multimodal models like Gemma 4 can process audio inputs directly, we provide a modular solution for cascading language models with separate, lightweight voice models for latency optimization and flexibility. Depending on your target devices and model requirements, our open-source code on GitHub shows you the optimized and recommended solution for Android using ML Kit, as well as MediaPipe Tasks and LiteRT for cross-platform coverage and open model support.

For developers building for Android devices, ML Kit’s GenAI Speech Recognition and Text-to-Speech APIs provide a standardized, platform-integrated path out of the box. With the underlying speech models managed through AICore and Google’s on-device speech services, developers get production-grade quality without having to manage model downloads or worry about APK size bloat. Your app also benefits automatically from hardware acceleration across NPU and TPU backends (when available) and seamless background model updates.

Integrating ASR and TTS into cross-platform applications usually requires handling low-level audio framing, Voice Activity Detection (VAD), and complex decoding pipelines. MediaPipe Task simplifies this by providing unified, turnkey ASR and TTS APIs across iOS, macOS, Windows, Linux, and Web that abstract away manual pre-processing, audio stream routing, and platform-specific audio management into simple, high-level interfaces:

Under the hood, ML Kit and MediaPipe Tasks are powered by LiteRT, Google’s high-performance on-device AI inference infrastructure. Cross-platform applications requiring even more fine-grained control over speech processing or custom model architectures can also build on it directly.

Speech models often rely on multiple subgraphs and auxiliary assets, such as encoder/decoder subgraphs and tokenizers for ASR, or phonemizer dictionaries, voice embeddings, and vocoders for TTS. All required components for each model are packaged into a single .litertlm file for easy deployment. The app ships with ready-to-use .litertlm models, including Parakeet-TDT-0.6B-v3 (multilingual ASR), PocketTTS 100M (multilingual TTS), Phonon 31M (ultra-lightweight English TTS), and Kokoro-82M (multilingual TTS). Beyond these, the vibrant Hugging Face LiteRT Community with over 10K+ followers offers a growing catalog of open-weight models, like Whisper, Qwen3-ASR and Moonshine for ASR, or Qwen3-TTS and Matcha for TTS. For developers seeking custom implementations or support for additional open-weight voice models, LiteRT provides a rich set of multimodal model examples to help you get started, with more on the way.

Adding a local voice pipeline takes on-device SLMs beyond typing-based chat interfaces, enabling natural, voice-first interactions that work anywhere, completely offline. All the building blocks to try these experiences or integrate them into your own apps are available today:

We'd like to extend a special thanks to our significant contributors for their work on this project:

#10231. The Galaxy A56 is a decent starter phone at just $299 right now

If you are looking for a reliable, everyday handset without spending flagship money, Samsung’s mid-range lineup is usually a safe bet. Right now, the Samsung Galaxy A56 has dropped to just $299 on Amazon, which makes a sizable $200 dent in its original $499 retail price.

At under $300, the biggest selling point here isn’t just the upfront savings; it is the longevity. Samsung committed to six years of OS and security updates for the Galaxy A56, meaning this device will stay current and secure well into 2032. For a budget-tier purchase, getting that kind of software runway is hard to beat and only matched by Google with the Pixel 10a.

Hardware-wise, the A56 punches above this sale price. You get a crisp 6.7-inch 120Hz Super AMOLED display, an IP67 dust- and water-resistance rating, and a 5,000mAh battery with 45W wired charging, matching the charging speeds of Samsung’s flagship models. The triple-camera array, led by a 50MP main sensor with OIS, won’t win any photography awards against Ultra-tier phones, though it is plenty capable for casual snaps. Plus, One UI brings plenty of handy Samsung ecosystem features and baked-in AI tools like Object Eraser.

Of course, the Google Pixel 10a is the obvious rival in this space. If you can catch the Pixel 10a on sale around the $350 mark, its cleaner Android experience, seven-year update promise, and superior computational photography make it a very tempting alternative.

That said, it hasn’t been available at that price point for a while and can only be picked up for $400 if you buy renewed. Which annoyingly means the Pixel is sitting closer to its $499 MSRP and has last years specs anyway, so in many ways that makes the Galaxy A56 potentially a smarter buy for your wallet despite being a year older.

In my opinion, for first-time smartphone owners, kids, or anyone who just wants a large, smooth screen and years of updates on a budget, $299 is tough to argue with.

OLED gaming monitors are hot right now, and Black Friday is an excellent time to be in the market.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

OLED panels are quickly becoming a must-have item for desktop PCs. Most of us were introduced to the panel technology in smartphones, and it’s become increasingly popular on laptops. For desktop gamers seeking an immediate visual upgrade, OLED is the way to go, and fortunately, Prime Day is proving to be a great time to take the plunge. From massive DQHD panels on the Samsung Odyssey OLED G9 (G93SC) to more value-oriented offerings like the Acer Nitro VG277U Xbiip, we have you covered.

Here are the best OLED gaming monitors that we’ve seen so far:

Mega-ultrawide gaming monitors are not for the faint of heart. Not only can these monitors be quite expensive, but they can also occupy a substantial amount of desktop space. However, for those who have the need (and the space), there are options such as the Samsung 49" Odyssey OLED G9 (G91SD), which is currently on sale for just $799.99 at Amazon.

It features a gorgeous 49-inch QD-OLED panel with a 5120 x 1440 resolution, but instead of a maximum 240 Hz refresh rate, you'll top out at 144 Hz. Brightness tops out at 250 nits (typical), and you'll still get a fast 0.03 ms GtG response time. The monitor supports AMD FreeSync Premium Pro, and it's also Nvidia G-Sync compatible. As for ports, you'll find one DisplayPort 1.4, two HDMI 2.1, and two downstream USB-C ports.

The Samsung Odyssey OLED G9 (G91SD) is a slightly less ambitious 49-inch QD-OLED gaming monitor with a DQHD resolution. Instead of a 240 Hz top speed, the G91SD dials it back to a maximum of 144 Hz.

The AOC Agon Pro AG346UCD is a highly desirable QD-OLED monitor that strikes a sweet spot with its 34-inch WQHD panel and a 165 Hz refresh rate.

If you're looking for something more in a “mid-range” size class, the AOC Agon Pro AG346UCD should be on your shopping list. This is a 34-inch QD-OLED monitor with a 3440 x 1440 resolution and a 165 Hz refresh rate. While that figure may not be as high as the 240 Hz more commonly found with 34-inch panels, the Agon Pro AG346UCD is priced at just $599, taking $50 off its typical asking price. In our review, we praised the monitor for its well-saturated color, excellent contrast, and out-of-the-box accuracy.

The Samsung Odyssey OLED G50SF is a 27-inch 1440p QD-OLED gaming monitor that offers up to 180 Hz refresh rate, 99% coverage of DCI-P3, 0.03ms of response time, and a bunch of features to prevent OLED burn-in.

The Asus ROG Strix XG27ACDNG might be small in stature, but its 27-inch QHD panel delivers a sizzling 360 Hz maximum refresh rate.

One of the best things to happen to gaming monitors in recent years has been the emergence of OLED panel technology. OLED panels are fast, offer rich color and blacker blacks, but have come with a big price premium. But as the technology has matured, prices have come down. That fact is exemplified by the 27-inch Samsung Odyssey OLED G50SF, which is currently priced at $319.99.

The Odyssey OLED G50SF features a QD-OLED panel measuring 27 inches with a QHD resolution of 2560×1440, which is currently the sweet spot for PC gaming. It supports up to a 180 Hz refresh rate via DisplayPort and 144 Hz through HDMI. Samsung claims that the display offers a response time of 0.03ms (GTG), essentially offering smooth and responsive visuals during fast action. With G-Sync compatibility and support for AMD FreeSync, expect zero screen lag and image tearing.

Another option is the Asus ROG Strix XG27ACDNG, which also sports a 27-inch QHD QD-OLED panel. When the ROG Strix XG27ACDNG arrived in our testing lab, we noted its accuracy, fast response, and stunning image quality. The monitor also boasts a 360 Hz refresh rate for ultra-twitch gamers. Currently, the ROG Strix XG27ACDNG is on sale for $549, representing a 28% savings.

#10227. Samsung’s Galaxy Z Fold 9 lineup looks like it’ll include a new TriFold, leak suggests

Following its short-lived Galaxy Z TriFold, Samsung appears to have a sequel on the way that’s set to arrive in the Galaxy Z Fold 9 lineup.

The Galaxy Z TriFold launched a few months after the Galaxy Z Fold 7, carrying the codename “Q7m.” GalaxyClub now reports that “Q9m” has surfaced, implying that Galaxy Z TriFold 2 will be based on the Galaxy Z Fold 9 series.

For one, don’t expect a new TriFold to arrive in the same schedule as the first one. The original Galaxy Z TriFold launched in late 2025 before expanding to other regions in early 2026. While it’s abundantly clear we’re not getting a new model before the year lets out, this codename effectively rules out a release before the Galaxy Z Fold 9 series launches which, assuming it follows the usual schedule, won’t happen until around July.

This also tells us that the Galaxy Z TriFold 2 will be based on the Galaxy Z Fold 9 series, likely including the latest chipset and Samsung’s Android 18 build.

There’s no guarantee yet about when the next TriFold might debut, how much it might cost, or where it might launch. GalaxyClub notes that the codename subtly suggests there’s no EU release in the plans, but it can’t be completely ruled out either.

Whatever happens with Galaxy Z TriFold 2, let’s just hope it lasts longer than the original.

#10226. How Developers Can Detect Fake Payment Gateways and Build Secure Checkout Integrations

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Online payments are an essential part of modern web applications. E-commerce platforms, subscription services, and online marketplaces all depend on payment gateways to process transactions and manage customer orders.

However, integrating a payment gateway involves more than adding a payment button or displaying a checkout form. Developers must ensure that payment requests are sent to legitimate providers, transaction information is validated correctly, and payment confirmations cannot be manipulated.

Fake payment gateways introduce another challenge. They can imitate legitimate payment interfaces, use copied checkout templates, and redirect customers to fraudulent websites. Even when a checkout page looks professional, its underlying implementation may not be trustworthy.

For developers, understanding these risks is important when designing payment workflows. This article explores common payment integration vulnerabilities and practical techniques for building secure checkout systems using JavaScript and Node.js.

A payment gateway connects an application with a payment processing service. It handles transaction requests and communicates payment results to the merchant's application.

A typical checkout process involves several components:

A frontend checkout interface where customers review their orders.

A backend application that creates payment requests.

A payment provider that processes transactions.

A webhook endpoint that receives payment notifications.

A database that stores order and transaction information.

Attackers can target different parts of this process.

For example, a fraudulent website may copy the appearance of a legitimate checkout page while sending submitted information to an unauthorized server. Another approach involves manipulating payment requests or creating fake confirmation pages that make an unsuccessful transaction appear complete.

These techniques are also relevant to marketplace fraud. An eBay scam can involve fraudulent payment requests and misleading transaction links, illustrating why developers need to consider payment security beyond the appearance of a checkout page.

The important distinction is that a legitimate-looking interface does not establish that the payment request is reaching an authorized provider.

One of the first steps in reviewing a payment integration is checking where the application sends its payment requests.

Developers should inspect network requests using browser developer tools. The Network tab can reveal which domains receive requests when a customer opens the checkout page or submits a payment form.

Look for unexpected requests, unfamiliar domains, and scripts loaded from sources that are not part of the approved integration.

For example, a checkout application might load a payment script like this:

The URL above is illustrative, not an actual payment provider.

Developers should compare the script URL and integration method with the provider's official documentation rather than trusting a URL simply because it appears familiar.

Reviewing external JavaScript dependencies.

Checking whether payment forms submit data to approved endpoints.

Confirming that HTTPS is used throughout the payment process.

Reviewing changes to payment-related scripts during development.

A Content Security Policy (CSP) can also restrict which domains are allowed to load scripts and receive certain types of requests.

These example domains must be replaced with the actual domains documented by the selected payment provider.

A carefully configured policy can reduce the risk of unauthorized scripts and unexpected form submissions.

A common mistake in payment development is trusting values submitted directly from the browser.

Consider an application where the frontend sends an order ID and payment amount to the backend.

An attacker could modify the amount before submitting the request. If the backend accepts that value without checking the original order, the application may create a payment session for an incorrect amount.

The safer approach is to retrieve the order from the database and calculate the amount on the server.

Here, payment Gateway create Checkout Session() represents a provider-specific server-side SDK method.

The important part is that the backend retrieves the amount from its own database rather than accepting a price supplied by the customer.

Developers should also validate product IDs, quantities, discounts, currency, and order ownership before creating a payment session.

This prevents frontend manipulation from directly determining the final transaction amount.

Developers should avoid building payment processing around unverified scripts, copied templates, or unknown third-party services.

Instead, use official APIs and SDKs provided by established payment processors.

A hosted checkout integration is one common option. The merchant's backend creates a payment session through the provider's API, and the customer is redirected to the provider's hosted checkout page.

This approach can reduce the amount of sensitive card information handled by the merchant's application.

However, developers still need to configure the integration correctly.

Store secret API keys in server-side environment variables or a secrets manager.

Never expose private credentials in frontend JavaScript.

Use the provider's official SDK where available.

Validate checkout session creation on the backend.

Restrict success and cancellation URLs to approved destinations.

Use HTTPS for application and payment communication.

Developers should also understand the difference between public and secret API credentials. Public keys may be designed for browser use, while secret keys must remain on the server.

The OWASP Third-Party Payment Gateway Integration Cheat Sheet provides additional guidance on securing payment requests, validating transaction details, and handling payment callbacks.

Payment providers often use webhooks to notify applications when transactions are completed, failed, or refunded.

However, webhook endpoints are publicly accessible URLs in many integrations. Without proper verification, an attacker may send a fabricated HTTP request that looks like a successful payment notification.

Developers should verify webhook signatures using the payment provider's official SDK.

Consider this simplified Node.js example:

The verify Webhook() method represents the signature verification functionality of a payment provider's SDK. It is not a universal Node.js method.

The example also assumes that the provider's event contains the relevant order and transaction details. Developers should follow the provider's actual event schema and retrieve the authoritative payment status when required.

The application should never mark an order as paid simply because a customer has reached a success URL.

Instead, payment confirmation should depend on an authenticated provider notification or a server-side verification request.

Another important issue in payment development is duplicate processing.

Imagine a customer clicks the payment button twice because the first request takes too long. The application may create two payment sessions or attempt to charge the customer more than once.

Network interruptions and automatic retries can create similar situations.

Idempotency helps prevent these problems by ensuring that repeated requests with the same idempotency key do not create duplicate operations.

A simplified example using Node.js might look like this:

The example uses a provider-specific idempotency option. Actual SDK syntax and key requirements vary between providers.

In a production application, developers should also store payment attempts in the database and enforce appropriate uniqueness constraints.

This helps maintain consistent transaction records even when requests are repeated or interrupted.

A payment integration should be tested beyond the normal successful checkout process.

Most established payment providers offer sandbox environments that allow developers to simulate transactions without charging real customers.

These environments are useful for testing different transaction scenarios.

Developers should also test whether the application handles provider downtime correctly.

For example, if the payment provider becomes temporarily unavailable, the application should not mark an order as completed or assume that the transaction succeeded.

Automated integration tests can help verify these scenarios before deployment. Monitoring payment failures, unexpected status changes, and repeated webhook errors can also help developers identify problems after release.

Security does not end when a payment integration goes live.

Developers should implement monitoring to identify unusual transaction patterns and unexpected changes in payment behavior.

Recording payment attempts and transaction IDs.

Monitoring webhook signature verification failures.

Detecting repeated requests for the same order.

Alerting on unexpected order status changes.

However, logging must be implemented carefully.

Applications should never store card verification codes or expose secret API keys in application logs. Sensitive information should be excluded from debugging output, and access to transaction records should be restricted.

Developers should also follow the payment provider's security requirements and applicable PCI DSS obligations when handling payment data.

Detecting fake payment gateways and building secure checkout integrations requires more than checking whether a payment page looks legitimate.

Developers need to examine the complete transaction flow, validate payment information on the server, use official payment APIs, verify webhook signatures, and prevent duplicate transactions.

JavaScript and Node.js provide useful tools for implementing these security controls, but the reliability of a payment system ultimately depends on how its frontend, backend, database, and payment provider communicate.

A well-designed checkout integration should verify every important transaction before fulfilling an order. By following these development practices, teams can build payment systems that are easier to maintain, more reliable, and better protected against common payment manipulation techniques.

#10224. Nearly every major smartphone brand is slashing targets for 2027

According to a new report out of China, nearly every major smartphone brand is rethinking its shipment targets for 2027 and slashing its numbers, some by nearly 30%.

Jiemian News reports (via @jukan05) that nearly every major brand will be cutting back its forecasts for 2027 compared to 2026. This includes:

These are just targets and subject to change, but they show that smartphone brands recognize that 2027 is going to be a much slower year amid the tight constraints of RAM, chip, and other component shortages and rising prices. Honor, notably, isn’t mentioned as the company is apparently aiming for the same 50 million units shipped as it was targeting in 2026 following a Q3 where it actually managed growth.

Check the best prices on DDR5 and DDR4 memory kits.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

In today's market, memory has become one of the most critical and expensive components in any build, and prices have only worsened thanks to the ongoing global DRAM shortage. We're keeping an eye on the volatile memory pricing to help identify any good deals that may arise.

Now, even more so than before the shortage began, price tags are fluctuating significantly from day to day, and it's becoming increasingly complex to tell when you're getting a fair deal versus overpaying for the same memory kit. We've compiled this comprehensive RAM price tracker that monitors the availability and pricing of different DDR5 and DDR4 memory kits across major U.S. retailers. Our goal is to take the guesswork out of memory shopping and help you make an informed purchasing decision.

We update this index regularly with the lowest prices available in the U.S. market for each memory kit. Our tracker covers a range of capacities and speeds across both budget and premium brands. We list the lowest price for each available memory kit, regardless of manufacturer, so you see the absolute best deals currently on offer.

The general availability of many memory kits is limited, with truly exceptional deals scarce and often selling out within seconds of appearing. High-capacity kits, particularly DDR5, frequently show the most severe stock shortages and price premiums. Therefore, timing and vigilance are especially important when shopping for memory in current times. Whether you're budget-conscious and want the best bang for your buck, or you're building a premium system and want the best RAM for gaming available, our index further below gives you the data you need to make the right purchase.

You can also check out our list of the best RAM and our RAM benchmark hierarchy to see evergreen performance data to help you make an informed decision when choosing a new GPU for your system.

Here are a few exceptional Black Friday RAM deals, but below that, we have a full list of all the lowest-priced RAM on the market.

Get a great deal on standalone RAM from Newegg. This deal is currently the lowest priced DDR5-6000 available. The T-Force Delta RGB not only adds flair to your chassis, but does so with decent speeds.

This RGB-laden kit from Corsair sports a CAS latency of 36 and is tested at 1.35 volts. This pair of 16GB memory sticks is colored black on the aluminum heat spreader, with RGB lighting running along the spine.

Above are a few of the standout deals from the Black Friday event, which is currently taking place. For a more extensive list of Black Friday deals, head to our Black Friday RAM deals article.

DDR5 memory prices have exploded over the last year, with triple and, in some cases, quadruple increases. For instance, a conventional 32GB (2x16GB) DDR5-6000 memory kit sold for around $100 to $200 in October 2025, but the same kit now starts at $430, if it's even in stock.

Retailers are taking advantage of the DRAM shortage, just as they did with the graphics card shortage a few years ago. They've started raising DDR5 prices to align with current market conditions and sometimes bundle DDR5 memory kits with processor or motherboard purchases.

Nonetheless, these DDR5 memory kits sometimes sell for more reasonable prices. However, you have to be quick to pull the trigger, as scalpers and automated shopping bots tend to gobble up what little stock is left.

The rising prices of DDR5 memory kits have forced some consumers to stick with DDR4. However, DDR4 has also seen price hikes over the last three months, though not as drastic as DDR5. We've seen the pricing double to triple.

For reference, it was common to find 32GB (2x16GB) DDR4 memory kits selling for between $60 to $90 back in October 2025. Jump to January 2026, and the same memory kit could cost anywhere between $150 and $180, so DDR4 isn't exactly a safe haven for consumers.

We've seen more reasonably priced DDR4 memory kits on the market than DDR5. Logically, it makes little sense to build a DDR4-based system now due to processor and motherboard availability. However, if you're in desperate need of a DDR4 memory upgrade, there are viable options on the market.

#10221. A2UI 1.0: Build delightful, fast, reliable, agentic experiences

User interactions with agents today are where computers were in the terminal era: capable, and answering almost everything in text. Users can ask an agent for anything by typing or talking, and what comes back is usually a wall of text, a poor way to compare three products or edit and approve a detailed plan.

Today, we're releasing A2UI 1.0, the first stable version of our open protocol for generative UI. It lets AI agents answer with interactive screens, such as a form to confirm or a chart. Developers can use it now with open-source renderer SDKs for the web, Android, and iOS and agent SDKs for Python, TypeScript, and Dart. Companies such as Atlassian, Alation, Bloomberg, and ByteDance are already building with it.

With A2UI, the agent describes what to show, and every app draws the UI with its own native components. The application receives A2UI messages and each app renders the content securely and at native speed. Users get the same low latency, high trust, and complete native fidelity whether they reach a company's agent in its own app or in a third-party AI assistant that hosts MCP apps, such as Gemini or Claude.

You wake up sick. Getting through the morning takes six apps: book a video visit, move your meetings, tell your team, turn on an out-of-office reply, ask your partner to do school pickup and order soup. Ask a text-only agent instead, and you get a numbered wall of text, then round after round of “which ones?”. A2UI won't handle your sick day, but it gives developers the framework to build an agent that can. That agent could answer with one view of all six apps. Changing your plan is a couple of taps instead of another round of messages, and nothing is sent until you confirm.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

The same agent output works on any screen. Ask an agent to “move dinner with Priya to Friday”, and it can answer with one view that lines up your calendar, open tables from a restaurant booking app and prices from a rideshare app, so the time that works for everyone is obvious. The developer builds that view once with A2UI, and it renders natively on your phone and on your laptop.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

In both examples, A2UI is the framework that simplifies building these experiences across platforms. Developers build the agent and choose the platforms it works with. A2UI gives them the protocol the agent uses to describe each view, a way to combine components from different apps in one view, and renderers that draw it natively on the web, Android, and iOS.

New to A2UI? Start with the A2UI Playground by CopilotKit or check out industry examples.

Atlassian is bringing A2UI to Jira, where teams can assign work items to AI agents. When an agent needs a follow-up from the team to finish a work item, such as a missing detail or a decision, it asks with an interactive card in the work item, and the team answers right there in Jira.

The Alation Intelligence Operating System (AIOS) builds its feeds using A2UI: metric cards, charts, and more. Each chart can show the SQL query and data behind it. Ask Claude for a feed about profitable products, and the feed renders in the conversation using A2UI to render metric cards, charts, and custom components. The same feed with the same information can be viewed directly inside Alation with a nearly identical layout.

ByteDance released an A2UI renderer for Lynx, its cross-platform framework, in June 2026. Lynx keeps streamed screens smooth and ships a default set of components built on its `lynx-ui` library. In the Lynx GenUI Playground, you can watch A2UI messages rendered natively, for example, as a two-day trip planner for Kyoto. Lynx supports A2UI 0.9 today and is adapting to 1.0 with the A2UI team.

Ask Gemini Notebook for a slide deck and it replies with a card that asks two things: presenter slides or a detailed deck, and which topics to focus on. A review card follows with the format, focus, and visual style, which you can edit before it generates the deck. The same cards guide Video Overviews on the web and on mobile.

Organizations can register their own agents that respond with A2UI. Gemini renders those responses using the components in its A2UI component gallery, and supports A2UI 0.9.1 today. The Google Cloud documentation covers registering an agent and hosting one on Cloud Run.

Several other Google teams, including some behind our largest products, are building features with A2UI.

Several other Google teams, including some behind our largest products, are building features with A2UI that haven’t launched yet. We’ll share more as they ship.

A2UI and MCP Apps work together. Alation already uses both to show its A2UI feeds in Claude. Our June post, A2UI + MCP Apps, covers three ways to combine them, and the A2UI in MCP Apps guide shows how to run A2UI screens inside an MCP App.

Jetpack Compose support for 1.0 is coming soon. Agents in Gemini Enterprise can already use A2UI, and we're working on upgrading Gemini Enterprise to A2UI 1.0. The A2UI roadmap covers the rest, from design-system primitives that render natively on every platform to official SDK support for decision models like Jev.

We’ve collaborated with partners who have built A2UI 1.0 demos across different industries to give you inspiration on how to adapt A2UI to your use cases. Explore what they’ve built:

From Files to Cited Answers in One API Call: Managed Ingestion and SearchDocuments in Agent Retrieval

#10219. How to Test Android Apps on Windows: A Practical Guide to Emulators and APK Testing

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Most Android bugs are not exotic. They are a button hidden behind a keyboard, a screen that breaks on a smaller display, a permission prompt nobody tested, or an install that fails on an older version of the system. You can catch many of these problems without owning a single test phone, as long as you set up your Windows PC properly and test with a plan.

This guide covers how to prepare Windows, how to choose and configure an emulator, how to install and verify APK files with Android Debug Bridge (ADB), what a good test session checks, and where virtual devices stop being trustworthy. It includes a small Python helper that explains install failures and reads launch times from ADB output, with tests. Tool versions and menu names change, so confirm details against each vendor’s current documentation.

● Emulators find the large, repeatable problems fast. Use them for functional checks, layouts and install testing, and confirm hardware behavior on real devices.

● Prepare Windows first. Enable hardware virtualization and the Windows Hypervisor Platform, and check the result with the emulator’s own acceleration check.

● Match the virtual device to real users. Test the oldest and newest Android versions you support, a small screen and a modest memory profile.

● Learn ADB. It makes installs, data resets and evidence capture scriptable and repeatable.

● Do not trust emulator performance numbers. Your PC is usually faster than your users’ phones.

Testing on a desktop gives you speed and control that physical phones rarely match. A fresh install takes seconds. You can wipe app data, change the screen size, switch the Android version and try again without hunting for a charger. When a tester finds a bug, they can record the screen, copy the log and attach both to a report from the same machine.

It also opens testing to people who are not developers. Product managers, support staff, designers and content reviewers can open a build, follow a script and report what they see with a full-size screen, a keyboard and a mouse. That alone shortens the feedback loop on most teams.

The tradeoff is realism, which this guide returns to later. For now, the point is that an emulator is the fastest way to find the large, obvious, repeatable problems, which is exactly what you want to clear out before involving real devices.

There are two broad families of tools, and they suit different work.

The first is the official Android Emulator that comes with Android Studio. It simulates hardware in detail, supports a wide range of device profiles and connects directly to debugging and profiling tools. It is the right choice when you are tracing a crash, profiling memory or testing against a specific system image. It is also heavier to install and configure.

The second family is general-purpose emulators built for speed and convenience. A good android emulator such as LDPlayer installs quickly, runs on ordinary hardware, can open several instances side by side, and makes it easy to drop in an APK, map keyboard controls and record the screen. Other examples include BlueStacks and Genymotion, the latter aimed at developers and test automation. Many of these tools are designed first for gaming or for general app use, so check whether they expose the Android versions, system images and debugging features your testing needs.

Many teams use both: the official tools for debugging, and a lighter emulator for broad functional testing. When you compare options, ask a few practical questions:

Preparing Windows Before You Install Anything

Ten minutes of preparation prevents most early frustration.

Turn on hardware virtualization. Emulators run dramatically faster when virtualization is enabled in your BIOS or UEFI. The setting is usually called Intel Virtualization Technology (VT-x) or SVM Mode on AMD systems. If your emulator feels slow for no clear reason, check this first.

Enable the Windows Hypervisor Platform. The Android documentation recommends the Windows Hypervisor Platform (WHPX) for Windows acceleration, and notes that you can turn it on under “Turn Windows features on or off”, then restart. Its older Android Emulator hypervisor driver is being phased out, so check the current emulator acceleration guide for your setup. Also note that a hardware-accelerated emulator cannot run inside another virtual machine.

Verify it. The emulator includes a check that reports whether acceleration is usable:

Update your graphics drivers. Android interfaces rely on graphics acceleration. Outdated drivers cause black screens, flickering and random crashes that testers wrongly blame on the app.

Check memory and storage. As a rule of thumb, 8 GB of RAM is workable for a single virtual device and 16 GB is more comfortable for several. Leave several gigabytes of free disk space, because every virtual device stores its own system image and data.

Close heavy background programs. Browsers with dozens of tabs and other virtual machines compete for the same resources and make performance results misleading.

The default configuration is rarely the one your audience uses. Spend a few minutes matching it to reality.

Pick a screen size and density similar to a popular mid-range phone rather than a very large display, because layout problems tend to appear on smaller screens first. Assign a sensible number of CPU cores and memory, enough to be smooth but not so much that you hide performance problems your users on modest phones will feel.

Choose the Android version carefully. If your app supports several versions, test at least the oldest and the newest. Behavior changes significantly between releases, especially around permissions, notifications, background activity and gesture navigation. For example, Android 13 (API level 33) introduced a runtime notification permission, so a flow that works on an older version can silently differ on a newer one.

With the Android Emulator, you can list and start saved devices from the command line, which helps when you keep several profiles:

Finally, confirm the network works by loading a web page in the virtual browser. A surprising number of reported app bugs turn out to be a virtual device with no connection.

An APK is the package file Android uses to install an app. Installing one on a virtual device is easy, but doing it carefully makes your results more reliable.

Use trusted files only. Take builds from your own team, your continuous integration system or the developer’s official release page. An APK from a random download site can be modified or carry malware, and testing it proves nothing about the real product.

Install it. Most emulators accept a dragged APK file or have an install option. If you use the command line, Android Debug Bridge (ADB) installs a package with a single command and is the better route when you want to automate repeated installs. These are the commands you will use most:

adb devices                          # confirm the emulator is connected

adb install -r app-debug.apk         # install, replacing an existing copy

adb shell pm clear com.example.app   # wipe app data for a fresh start

adb shell am start -W -n com.example.app/.MainActivity   # launch and print timing

adb logcat -d > session.log          # save the log for a bug report

adb exec-out screencap -p > shot.png # take a screenshot

Know the common install failures. If the install fails, the message usually explains why. A package built for a newer Android version than your device runs is rejected, a build compiled only for a different processor architecture may not install or may crash on launch, and an update signed with a different key than the installed version is refused, in which case you uninstall the old version first. The helper below turns raw ADB output into a one-line explanation and also reads the launch time that am start -W prints:

"""Small helpers for reading adb output during APK testing."""

"INSTALL_FAILED_OLDER_SDK": "The app needs a newer Android version than this device runs.",

"INSTALL_FAILED_NO_MATCHING_ABIS": "The app has no native code for this device's CPU architecture.",

"INSTALL_FAILED_UPDATE_INCOMPATIBLE": "Signed with a different key than the installed copy. Uninstall first.",

"INSTALL_FAILED_VERSION_DOWNGRADE": "The installed copy has a higher version code. Uninstall first.",

"INSTALL_FAILED_INSUFFICIENT_STORAGE": "The virtual device is out of storage. Wipe data or enlarge the disk.",

def explain_install_failure(output: str) -> str:

"""Turn raw adb install output into a one-line explanation."""

m = re.search(r"(INSTALL_[A-Z_]+)", output)

return "Unknown failure. Read the full adb message."

return f"{code}: " + INSTALL_HINTS.get(code, "No hint recorded for this code.")

"""Read TotalTime from adb shell am start -W output, or None if the launch failed."""

m = re.search(r"TotalTime:\s*(\d+)", output)

The tests feed it realistic ADB output, including a successful install, three common failures, an unknown code, a successful launch and a failed one:

from adbtools import explain_install_failure, launch_time_ms

assert explain_install_failure("Performing Streamed Install\nSuccess") == "Installed."

older = "adb: failed to install app.apk: Failure [INSTALL_FAILED_OLDER_SDK]"

assert "newer Android" in explain_install_failure(older)

abi = "Failure [INSTALL_FAILED_NO_MATCHING_ABIS: Failed to extract native libraries, res=-113]"

assert "architecture" in explain_install_failure(abi)

sig = "Failure [INSTALL_FAILED_UPDATE_INCOMPATIBLE: Package com.example signatures do not match]"

assert "Uninstall first" in explain_install_failure(sig)

assert explain_install_failure("Failure [INSTALL_FAILED_SOMETHING_NEW]").endswith("No hint recorded for this code.")

assert explain_install_failure("error: device offline").startswith("Unknown failure")

good = "Starting: Intent { cmp=com.example/.MainActivity }\nStatus: ok\nActivity: com.example/.MainActivity\nTotalTime: 523\nWaitTime: 530\nComplete"

assert launch_time_ms("Error type 3\nError: Activity class does not exist.") is None

Understand split packages. Many modern apps ship as bundles made of several files instead of one APK. If you are given a bundle format, you may need a tool that installs all the parts together, or a universal APK generated for testing.

Test the right build. Debug builds and release builds do not always behave the same. Release builds often apply code shrinking and optimization that can break features that worked in debug. Before sign-off, run your main flows on a release build, not only the one developers use daily.

Wandering through an app and tapping things will find some problems. A structured pass finds far more. Work through these areas on each build.

Capture evidence as you go. A short screen recording with the exact steps, plus the saved log, is worth far more to a developer than a paragraph of description.

A very common mistake is testing everything on one virtual device and assuming the result applies everywhere. Android is a fragmented platform, and your audience is spread across many versions, screen sizes and memory limits.

Create a small set of profiles and rotate through them. A low-memory profile reveals how the app behaves on budget hardware. A small screen exposes cramped layouts. An older Android version shows compatibility gaps. A different language and region setting reveals text-length problems, date and number formatting issues and right-to-left layout errors.

Because tools such as the Android Emulator and several third-party emulators let you keep multiple saved devices, you can keep these profiles ready and compare behavior across them. This is where testing on Windows becomes efficient, turning a long list of devices you would have to buy into a handful of saved configurations.

Being honest about the limits protects you from false confidence.

● Hardware features. Camera quality, fingerprint sensors, Bluetooth accessories, NFC, precise GPS behavior and motion sensors are hard to reproduce faithfully. You can simulate basic values, but real hardware behaves differently.

● Real performance. Speed on an emulator depends on your PC. A strong computer can make an app feel faster than it will on a typical phone, so never use emulator results as proof of smooth performance for users.

● Manufacturer customizations. Phone makers modify Android with their own battery management, notification handling and interface layers. An app can work on a clean system and fail on one brand’s modified version, particularly around background tasks and push notifications.

● Real networks. A home network is stable. Mobile networks have weak signal, handoffs between towers and restrictions that an emulator will not recreate.

● Apps that block virtual devices. Some apps, particularly in banking, security and competitive gaming, detect virtual environments and refuse to run. That is a deliberate choice by the developer, and testers should respect it instead of attempting to bypass it.

The sensible approach is to let emulators handle the broad middle of your testing, then confirm anything involving hardware, payments or notifications on a few real devices before release. The script above also only interprets a handful of ADB messages, so it is a convenience for triage, not a replacement for reading the full output.

If you want something simple to adopt, use this sequence on every build.

Start with a clean virtual device matching a common phone.

Install the new build and run a quick smoke test, a fast pass over the main features to confirm nothing is fundamentally broken.

Test the specific areas that changed in this version in more depth.

Repeat the most important flows on a second profile with a different Android version and screen size.

Log every problem with exact steps, the build number, the device profile and a screenshot or recording.

Before release, verify camera, location, notifications and payments on at least one physical phone.

Write this down as a short checklist and share it. Consistent steps make results comparable between testers and between builds, which is how you notice regressions instead of rediscovering the same bugs.

Do I need Android Studio to test an APK on Windows? Not necessarily. Many emulators accept an APK directly, and ADB can be installed on its own as part of the Android platform tools. Android Studio becomes useful when you need to debug or profile.

Why is my emulator slow? The usual causes are virtualization or the Windows Hypervisor Platform being off, outdated graphics drivers, too little memory and heavy background programs. Run the acceleration check first.

Can an emulator replace a real test phone? No. It covers the broad middle of testing, but hardware, true performance, manufacturer behavior and real networks need a few physical devices.

Is it safe to install APKs from the internet? Only from trusted sources, such as your own team, your build system or an official release page. Files from random sites can be modified or carry malware.

Testing Android apps on Windows is practical, affordable and flexible when you treat the emulator as a tool with a clear purpose. It gives you fast installs, repeatable conditions and easy access to many device profiles, which makes it well suited to functional checks, layout reviews and APK testing. It does not replace real hardware for sensors, true performance and manufacturer-specific behavior, so keep a few physical devices for the final confirmation.

Prepare your PC, choose a tool that fits your workflow, configure devices that resemble real users, follow a consistent checklist and vary your environments. Do that consistently and you will catch most problems long before they reach your users.

As you can read about in our review of the larger Creative Pebble Pro speakers, this range offers plenty of headroom, providing clean sound at higher volumes despite the tiny woofer size (just two inches). Like many Bluetooth speakers, the Pebble V2 set features passive radiators, capturing low-end frequencies to give the impression of a subwoofer. Of course, it's not the same as a subwoofer, but passive bass chambers help a lot with these smaller speakers.

You connect the speakers over a 3.5mm cable for audio, and power them with USB-C (the speakers call for a 10W port). Sustained RMS power is just 8W for the speakers, but they can peak a bit higher at 16W. They run lower by default, but a high-gain switch on the back will kick the power amp into full gear if you need the speakers louder.

The G2000 speakers from Edifier come with a peak power raing of 32W and a frequency response of 98 Hz up to 22 kHz, along with analog and Bluetooth connections.

The Edifer G2000 speakers double the power output of the Pebble V2's, and they feature larger 2.75-inch drivers (though no tweeter). At $80 on sale, they're much more expensive, but Edifier justifies the extra price with USB and Bluetooth output, alongside a standard 3.5mm connection, as well as a dedicated power adapter to avoid unwanted noise over USB power. If you want to expand your setup, there's also a subwoofer output.

RMS power here is 16W, giving you significantly more headroom at higher volumes to avoid any distortion. The 2.75-inch full range drivers offers decent low-end, though the frequency response cuts off just below 100 Hz, leaving precious sub-region frequencies out of the sonic picture. That gives you a good cutoff point to add a subwoofer, however.

The Edifier M60 speakers feature a 3-inch woofer and 1-inch tweeter, as well as 66W of total output across the four drivers.

Edifer's M60 speakers are larger, and they take some obvious notes from Audioengine. They're not quite the same as the A2+ speakers, however. The M60s feature a 3-inch woofer and 1-inch tweeter, with an average RMS of 66W across all four drivers (18W each for the woofers, and 15W for the tweeters).

The speakers come with a Hi-Res audio certification, extending the frequency response up from the typical 20 kHz to 40 kHz. Although the human ear typically can't hear about 20 kHz, extra harmonic information down in the audible range of frequencies can add richness to the sound. The speakers can output via USB-C, Bluetooth 5.3, or a 3.5mm, and they come with included stands, which is something even AudioEngine lacks.

These 6.81 x 4.68 x 5.51-inch speakers have attractive removable cloth fronts, 10-degree angled stands to aim them up at your ears, and they performed well in our testing. They also have ample connectivity, with Bluetooth 5.3 LE, USB-C, RCA (with a built-in Phono stage for vinyl record players), optical, and a subwoofer connector.

For slightly more than the M60s, we have a pair of the GX-10DB speakers from the Japanese brand Onkyo. They come with 3-inch woofers, as well, though slightly smaller 0.75-inch tweets, as well as a Class D amp that can drive RMS of 30W through the speakers. Onkyo gives you a ton of ways to connect to the speakers, including USB-C, optical, Bluetooth, and RCA. The RCA inputs feature a phono stage for use with a turntable, as well.

Although these are relatively inexpensive speakers, Onkyo still makes them feel premium with removable speaker grilles and built-in, wood-finished stands that angle the speakers up toward your ears.

This desktop speaker includes dual four-inch drivers, as well as an integrated EQ and wireless remote.

If bookshelf speakers aren't your speed, you can save 44% on the Edifier D12 all-in-one desktop speaker. It's a 70-watt speaker with Bluetooth, RCA, and 3.5mm inputs with an attractive wood-grain finish and sleek removeable front grille that sports the Edifier logo.

The speaker is equipped with dual 4-inch drivers along with two 19mm silk down tweeters. Two bass radiator ports provide low-end support. At the top of the speaker, you'll find bass, middle, and treble controls, and the speaker includes a remote so you can control it wirelessly.

If you're looking for maximum power, you get 120W of goodness with Edifier's S1000MKII speakers, which are on sale for $100 off right now. These are large speakers with 5.5-inch aluminum alloy drivers and 1-inch titanium dome tweeters.

It features Bluetooth, optical, coaxial, and RCA inputs, and there's a universal bass and treble control. These are large bookshelf speakers meant for listening to music, so make sure you have a large-enough desk to fit them byside your monitor.

#10214. Android Auto update improves support for wide displays, but not everyone will see it [Gallery]

Google has rolled out a new update for Android Auto that improves support for wider display formats, but there’s a good chance you won’t be able to see it.

Android Auto has to scale to a lot of different display shapes and sizes, and its functionality varies depending on what you’re using as the platform adapts to what your display can handle. This can sometimes mean you are locked out of a specific feature or need to access it another way. A couple of infamous examples of this include the “Report” option in Google Maps being hidden on smaller displays, as well as the weather card only being on larger, taller displays.

That said, Google is now making an effort to improve how apps on Android Auto adapters to wider formats.

In the latest app update (v18.2), Android Auto is tweaking apps to switch from a top-bar layout to a sidebar for navigation. So, in Spotify, this means the “Home,” “Recents,” “Browse,” and “Library” shortcuts move to a sidebar rather than being up top.

It’s a good use of space, as it allows more content to be visible on wide but short displays. Android Authority spotted the change across Spotify, YouTube Music, and Phone, implying this is probably a platform-level change rather than being tied to individual app updates.

It’s a great change, but one you might not be able to see.

As mentioned, Android Auto adjusts to take advantage of whatever display space it has available, and that often includes putting Google Maps off to the side of other apps. While you can easily set Maps to take up the full display, other apps are less likely to be able to do that, with Maps semi-permanently off to the opposite side. In a car with a wide display we tested for this new format, Maps prevented any other apps from going wide enough to show the new layout, so your results may vary. In many cases, this new layout may simply not be visible. In a way, though, that’s a good thing – it shows that Google is addressing even the most niche aspects of Android Auto, and that’s a win for everyone.

#10213. What to Check Before Adding a Tripo Model to a Website

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A 3D model can look convincing in a still image and still be awkward on a website. The real questions appear when someone rotates it, loads it on a typical connection, or views it on a narrow phone screen. AI generators have made the first draft of a model cheap, which shifts the work toward the part developers and designers already know: budgets, testing and fallbacks.

This article walks through a web-readiness review for an AI-generated model, using an openwork ceramic vessel generated from a single reference image with Tripo as the worked example. The checks apply equally to output from other generators. It includes a small Node.js script that audits a GLB file against a size budget, and the markup for a lightweight viewer with a static fallback. The model shown is one candidate, so it cannot prove browser speed, mobile behavior, hidden geometry or export compatibility. Those are checks to run on your own asset.

Traditional 3D work involves modeling, polygon layout, materials, scale and export. AI generation can shorten the blank-workspace stage. A beginner may discover the category through a search such as AI 3D model generator free, but an initial candidate is useful only if it survives review.

For this vessel, start with the negative space, meaning the openings between the ribs. A flattering front view can hide a blocked cavity, a thin wall, or two surfaces passing through each other. Generation moves the bottleneck from making a first shape to deciding whether that shape is usable.

Image-to-3D tools use a visual reference to create a model candidate, while text-to-3D routes start from a written prompt. Tripo offers both, and other hosted options include Meshy, Rodin by Hyper3D and 3D AI Studio, all of which list GLB among their export formats, while open-source models such as TripoSR and Stable Fast 3D let you run generation yourself. Features, pricing, licensing and output quality differ and change often, so check each vendor’s current documentation. The image route suits this example because the folds, uneven rim and openings already exist in the source.

Whichever tool you use, it can provide a candidate for inspection. It cannot decide whether the source is licensed, whether the unseen rear structure is believable, or whether the exported asset suits a particular viewer. Those judgments belong to the person publishing the page.

Give the object one job. In a portfolio, this vessel might show how its shell and openings relate. That is narrower than calling it printable, manufacturable or dimensionally accurate. Note what must remain recognizable, and provide a still image if the interactive view fails.

Use an image you own or have permission to reuse. Busy backgrounds can distract an image-based workflow, so isolate the object when possible. One photograph also leaves the back and underside unknown. Treat those areas as review risks, not as details confirmed by the source.

After generation, rotate beyond the familiar front. Check the rear, top, underside and sides. Trace each large opening. Look for merged ribs, intersecting surfaces or an unstable base. A wireframe view, when available, can expose crowded geometry. A single front-facing candidate cannot answer these hidden-angle questions, so check them on your own model.

Record the download size, polygon count and texture dimensions. Polygons are the small flat faces that form the shape, and web viewers draw them as triangles. This matters because generator defaults are often tuned for quality, not for the web. In the generation screenshot for this vessel, the viewer reports roughly 1.95 million faces and the settings show 4K texture quality. That is a fine starting point for inspection but a heavy asset for a phone on a mobile connection. Curved ribs may need many triangles, but the count should still fit your page budget.

glTF is the open format most web viewers expect, and GLB is its single-file form that can bundle geometry, textures and materials. The format does not guarantee good performance, so measure the package. The script below reads a GLB with the open-source glTF Transform library, counts triangles, lists texture sizes, and compares them with a budget. The budget values are placeholders; set your own from your target devices.

Run it with node audit.mjs model.glb after installing @gltf-transform/core. It exits with a non-zero status when the asset is over budget, so you can run it in CI. A short test builds two GLBs with known triangle counts, one small and one with 200,000 triangles, and checks that the first passes and the second fails with the right message:

When an asset fails, glTF Transform also has a command line. gltf-transform inspect model.glb reports what is inside a file, and gltf-transform optimize input.glb output.glb --compress draco --texture-compress webp applies geometry and texture compression. Compression and mesh simplification can introduce artifacts, especially around thin ribs and openings, so reopen the result and re-check the angles from Gate 3 instead of accepting a smaller file blindly. Also check that your viewer supports the compression you choose.

Load the asset in the intended viewer at desktop and phone widths. Check framing, touch rotation, keyboard access, scrolling and a slower connection. The Google-backed model-viewer web component is one low-code option, and libraries such as three.js give you more control at the cost of more code. The markup below shows the pieces that matter for a first release: a poster image that loads first, lazy loading so the heavy file waits until needed, a text alternative, and visible controls.

The alt text, a poster and touch-action="pan-y" (so a vertical swipe still scrolls the page on a phone) address the common failures. Check the current model-viewer documentation for attribute names and the recommended script include, since they can change between versions. Also consider the WCAG guidance on non-text content: provide a useful description, and offer instructions plus a static image for people who cannot operate the viewer.

A single front-facing candidate is enough to discuss the method, not to certify a finished web asset. Call it a prototype until the exported file passes the checks required by its actual destination.

The same review pattern can be adapted to an architectural ornament, exhibition object, packaging concept or game prop, but each destination adds its own geometry, scale and delivery requirements. A portfolio may accept an inferred rear surface if that limitation is clear. A sales page, printable file or measured product needs stronger references and technical validation.

The audit script counts triangles, texture sizes and file size only. It does not detect self-intersections, thin walls, flipped normals or licensing problems, and passing it does not make an asset good. The budget numbers are illustrative, and real limits depend on your audience’s devices and connections. Tool features and terms change, so verify them with the vendor before you rely on them, and treat any speed or quality claims on vendor pages as the vendor’s own.

Inspect the angle you would normally crop out. Confirm input rights, measure the download, and test touch, keyboard and a slow connection. Reread the surrounding copy, because claims of fabrication accuracy or production readiness need supporting evidence. For this vessel, “exploratory web model” is an honest label, and more demanding claims require better references and a specialist review.

Decide what visitors should learn or do before adding a 3D viewer. A modest prototype with a useful fallback can beat a dramatic asset that loads slowly or hides limitations.

Do I need to code to create a model with an AI generator? No code is required to generate an initial model candidate in Tripo or similar tools. Publishing it interactively is separate work and usually involves a viewer, hosting, responsive testing and enough HTML or JavaScript knowledge to diagnose problems.

When should I use image-to-3D instead of text-to-3D? Use image-to-3D when a rights-cleared visual should guide the form, and text-to-3D when the idea begins as words. Do not treat the two routes as interchangeable evidence.

Is a generated model ready to publish as soon as it exports? No. Inspect the shape from several angles, measure the delivery package, test the intended viewer and prepare a fallback. Higher-stakes uses may also need specialist geometry, scale, material and rights review.

How can a beginner keep web 3D manageable? Give one object one clear job. Set a file-size target, test desktop and phone widths, keep interaction simple, and retain a still image for readers who cannot load or operate the viewer.

#10211. WhatsApp officially adds scheduled messages, and the rollout is scheduled too

It’s about time. WhatsApp announced today that it is finally adding support for scheduled messages in its app.

A staple of many other messaging apps, WhatsApp is adding scheduled messages to its app. This gives users the ability to write a message but schedule sending it to another user or group chat at a later time.

Scheduled messages on WhatsApp can be sent up to 14 days after you draft them in both 1:1 chats and group chats. You can schedule through a long-press on the send button, with a menu popping up to let you pick the future time when the message will be sent. You can also see scheduled messages before they’re sent to cancel sending or edit the message.

WhatsApp notes that these messages are sent even if your phone’s battery is dead or if the device is off, and everything is still end-to-end encrypted. It works with text, photos, and videos, but apparently not with voice notes.

That said, the rollout is also scheduled out. WhatsApp says the feature will start showing up for users “over the coming weeks” globally, so you might be waiting a bit longer for this long overdue release.

#10207. Save serious money with RedPocket Mobile’s $5 Black Friday plans

You’re not going to believe this, but there’s one part of your life that’s not actually getting more expensive. If you switch to RedPocket Mobile during its Black Friday sale, you can ditch your costly carrier and get a plan that starts at $5 per month. It’s not too good to be true: RedPocket has been around for 20 years, and unlike most MVNOs, it’s independently owned and operated. That gives it the ability to offer you real network choice when you sign up for a new plan (we’ll explain more later), and keeps your costs low. Really low.

But you’re reading this on 9to5Google, so how does it impact you as an Android user? That’s a great question.

RedPocket is offering $150 rebates on Android devices like the Pixel 11, Galaxy S26, Galaxy Z Flip 7, Moto G Play, and more. (You can also get $100 rebates for iPhones if you’re into that sort of thing.)

These offers run from November 9 through the 30th at redpocket.com, so don’t wait too long to save big.

It’s OK if you haven’t heard of RedPocket Mobile, because it has been quietly working for 20 years to improve the way people use their phones. Founded in 2006, the company works with all three major US carriers — AT&T, T-Mobile, and Verizon — and because it doesn’t own any of its towers, it can pass along significant savings to its customers. Here’s what the company says on its website:

RedPocket was built for price-conscious consumers who want real value and freedom from rigid, high-cost contracts. As a no-contract carrier, we’ve stayed focused on making reliable, transparent, and flexible phone service for everyone. Our plans are designed to fit real lifestyles, whether you’re a heavy data user, a frequent traveler, or just looking to cut your monthly bill.

Most MVNOs are owned by the Big Three, which means you don’t really have a choice of network when you sign up. But RedPocket works with all three major carriers and lets you choose which one works best for your location and usage patterns.

RedPocket offers a tool called CoverageGenius that helps you decide which network is right for you. While you can’t switch between networks once your line is set up, it’s a great way to figure out where to start.

I teased that $5/month cost at the beginning of the article, but here’s the real breakdown: you can get a full year of service for $60 if you’re a light data user. RedPocket’s Annual Essentials and Annual Plus plans get you unlimited calling and texting in the US, along with calling and data roaming in dozens of countries around the world. The main difference is how much data you get: 3GB for Essentials and 20GB for Plus.

Annual Premium ups the ante to unlimited data, plus calling and data roaming in even more countries.

Across all three plans, you’re saving between 33% and 50% off the regular price when you sign up between November 9th and 30th.

One thing to note about all of these plans: the annual prices are paid upfront, not billed monthly. Promotional pricing applies to the first year, and after that, annual plans renew at $110 for Essentials, $220 for Plus, and $330 for Premium.

RedPocket understands that it may be hard to commit to a full year of service even if it’s super cheap. That’s why it offers monthly plans, too, that can serve as a trial for any of the networks. It costs just $10 for the first month to try any of the three plans, with Monthly Plus rising to $20/month after that and Premium $30/month thereafter (Essentials stays at $10/month).

If you need a new phone to go along with your new plan, RedPocket is a great place to get it.
