#10182. Catch Go bugs that fuzzing misses with Gemini and a constraint solver

I help lead Google's agent skills program, currently with 175 agent skills that supplement community collections like go-skills. To keep that many skill files consistent, I wrote skill-lint, a standalone Go CLI that checks SKILL.md files and applies LLM-generated line edits before they ship. Even with skill-lint's own unit and fuzz test suite, a small slice or line-number mistake inside its Markdown parsers or edit applier can sit dormant until an unusual file or edit range triggers a runtime panic in the linter.

In Go, every slice or index operation that can panic at runtime compiles down to an explicit bounds-check trap in machine code, after the compiler's optimizer strips out the checks it can already rule out. To hunt for crashes, I compiled key functions from my code to see which panic traps survived optimization, and Gemini 4 Argon translated the path to each trap into rules for the Z3 constraint solver. An LLM-written solver model isn't a sound proof system, but it gives the agent an independent way to reason about control flow and synthesize concrete Go reproducers, and every crash it finds is real because go test runs the offending bytes directly. That workflow caught all three bugs I planted in test parsers and turned up three latent bugs in skill-lint.

In this post, I'll walk through how the Gemini and Z3 pipeline works, why go test -fuzz missed two of those crashes (and the one-line change that fixes it), and where this approach fits in a day-to-day Go workflow.

A recent post from Anthropic showed how formal verification catches latent bugs in C and C++ by having frontier AI models write machine-checked Rocq proofs across OSS-Fuzz libraries. Instead of sampling random inputs like a fuzzer, a formal tool works backward from each failure condition to solve for the exact input that triggers it. Full theorem proving is still too heavy for most day-to-day Go projects, so I wanted to see how much of that bug-hunting power an agent can get from an LLM and a constraint solver in about a minute per function.

In C and C++, reading or writing past the end of a buffer corrupts memory. Go catches out-of-bounds access at runtime instead, compiling every slice or index operation it can't prove safe into an explicit branch to runtime.panicBounds that panics, crashing the process unless recovered.

Before generating machine code, Go's SSA prove pass already eliminates most of those checks by tracking slice lengths and loop variables. That optimization pass leaves behind only the runtime.panicBounds traps the compiler couldn't rule out on its own, giving the solver a short list of candidate crash sites to target.

I compiled each target with GOOS=linux GOARCH=riscv64 go build -gcflags="-S -d=ssa/check_bce/debug=1" (-S prints the assembly listing and ssa/check_bce/debug=1 logs every surviving bounds check) and passed both the Go source and the disassembly to the model. Looking at Go source alone, you can't see which bounds checks the compiler already removed or where a register gets truncated to 8 bits; in the disassembly, both show up. I targeted RISC-V because it's the instruction set Anthropic's post targets for its Rocq proofs, and Go cross-compiles to it out of the box.

From there, Argon read the Go source and disassembly and returned a Z3 script modeling the path to each surviving trap, along with a patched function whenever it flagged a bug. Z3 then checked whether those constraints could be satisfied, and whenever it found a crashing input, go test replayed those bytes against the real function. Using an LLM saved me from translating assembly into solver constraints by hand. Argon's translation dominated the runtime, at about a minute per function, while Z3 itself finished in milliseconds.

Anthropic's approach relies on interactive theorem proving, where experts model machine semantics and Rocq checks LLM-generated proofs against those rules. In contrast, my workflow uses a constraint solver based on satisfiability modulo theories (SMT), relying on Argon to translate assembly directly into Z3 constraints. While a constraint solver doesn't provide a complete correctness proof without a semantic model, it is highly effective for finding bugs. Z3's check-sat command returns sat when input constraints can be satisfied and unsat when they cannot.

A sat answer ("definitely buggy") comes with concrete bytes aimed at the trap, and go test runs them against the real function. Once go test panics, you have a concrete bug reproducer regardless of whether Argon's model was sound. Like a Bloom filter for bugs, an unsat answer only means "maybe correct." Trading away a correctness proof is what makes the approach so practical: you get most of the bug-finding value of formal methods for a tiny fraction of the effort.

Anthropic's setup proved no-crash properties on whole binaries under a 64 MB budget; I checked individual functions on fresh 64-byte slices where cap(buf) == len(buf), so any slice past len(buf) panics. (If you want the full-binary Rocq setup for Go, theorem-labs/oss-verify includes a Go 1.27 runtime overlay that makes the runtime single-threaded and deterministic.)

For the fuzzing baseline, I ran unseeded go test -fuzz on Go 1.27.3 against each planted bug: one 3-second run and three 60-second runs, each with a fresh fuzz cache so a short time budget wouldn't explain a miss.

I started with three safe parsers to make sure the pipeline didn't flag clean code. The compiler's prove pass removed every bounds check in a TOML key parser on its own, and Z3 returned unsat on the remaining traps in an HTTP header scanner and a varint decoder.

Then I wrote three parsers with subtle panics. Here's the first one, ParseTwoChunkFrame:

In the RISC-V disassembly, the bug is much easier to see:

total := hdrLen + bodyLen adds two uint8 values, so MOVBU wraps the sum at 256 before the length guard checks it, while endBody adds the full 64-bit ints. Argon turned those instructions into a Z3 query asking whether a fresh slice of up to 64 bytes can pass the guard and still fail the endBody check at 0x0092:

Z3 returned a 59-byte counterexample with hdrLen = 48 and bodyLen = 236: their uint8 sum wraps from 284 down to 28 (so 28 + 4 ≤ 59 passes the guard), and buf[endHdr:endBody] then slices to index 288 and panics.

At 59 bytes, 19.4% of all possible (hdrLen, bodyLen) pairs trigger this crash. Yet a 3-second fuzz run missed it, and so did three 60-second runs.

Go checks a slice expression like buf[endHdr:endBody] against the slice's capacity, not its length, and Go's fuzzer rarely passes a slice without spare capacity. In a 10-second probe of 386,741 fuzz calls, 89% of inputs were views into a shared ~100 MB buffer, and only 0.07% had cap == len. With that much spare capacity, endBody (at most 514) never exceeds cap(buf), so the buggy parser returns bytes past the end of buf instead of panicking.

The second target, ExtractQuotedToken, hit the same blind spot when a trailing backslash pushed the index one byte past len(buf). Z3 caught it immediately with the 2-byte input []byte{'"', '\\'}, while the fuzzer absorbed the overrun in spare capacity.

Clipping capacity to length with a full slice expression turns any slice past len into a panic:

After that change, the fuzzer crashed both ParseTwoChunkFrame and ExtractQuotedToken in all three runs (median 0.28 s and 0.17 s). The third target, ParseMagicPacket, was a control because its signed int8 length bug panics on a negative index regardless of capacity, and the fuzzer found it in every run either way. Fuzzing still can't give you an unsat signal across the remaining paths, which is how I checked the three safe parsers and Argon's patches.

Next, I pointed the pipeline at skill-lint itself. A whole Go CLI is too large to hand to a solver directly. Even a 10-line parser links 1,559 symbols from the Go runtime, and the 28 MB skill-lint binary has 32,321 symbols in total. Following Functional Core, Imperative Shell, I left CLI flags, network calls, and file I/O to go test -fuzz and go test -race, and ran the solver only on pure functions and slice helpers extracted from file-reading code.

Of the five functions I tested, two scanners came back unsat (including a CRLF line splitter with six surviving bounds checks), and the other three had bugs. The sharpest was in ApplyReplacementChunks, which validates LLM-generated {StartLine, EndLine} edits against the initial line count and sorts them bottom-to-top so earlier edits don't shift later line numbers:

Z3 immediately returned sat because sorting by StartLine doesn't prevent two chunks from overlapping. In the extracted helper, which checks bounds once up front, replacing lines 3 and 4 of a 4-line file with one line shrinks lines to 3 elements, so a second chunk spanning lines 2 through 4 evaluates lines[4:] and panics with slice bounds out of range [4:3]. In the shipped ApplyChunks, a per-iteration bounds check turns that case into a misleading range error, while overlapping chunks that stay within len(lines) silently overwrite the earlier edit. Rejecting overlapping chunks after the sort fixes both.

The other two bugs were similar accounting errors. In the local fixer, target line numbers were checked once per violation before the inner chunk loop applied deletions that shrank the slice (a latent panic if a fixer ever returns a second chunk after a deletion, though DeleteLineFix only returns one today). In the prompt packer, a truncation check reserved a flat 100 bytes for a ~70-byte wrapper plus file path, overshooting the 2 MB context budget in the shipped code when a path exceeds about 30 bytes and panicking in the extracted helper.

When you check a function modularly like this, the target has to include its own entry-point guards (like the bounds check and sort in ApplyReplacementChunks). If an internal helper assumes preconditions that are only enforced by its caller or stated in a doc comment, Z3 will happily invent inputs that violate them. Because each extracted helper included its entry guards, false positives weren't an issue: across all six buggy functions in my tests, the first sat assignment Z3 returned triggered a real panic in go test without needing to filter out non-crashing candidates or loop back into the solver.

Once I pulled these helpers away from file I/O, a 3-second fuzz run caught all three bugs too. To make sure Argon wasn't leaning on source comments in my test files, I also reran the skill-lint targets with all comments stripped; Argon and Z3 still caught all three bugs and returned unsat on the two scanners and all three patches.

Staying entirely inside the target language makes a whole class of bugs hard for an agent to see. When an LLM reviews Go source directly, a line like if int(total)+4 > len(buf) looks like a textbook bounds check, and when it runs go test -fuzz, target-language runtime details like spare slice capacity or multi-step precondition checks can hide the crash indefinitely. Asking the agent to translate Go and assembly into a constraint solver forces a change of representation that strips away surface syntax and makes every bit width and slice bound explicit, while go test turns any solver hit back into a concrete Go reproducer.

For most Go code, fuzzing pure helpers with slice capacity clipped to length will catch bounds bugs in seconds. A solver is worth the extra minute on the traps that survive both the compiler's range prover and a quick fuzz run, especially in parsers and editors that compute offsets from multi-field headers or line ranges.

Single-threaded solver models don't see data races or deadlocks, so concurrent code still belongs with the race detector, deterministic concurrency testing, and Go 1.27's goroutine leak profiles.

If you're experimenting with solvers or coding agents on Go, let's compare notes on X or LinkedIn.

#10179. Motorola Signature 27 hands-on: Pure elegance on display

It’s hard to believe we finally got a proper premium phone from Motorola again, and it feels a bit like the return of the Moto X.

Motorola’s slow regeneration in North America from a mostly budget device-minded company to one offering the most expensive non-folding flagship phone might become the thing of legend. That’s not to say the company hasn’t shown true greatness in the past — the Moto X, Moto Z, and Razr lines are proof of that — but it’s always shied away from pulling out all the stops until the Motorola Signature 27 arrived.

Freshly unboxed and sitting in my hands, the textile-clad Capulet Olive Motorola Signature 27 colorway has impressed me in every way, shape, and form over the past 24 hours. It’s a much longer period of time than I usually get with hands-on opportunities and, yet, the newness of the experience has yet to wear off.

Part and parcel to that experience is the design. For whatever reason, this is the year Motorola seems to have truly figured out what makes a phone nice to hold. The textile back, ribbed metal frame, rounded corners, and curved edges on the front and back of the phone are purely blissful to hold. It’s a reminder that a phone can be as good looking as it feels instead of being a flat, boring, off-balance slab like too many phones are now.

The weight also surprised me. On paper this sits right in the middle of all the Ultra/Pro Max-named phones, yet it feels lighter for some intangible reason. The camera hump makes it slightly top-heavy, and it’s almost as wobble-free as a Pixel (there’s just one off-center camera hump).

The new 120Hz LTPO tandem OLED display is as top-notch as you’re going to get. I had all of maybe 2 hours of direct sunlight yesterday to test it with, and boy does it ever get bright. The rest of the time, I’ve been either inside or out in overcast weather, all of which it looked brilliant in. And it uses a combination of DC dimming above 20% brightness, and 2160Hz PWM dimming below 20% brightness, making it super comfortable for me.

Motorola’s software has evolved again with this release, and the verdict is still out on whether or not this is actually better than the company’s Android 16 designs. The build of Android 17 that ships with this phone utilizes some major design changes we’ve only seen on a few random low-end Motorola phones, and I’m not sure I care for the gentle gradients on the quick toggle icons and some other system UI elements. It feels like a weirdly cheap effect that clashes with Material Design’s flatter motif.

Despite that, this is the most Pixelly (is that a word?) a Motorola phone has ever felt. Features like Rambler, Sign to Text, and Create My Widget are here and work impeccably well. Gemini Intelligence is also on the way, and while there’s no specific ETA for that at the moment, it’s exciting to know that the most advanced Android features — which are currently only available on a very small number of devices — are coming to this phone.

This may also be the fastest phone I’ve used to date. Everything is unbelievably snappy. Tap an icon and the thing opens instantly. Animations are zippy, and the new lock screen and customization options feel cohesive. Plus, all the favorite Moto actions and gestures are still here, which I greatly appreciate.

I’ve got some lofty expectations for this camera hardware, too, and it’s because Motorola is talking up such a big game. DXOMark’s camera review rated it the 2nd-best smartphone camera in the world, just under the Huawei Pura 80 Ultra and above the iPhone 18 Pro. Some people don’t take much stock in DXOMark, but it’s still worth noting that they’ve praised the heck out of the camera and Motorola is also pushing this as a winner in a way they’ve never done before.

The hype for the Motorola Signature 27 certainly seems to be realized, and now only time will truly tell if it can live up to the lofty expectations Motorola’s $1,599 price has set for it. If nothing, it’s certainly off to a good start and has made an excellent first impression.

#10176. Your Pose Model Gives You Coordinates, Not Meaning

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Pose detection in the browser is a solved problem. You can load a model, point it at a webcam, and get a skeleton tracking a human body in real time, on a laptop, with no server involved.

And then you have a skeleton that follows someone around the screen, which is a toy.

The gap between that toy and a feature someone would pay for is smaller than most developers expect, and it isn't made of machine learning. It's made of trigonometry you already know. This article builds a working bicep-curl rep counter to show exactly where that line falls — and more importantly, which side of it your bugs live on.

I'm using MediaPipe Pose Landmarker from Google AI Edge, which is Apache-2.0 licensed and runs entirely in the tab. Here's the whole setup:

Point that at a

Thirty-three points. Each one is a normalised coordinate — x and y run 0 to 1 across the frame, z is a relative depth estimate — plus a visibility score saying how confident the model is that it can actually see that joint.

That's the entire output. Coordinates and confidences.

The model has no concept of exercise. It doesn't know what an arm is for. It has never heard of a bicep curl. It reports where it thinks your joints are, thirty-odd times a second, and stops.

Everything that turns that into a product is yours, and it's ordinary code.

A bicep curl is a change in the angle at the elbow. The elbow angle is defined by three of those 33 points: shoulder, elbow, wrist. In MediaPipe's pose topology those are indices 11, 13 and 15 on the left side, 12, 14 and 16 on the right.

Getting the angle is two vectors and a dot product:

This is the same maths you'd use to point a game character in a direction. Nothing about it is machine learning, and that matters for a reason I'll come back to.

One thing to notice: I'm ignoring z entirely. The depth estimate is relative, not metric — it's good enough for working out which parts of a body are nearer the camera, and not good enough to measure anything. For a 2D elbow angle, using it adds noise and buys nothing.

Here's where almost everyone's first attempt breaks.

The naive version looks at the angle and counts whenever the arm is up:

Run that at 30 frames a second and one slow curl gives you about twenty reps. You haven't counted repetitions. You've counted frames in which the arm happened to be bent.

A rep isn't a state. It's a transition between states:

Fifteen lines, and that's the feature. The counter increments exactly once per cycle, on the edge, because the guard on phase makes every other frame in that cycle a no-op.

If you used a single threshold — say, 100 degrees — you'd get a counter that fires repeatedly whenever someone holds their arm near 100 degrees and the estimate jitters by a degree either side. Every tiny fluctuation across the line is a transition, and every transition is a rep.

Two widely separated thresholds give you hysteresis. Once the arm is "up," it has to travel all the way past 150 degrees before the counter will consider another rep. Noise of a few degrees can't produce a count because no amount of jitter near 55 crosses 150.

This is the same trick a thermostat uses to avoid switching the heating on and off every few seconds, and it's the single most useful pattern in this whole category of work. Any time you turn a continuous signal into discrete events, you want two thresholds, not one.

visibility is the field people skip, and skipping it is how you get phantom reps.

When a joint goes out of frame or gets occluded, the model still returns a coordinate for it. It is obliged to return 33 points. What it does is return a guess, with a low visibility score attached — and a guessed wrist position can swing wildly between frames, which looks exactly like a very fast curl.

Returning null and having update bail out means an occluded frame holds the current state rather than corrupting it. The arm stays "up" until the model can actually see it come down.

If you want to be stricter, smooth the angle over the last three or four frames with a rolling median before it reaches the state machine. A median rejects single-frame outliers in a way a mean doesn't.

Once you've written one of these, you start recognising the shape everywhere:

None of those require a different model. They're the same 33 coordinates with different geometry sitting on top, and the geometry is a few dozen lines each.

Here's why I keep insisting on the distinction between coordinates and meaning.

When my rep counter miscounts, I don't retrain anything. I don't collect a dataset, I don't label examples, I don't spin up a training job. I look at two things: my thresholds, and my state machine. One of them is wrong.

That's a normal bug in normal code, and you already know how to find it. You can log the angle. You can set a breakpoint on the transition. You can write a unit test that feeds a synthetic sequence of angles through update and asserts the count — no camera, no model, no fixtures:

The hard part of this problem — turning pixels into joint positions — was solved by a research team and shipped to you as a file you can download. The part that's left is the part you're already good at.

You are not blocked on machine learning knowledge. You were blocked on nobody telling you this was available.

I set delegate: 'GPU' above without comment, and it deserves one. MediaPipe Tasks lets you choose between a CPU path and a GPU path, and availability of the GPU path varies by browser, platform and graphics driver.

The practical consequence is that you have to test the CPU path, because for some meaningful share of your users it isn't a fallback — it's the only path they will ever take. If you develop on a machine where the GPU delegate works and never exercise the CPU one, you have shipped something you've never run.

That, and the question of how often you should run the model at all, is a bigger subject than this article. It's the next one.

Jay Nirmal is a Senior Software Engineer at Meazure Learning with over eight years of experience in distributed systems, cloud infrastructure, WebRTC, and production reliability. He leads engineering initiatives supporting large-scale digital assessment platforms, focusing on high-availability systems, real-time media infrastructure, recording reliability, and assessment integrity. Jay holds a Master of Science in Information Systems from Northeastern University and a Bachelor of Engineering in Computer Engineering from the University of Mumbai.

#10172. Unlocking Stable On-Policy RL on TPU with Bitwise-Zero Train–Inference Mismatch

Reinforcement learning (RL) for large language models is notoriously sensitive to numerical instability: even tiny discrepancies between rollout generation and gradient updates can destabilize training or stall convergence. In LLM RL, the model serves as the policy, π(action | previous tokens), and true on-policy learning requires every action in an update to be generated and evaluated by that exact same policy. While a synchronous loop ensures each rollout batch is scored using the same weights that generated it, a critical numerical requirement remains: the sampler and trainer must evaluate that policy identically.

In practice, production RL stacks run sampling and training through separate, specialized execution paths. The sampler chooses each action token during incremental decoding and records its behavior logprob. The trainer evaluates the same token in batched forward-backward passes to compute its current logprob and gradients. Under the same weights, these differing execution paths can still assign different logprobs to the same token and context. The training schedule is on-policy, but the numerical execution has introduced a hidden off-policy gap that compounds over multi-turn trajectories.

Our zero train–inference mismatch (Zero-TIM) implementation, developed in Tunix and verified at multi-host scale across 64 TPU v5p chips (DP8×TP8), closes this numerical gap. At the same weights, the sampler and trainer assign bitwise-identical logprobs to the same action and token history—bringing state-of-the-art, true on-policy stability to TPU RL training.

A reduction is a tree of floating-point additions. Floating-point addition is not associative, so changing the tree changes where rounding occurs. The effect is easier to expose in bfloat16, which retains less precision than float32.

The following bfloat16 example isolates the effect of reduction order:

The same issue appears in model reductions: matrix products accumulate partial sums, normalization computes statistics, and tensor-parallel shards combine partial outputs. In our original stack, sampler decode and trainer forward used different token-array shapes and forward implementations. JAX specializes computations to their input shapes, and the training forward also runs inside a differentiated program.

Across these paths, reduction order and intermediate precision were not consistently aligned. Floating-point rounding turned those arithmetic differences into different logprob bits. The weights and model equations were identical; the arithmetic was not.

The contract is simple: across sampler decode, serving prefill, and trainer forward, all three paths must produce bitwise-identical logprobs for every sampled action—comparing raw floating-point bytes under the same rollout weights, token history, and sampling settings, with zero rounding or tolerance.

Why three paths instead of two? In a decoupled RL stack, a direct sampler–trainer disagreement shows that a numerical gap exists, but not where it enters. Introducing serving prefill as a middle checkpoint turns alignment into a systematic two-step diagnostic that any RL stack can adopt:

Sampler decode vs. prefill verifies that the serving engine agrees with itself between incremental token generation and full-sequence re-scoring, while prefill vs. trainer verifies that the serving and training engines agree across compilation and differentiation boundaries.

Achieving Zero-TIM does not require merging the sampler and trainer into a single monolithic engine. Instead, we use a generalizable four-part recipe that bridges separate serving and training pipelines across multi-host TPU meshes: locking within-shard kernel accumulation, ordering cross-shard reductions, standardizing the logprob scoring tail, and wrapping serving kernels with custom differentiation rules.

Inside each tensor-parallel rank, batch-invariant Pallas kernels control accumulation order and intermediate precision. Projection kernels traverse fixed contraction blocks in order, keeping the reduction blocking independent of token-row count. Partial products accumulate in float32, with explicit output casts.

The production kernel initializes its scratch accumulator before the tile traversal, then applies this core to each tile:

The next tile continues from the float32 accumulator, not the cast output. This construction covers the attention and MLP projections and the LM head. Dedicated kernels fix RMSNorm reductions and SwiGLU’s intermediate casts. The existing Pallas paged-attention forward is retained.

For row-parallel projections, each tensor-parallel rank produces a partial output. We separate communication from addition: ppermute transports the partials, then every rank adds them in the same source-rank order.

The reduction order is explicit in the program rather than selected by a collective implementation.

Alignment must extend beyond model logits to the probability of the sampled action. Decode, prefill, and trainer apply matching sampling transforms, use the same fixed Pallas log-softmax construction, and select the recorded action token.

Prefill and trainer score the action generated during rollout rather than choosing another action. We also preserve sampled token IDs across environment turns so that every path evaluates the same token history.

The trainer reuses the serving prefill computation. Custom vector–Jacobian products retain the serving kernels for forward evaluation and provide their backward rules. The matmul example shows this separation:

The differentiable replica mirrors the kernel’s accumulation and casts, but is used only to construct the pullback. Forward values still come from the serving Pallas kernel.

Training places that forward inside a differentiated JAX program. We pass --xla_allow_excess_precision=false through XLA_FLAGS to the compiler process, constraining excess intermediate precision in the operations surrounding the Pallas kernels.

Long trajectories are evaluated in fixed-size chunks, carrying the paged KV cache forward. All action logprobs feed the complete train-batch RL loss. The reverse pass propagates gradients through those cache connections and combines parameter-gradient contributions before the optimizer update.

Across the AI systems community, eliminating train–inference mismatch has rapidly become a state-of-the-art requirement for stabilizing large-scale RL convergence. Recent open-source efforts—including vLLM with TorchTitan and RadixArk/LMSys Miles—have demonstrated how batch-invariant kernels and bitwise numerical alignment prevent off-policy drift during policy optimization.

Our work delivers an open-source, modular JAX/XLA and Pallas implementation of this contract for TPU in Tunix. Verified across a multi-host 64 TPU v5p (DP8×TP8) mesh, these kernel and collective patterns provide a reusable blueprint that teams can adopt to achieve bit-exact on-policy RL across distributed JAX/TPU stacks.

To benchmark our TPU kernels, we run a 200-step numerical verification test on the synthetic FrozenLake environment using a public Qwen3-8B checkpoint as a baseline test case using 64 TPU v5p chips with a DP8×TP8 mesh. The recipe uses a GSPO-token loss with leave-one-out advantages. We compare Standard training with trainer-old logprobs and no additional sampler correction, token-level truncated importance sampling (TIS), and Zero-TIM.

The Zero-TIM configuration also uses token-in/token-out (TiTO) handling to preserve sampled token IDs across environment turns. The comparison runs retain their existing tokenization paths.

Zero-TIM records zero mean absolute sampler–trainer logprob difference at every logged step in this window, while its training solve rate increases. Standard and TIS retain nonzero differences: TIS adjusts their contribution to the update without removing the underlying mismatch. Zero-TIM reaches higher observed solve rates in these runs.

We developed Zero-TIM prototype as part of Tunix, Google's open-source, JAX-native library for LLM RL and post-training on Cloud TPU. The kernel patterns and three-way verification contract are modular by design—making them straightforward to adopt across existing multi-host JAX/TPU serving and training pipelines—and we are actively extending support to linear-attention and Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures.

#10167. How to Build Conference Talk Slides With AI Without Looking Generic

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Conference talks fail on slides more often than on content. The speaker knows the material and has something worth saying, but the deck is a wall of bullets, or a template everyone in the room has seen before, or forty slides for a twenty-minute slot. AI presentation makers can now produce a complete deck from an outline in minutes, which solves the time problem and creates a new one: the output tends toward exactly the uniform, stock-image, bullet-heavy style that audiences have learned to tune out.

This article explains how conference slides differ from other decks, why AI-generated decks look generic and what specifically causes it, a workflow that uses an AI presentation maker for speed while keeping the talk distinctive, how to handle the slide types that matter most in a talk (the opening, the demonstration, the data, the close), and a checklist to run before the talk.

A conference talk is spoken, timed and performed to a room. That changes the rules compared with a reading deck or a business presentation.

The causes are specific and therefore fixable.

Template uniformity. Every slide uses the same layout family, so the deck has no visual rhythm. Audiences read the sameness as mass-produced.

Bullet default. Given a paragraph, the tool produces three to five bullets. Given twenty paragraphs, twenty bullet slides.

Stock imagery. Icons and photos chosen by keyword match the topic superficially and add no meaning. A talk about database indexing gets a picture of a filing cabinet.

Label titles. “Introduction”, “Background”, “Key Challenges”, “Conclusion”. Titles that name the category rather than make the point.

Flattened emphasis. The tool does not know which of your ideas is the one the audience should leave with, so every slide gets equal weight.

No pacing. Every slide carries the same amount of content, so there is no build, no pause, no payoff.

Each of these is a default that can be overridden, either in how the input is prepared or in a short editing pass afterwards.

Step 1: Write the talk before the slides. Not a script necessarily, but the argument: what the audience knows at the start, what they know at the end, and the four to six moves that get them there. Write the one sentence you want them to repeat to a colleague afterwards. If this is not clear, no deck will fix it.

Step 2: Write the slide titles as the things you will say. Each title is a sentence, in your voice, that states the point of that moment in the talk. “Indexes make reads fast by making writes slower” is a title. “Index trade-offs” is not. Read the titles in order; they should be the talk in miniature.

Step 3: Mark the pacing. Next to each title, note whether the slide is a hold (a key point, stays up while you talk for a minute or more), a beat (a quick slide in a sequence), or a reveal (something shown, a demo, a result). This tells you where to put effort.

Step 4: Generate. Paste the titles with one or two sentences of content under each into an ai presentation maker. Ask for a slide count close to your title count. Pick the plainest theme available; distinctiveness will come from your content, not the template.

Step 5: Strip the bullets. On every hold slide, delete the bullets and leave the title, or the title plus one image or one number. On beat slides, keep at most one short line. The content that was in the bullets goes into speaker notes, which is where it belonged.

Step 6: Replace every stock image. Delete icons. Replace keyword-matched photos with your own material: a screenshot, a diagram you drew, a chart from your data, a photograph you took, a single large word. If a slide has no genuine visual, a title alone on a plain background is better than a filler image.

Step 7: Build the reveals by hand. Demos, before-and-after comparisons, the key chart. These are the slides the audience will remember, and they need more care than a generator can give. See the section below.

Step 8: Break the layout rhythm deliberately. Make the two or three most important slides look different: full-bleed image, a single enormous number, a black slide with one line of white text. Contrast is what makes a moment land.

Step 9: Rehearse with a timer. The slide count will be wrong in the first rehearsal. Cut, merge or split until the pacing matches the time.

The opening. Not a title slide with your name and a logo while you say hello. Open on something that earns attention: a surprising number, a question, a screenshot of the problem, a single provocative sentence. Your name and affiliation can come on slide two, or be said rather than shown.

The problem statement. One slide, one sentence, stays up while you explain why the audience should care. No bullets.

The demonstration. If the talk has a demo, record it. Live demos fail in rooms with unreliable connections and unfamiliar projectors. A recorded demo with the audio off, narrated live, gives the same effect with none of the risk. If you must go live, have the recording ready as a fallback.

The data. One chart per slide, one message per chart, the message in the title. Strip gridlines, legends and secondary series. Highlight the one line or bar that matters in colour and grey out the rest. Label directly. Type large enough to read from the back.

The “so what.” After the evidence, a slide that states what it means in one sentence. This is a hold slide; give it time.

The close. The one sentence you want them to repeat, alone on the final slide. Not “Thank you” or “Questions?”; those can be said. The last thing on screen while you take questions should be your point.

Because the slides are now sparse, two things need handling.

First, the speaker notes must carry what you intend to say, in cue form: the key phrases, the number you must not forget, the transition to the next slide. The generator will have put the stripped bullet content there; edit it into cues rather than prose.

Second, the shared deck. Attendees who download a sparse deck afterwards get little from it. Options: export with notes visible; publish a companion blog post or document; or build a second “reading” version with more text on each slide. Many speakers do the first and link to a post.

For a twenty-minute talk, fifteen to twenty-five slides is typical once hold and beat slides are counted. For a forty-five-minute talk, thirty to fifty. Build-up sequences of several quick beats inflate the count without inflating the time, which is fine. What is not fine is a deck of forty dense slides for twenty minutes; that is a talk that will run over and leave the audience behind.

Rehearse at least twice with a timer, once alone and once with a listener. Cut the content you rush. Nobody has ever complained that a talk finished two minutes early.

Lightning talks (five to ten minutes). Five to eight slides, almost all holds. One idea, one piece of evidence, one takeaway. Cut everything else.

Workshops and tutorials. More slides, more text, because attendees will refer back and follow along. The reading-deck rules apply more here; still, keep each step to one slide.

Panel contributions. Often no slides at all, or two or three for a specific point. Resist bringing a full deck.

Keynotes. Fewer slides, longer holds, more full-bleed images and single sentences. The speaker carries almost everything; the slides punctuate.

Remote talks. The screen is small and the audience is distracted. Larger type still, more frequent slide changes to hold attention, and a recorded demo is essential because screen-sharing a live one adds another failure point.

How much of the deck should the AI tool produce? The structural first draft: layouts, consistency, speaker notes populated. Expect to edit every slide and rebuild the key ones.

Is it acceptable to have slides with only a title? Yes, and it is often the strongest format for a spoken talk. The audience listens instead of reading.

What about code on slides? Short excerpts only, in a large monospace type, with the relevant lines highlighted and the rest dimmed. Anything longer belongs in the companion post.

Should I use animations? Simple builds (revealing elements in sequence) help pacing. Transitions and effects distract. Keep to the former.

How do I make the deck distinctive if I cannot design? Your material is what makes it distinctive: your screenshots, your data, your diagrams, your sentences. Design restraint plus real content beats decorated templates every time.

#10150. Why the Agent Isn’t the Hard Part: Where Developers Go Wrong With Agentic Workflows

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You can build an AI agent in an afternoon now. Pick a model, write a loop, hand it a few tools and watch it book a meeting or open a pull request. The demo works, and everyone in the room is impressed.

Then it meets a real system with real permissions and real customers, and things get quiet. Gartner has predicted that over 40% of agentic AI projects will be canceled by the end of 2027, citing rising costs, unclear business value and inadequate risk controls. Its analyst Anushree Verma described most current projects as early-stage experiments that are driven by hype and often misapplied. A forecast is not a measurement, but the pattern it describes matches what many teams report: the agent is rarely what breaks. The workflow around it is.

This article covers where that workflow tends to fail, with a focus on permissions, tool connections and human approval. It includes a small, runnable policy gate in JavaScript, compares the main safeguards and notes their limits. Standards and guidance change quickly in this area, so check the linked sources for the current versions.

A year or two ago the model was often the differentiator. Today many teams can reach the same frontier models through the same kinds of APIs, so if the intelligence is similar, the advantage has to come from somewhere else.

Anthropic’s guide to building effective agents reached a similar conclusion after working with teams building agents. It reports that the most successful implementations were not using complex frameworks or specialized libraries, and were building with simple, composable patterns instead. It also separates workflows, where code paths are predefined, from agents, where the model chooses its own steps, and advises using the simplest approach that works and adding complexity only when measurement shows it helps.

That should change where developers spend their time. Frameworks matter, but three less glamorous questions matter more: what can the agent touch, when does it stop, and who checks its work?

The OWASP guidance on Excessive Agency names what goes wrong here. It covers agents that can do more than they need to, and it traces the problem to three root causes: excessive functionality, excessive permissions and excessive autonomy.

In practice this looks familiar. An agent that only needs to read tickets is given a token that can also close them. A summarization tool connects with an admin account because that was quicker to set up. A generic “run shell command” tool is exposed when the agent only ever needs to write one file.

The fix is ordinary engineering discipline applied to a new kind of caller. OWASP’s recommendations include giving each tool only the functions and permissions it needs, preferring narrow tools over open-ended ones, running actions under the user’s own scoped identity, requiring human approval for high-impact actions and enforcing authorization in the downstream system rather than letting the model decide. The last point is the important one: a model can be talked out of a rule written in a prompt, but an API that checks scopes cannot.

Make the risky properties of a tool explicit in its definition so your orchestration layer can act on them:

Nothing clever is happening there, which is the point. The decision about what needs a human is made by a developer at design time, not by a model at runtime.

The definitions above only help if something enforces them. The sketch below is a policy gate that sits between the model and your tools. It checks that the tool exists, that the caller was granted the scope, and whether the action is reversible, and it records each decision. It runs on Node.js 18 or later with no dependencies.

A short test file shows the behavior. Unknown tools and missing scopes are denied, reads run, and the irreversible send waits for a person:

This is deliberately minimal. A real system would load scopes from the user’s actual session, enforce the same scopes again in the downstream API, and store the audit log somewhere durable. The gate is a second line of defense, not a replacement for authorization in the systems the agent calls.

The Model Context Protocol has become a common way to connect agents to tools. Anthropic donated MCP to the Agentic AI Foundation, a directed fund under the Linux Foundation, in December 2025. Block contributed goose and OpenAI contributed AGENTS.md as founding projects, which shows how central the ecosystem has become.

A standard protocol is good news. It also means you can install a tool server in minutes and expose every endpoint of your issue tracker, repository or CRM to your agent, whether it needs them or not. Easy connection quietly pushes more power to the agent.

The protocol’s own security best practices document covers several relevant risks, including confused deputy problems in proxy servers, token passthrough, local server compromise and scope minimization. For scopes, it recommends starting with a minimal set and elevating only when a privileged operation is first attempted, and it lists wildcard or omnibus scopes as a common mistake.

A sensible rule is to treat every tool server like a third-party dependency running with production credentials. Review the tools it exposes, switch off the ones the workflow does not use, pin versions and log every call. If you would not let a new contractor do it on day one, the agent should not do it either.

Human-in-the-loop design is often implemented as “approve everything”. That fails in a predictable way: people click approve forty times a day and stop reading by lunchtime.

A better rule is to gate on reversibility. Reads run freely, reversible writes run and get logged, and irreversible actions such as sending, paying or deleting wait for a person. Everything leaves a trace that a developer can replay when something looks odd. The gate above implements exactly this split.

Some teams build these pieces themselves, and others buy them. Elsewhen, a London-based consultancy that works on agentic operations for enterprise teams, describes its own service in similar terms. Its page lists approval gates where people sign off consequential decisions, an audit trail of what each agent read and which systems it touched, and a shared layer of permissions and guardrails that is reused across workflows. Elsewhen also says its Planet Agent report found that agents tend to start at the edges of an organization, where tasks are repeatable and mistakes are cheap, and then move toward the core. Treat that as the vendor’s own finding, and ask for the underlying evidence before relying on it.

The same logic applies outside engineering. The first question worth asking any AI partner is what they have actually put into production, and developers can ask the same of their own work.

No single control is enough. The table summarizes what each safeguard does well and where it falls short.

It is the OWASP term for an agent that can do more than it needs to, caused by excessive functionality, permissions or autonomy.

A prompt rule can be bypassed or ignored, while a scope check in the downstream API is enforced regardless of what the model says.

Usually not. Gating on reversibility keeps humans focused on actions such as sending, paying and deleting, and avoids approval fatigue.

Treat them like any third-party dependency. Review the tools they expose, limit scopes, pin versions and log calls.

The agentic projects that survive the next two years will probably not be the ones with the most ambitious autonomy. They will be the ones where someone sat down early and decided what the agent could touch, what it had to ask about and how anyone would know what it did.

That work does not demo well and it will not trend on social media. It is still the part that decides whether your agent becomes infrastructure or ends up among the projects that get cancelled.

#10148. TPU Model Design Considerations and Optimization Guide

Google designed TPUs from the ground up to be exceptionally efficient for machine learning workloads, delivering top-tier performance per total cost of ownership (TCO). Because TPUs trade general-purpose hardware scheduling for wide systolic arrays, dedicated SparseCore engines, and custom interconnect fabrics, adhering to specific architectural best practices is essential to achieve maximum performance. To help developers get the most out of TPUs, we are launching the open-source AI-Hypercomputer/tpu-codesign guide. This post highlights its key principles—spanning shape alignment, architectural choices like LatentMoE, and parallelism strategies—to accelerate LLMs, Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models, and other hardware-intensive workloads on TPUs.

Performance tuning starts with model definition. Architectural choices made before the first training step set permanent ceilings on MFU (Model FLOP Utilization), cost, and serving latency. This guide gives concrete, hardware-grounded design rules for teams building or adapting models for TPUs, organized into three groups:

How to use this guide. The shape rules apply to anyone designing a new model. If you are adapting an existing model rather than designing one, the Expressing Parallelism section applies without touching the checkpoint. The general rules hold for any large-matmul architecture (dense LLMs, multimodal, and world models), and the companion AI-Hypercomputer/tpu-codesign repository grows model- and workload-specific sections over time.

Eighth-generation TPUs. These principles carry forward to TPU 8t and TPU 8i and to the newly announced TPU 8tx and TPU 8i Max. The reasoning stays the same and the thresholds move with the hardware. TPU 8t and TPU 8i both add native FP4. For training, TPU 8t also brings more balanced Vector Processing Unit (VPU) scaling. For serving, TPU 8i adds the Boardfly topology, a Collectives Acceleration Engine (CAE), and 3x more on-chip SRAM. We note the effects inline in Principles 5–8, and follow-up posts will cover eighth-generation designs in depth.

Measured evidence. Alongside the published sources, notes marked Measured carry results from our own Ironwood profiling program: open 20–30B MoEs (a Qwen3-30B-derived LatentMoE variant; GPT-OSS-20B) plus dense controls, single slice (except for the 512-chip TPU v7x and 1,024-chip TPU v4 ablations in Principle 4), BF16, training shapes up to 32K sequence, all against tuned-systems baselines. Treat the trends and rankings as transferable, the exact percentages as config-specific.

TPUs are purpose-built custom ML ASICs designed from the ground up to deliver state-of-the-art performance per total cost of ownership (TCO). While their architectural choices—such as wide 256×256 systolic arrays, software-managed VMEM, dedicated SparseCore engines, and direct multi-dimensional ICI fabrics—enable exceptional efficiency, they inherently create specific structural constraints and programming patterns that must be carefully followed to unlock maximal performance. Reaching peak efficiency requires aligning model shapes, routing, and parallelism directly with these underlying hardware characteristics:

Make d_model, FFN and expert intermediate widths (d_ff), latent widths, attention projections, and padded vocabulary sizes (used as contracting or output dimensions) multiples of 256 to match the 256×256 MXU systolic array. Token/batch axes and counts such as heads, layers, and experts are exempt. The most frequently missed dimension is head_dim: at 64 or 128, flash-attention QK products leave the MXU ≥ 50% idle. Reshape to head_dim = 256 with proportionally fewer Q and KV heads, preserving total projection widths and logical KV cache bytes (iso-quality in our ablations).

Measured: Reshaping Qwen3-30B MoE attention from 32 × 128 to 16 × 256 (identical parameters and FLOPs) sped up end-to-end training, with gains widening at longer context:

These rules apply for non-quadratic attention as well, notably GDN ablations from Yang et al. 2025 also noted at par or better quality for head dimension 256 for their Gated DeltaNet Attention.

XLA compiles each program for exact tensor shapes, so every buffer has a fixed address and every loop a known trip count, and a new shape means a recompilation and a host stall. What about dynamic shapes? Dynamic workloads fit this model: the trick is to separate the shape of a buffer from the extent of the data inside it.

Instead of padding to the largest group or the longest sequence, we keep the buffers at fixed shapes (tokens sorted by expert and padded only at the tail, the KV cache as a fixed pool of pages) and describe the ragged structure with a few small integer arrays (group sizes and offsets; KV lengths, the page table, cumulative query lengths). Pallas copies these into the TensorCore's scalar memory before the grid starts (PrefetchScalarGridSpec), and the kernel reads them to choose the next tile and bound its loops, with a mask covering a tile that straddles a group boundary or a partly filled last page. The data dependence becomes an address and a trip count instead of a shape, so the compiled program is the same for every routing and every batch composition and the kernel runs at the unpadded FLOP count.

Standard EP dispatches every routed token at full d_model width, making EP + FSDP slower than plain FSDP in our Ironwood benchmarks. LatentMoE projects routed tokens from d_model down to latent width ℓ (d/ℓ ≈ 2–4×) before dispatch, executes experts in latent space, and projects back up after combine (introduced by Nemotron 3 and adopted by 962B MAI-Base-1 and 2.8T Kimi K3):

Measured: On a 30B / 128-expert MoE, an ℓ = 768 (2.67×) LatentMoE cut on-device permute time 2.8×, lifted end-to-end training throughput +14% tokens/s (322 vs 270 TFLOP/s/chip, or +19%, since the latent projections add ~5% FLOPs per token; tokens stay ℓ-wide past the down-projection, so on-device permute and combine shrink alongside EP all-to-all), and freed ~25 GB/device of activation HBM when remat checkpoints were anchored on the compressed latent. While current MoE recipes in tpu-recipes run FSDP (+ TP), LatentMoE + FP8 dispatch makes EP roofline-viable when per-device batch is too small to hide FSDP weight all-gathers. Under both FSDP and EP, avoid over-fragmenting experts: small tokens × K / N group sizes starve MXU tiles.

When candidate MoE layouts match on quality, pick the one with higher compute intensity at a similar active-parameter budget. A larger shared expert (wider d_shared) shifts work into dense, communication-free GEMMs that overlap EP dispatch/combine in training and amortize weight reads across the decode batch. Fewer, wider routed experts (halving N and K while doubling d_ff at fixed sparsity K/N) cut dispatch traffic in half and double per-expert GEMM fill. Optimal (N, K) grows with model size, and over-coarsening hurts routing diversity. Ablate expert granularity and the shared-to-routed split at your target scale rather than copying GPU co-designed models, since the TPU optimum is more shared-heavy.

Measured: On a 63B LatentMoE (ℓ = 768, K/N = 1/32, 8K context, 512 TPU v7x chips at EP = 128), halving (N, K) from (512, 16) to (256, 8) while doubling d_ff and the shared expert lifted training throughput +57% tok/s and held lower loss across 120.8B tokens:

Low-concurrency decode streams model weights every token (~300 concurrent tokens per weight shard are needed in dense FP8 to reach compute roofline). Combine two architectural levers with explicit concurrency sizing:

Norms, activations, masking, routing math and quantization scale applies run on the Vector Processing Unit (VPU), which is roughly two orders of magnitude below the MXU in throughput and executes in sequence with the matmuls unless XLA fuses it into an adjacent GEMM or we move it off the TensorCore. Therefore we drop reshapes and transposes that carry no quality, standardize on fusable RMSNorm and SwiGLU so the elementwise work folds into the neighbouring GEMM, and offload the MoE sort, gather and scatter to the SparseCore.

Note that fused VPU work is free only while its cycle count fits inside the MXU window it is fused into. A quick illustration of this concept is block-scaled fp8 GEMM.

With a scale per block of B elements along the contraction axis, the MXU emits one partial sum per output element per block and the VPU rescales each one (about three vector ops), so per output element the MXU does 2K flops and the VPU does 3·ceil(K/B) ops. The rescale hides when B ≥ 1.5 × r_mxu / r_vpu, which on v7x (fp8 peak 4,614 TFLOPS against a 19.2 TFLOPS vector peak, a ratio near 240) puts the threshold near 340. In simulation on a DeepSeek-V3 expert shape, block 512 runs at the per-tensor time, block 256 is VPU-bound at 1.2x and block 128 at 2.4x. Achieved MXU efficiency is lower than peak, which lowers the effective ratio and can make the smaller blocks workable; the way to decide is to measure the GEMM at each block instead of using peak ratio. This threshold is specific to Ironwood. TPU 8t is designed with specialized support for scaling so quantization for MXFP4 and MXFP8 formats has no overhead. Re-measure the block-size threshold there instead of reusing 340. The rule itself carries forward: keep vector work hidden inside a matmul window.

For workloads dominated by BF16-sensitive matmuls (most notably long-context quadratic attention where direct FP8 degrades accuracy), native single-pass BF16 leaves MXU capacity unused whenever hardware low-precision MXU throughput exceeds native BF16 throughput by more than the pass count. Use the multi-pass BF16 algorithm to execute BF16-accurate matmuls via multiple passes over the higher-throughput lower-precision MXU pipeline without losing numerical fidelity. This matters more as low-precision throughput pulls ahead of BF16. TPU 8t adds native FP4, which doubles MXU throughput. On Ironwood the FP8 to BF16 ratio is exactly 2:1, so two or more passes cannot beat native BF16.

Assign communication-heavy parallel axes to the fastest physical links and keep logical mesh factors aligned with torus dimensions:

Minimum-degree CP matched to kernel trade-offs: All-gather CP is simplest (though its all-gathers can sit exposed) when paired with zigzag load balancing (context_parallel_load_balance in MaxText) so causal masking does not idle tail ranks; Ring CP overlaps collectives but trades off SplashAttention chunk efficiency; Ulysses A2A CP avoids causal skew but exposes all-to-alls and caps at head count.

Start every project with the quality-safe foundation: 256-aligned dimensions (1), static shapes with dropless routing (2), one parallelism plan mapped to physical links (8), wall-clock ablations on a tuned stack, and published reference targets (both in Measuring what matters below). For MoEs, add LatentMoE (3) after an ablation to cut routing/dispatch traffic and decode weight reads. Then prioritize by bottleneck regime:

Google engineered TPUs from the ground up as custom ML ASICs to deliver top-tier performance per total cost of ownership (TCO). Unlocking that potential requires bridging the gap between hardware architecture and model design. By standardizing on 256-aligned shapes, leveraging LatentMoE, and mapping parallel axes directly to physical links, engineering teams can maximize TPU efficiency across both training and serving workloads.

A reference blueprint is only as good as the loop used to validate it. Always evaluate architectural changes in wall-clock time on a tuned software stack rather than paper FLOPs: in our Ironwood benchmarks, pure systems tuning (SparseCore offload, MXU-aligned tiles, and remat tuning) lifted an open 30B MoE from 12% to 23% MFU before any model change, and published Ironwood baselines show 256-aligned MoEs (DeepSeek-V3, Qwen3-235B at 608–630 BF16 TFLOP/s/chip) running at roughly 2× the throughput of unaligned GPT-OSS-120B (330 TFLOP/s/chip). For the 6-signal XProf (profiler=xplane) triage checklist, roofline and HBM budgeting rules, wall-clock ablation methodology, published Ironwood reference targets, and ongoing updates as new TPU generations and kernels land, explore the living diagnostics guide and companion patterns in the open-source AI-Hypercomputer/tpu-codesign repository.

#10132. Taming Typography: Building a Modern CSS Reset

CSS can now make choices about type that used to sit outside a reset. text-wrap: pretty can prevent a stranded word, container query units can scale a heading to the column it occupies, and text-box can trim the half-leading built into a line box. Some of these features are experimental and browser support is uneven, so they need guardrails rather than blind inclusion.

I’ve put together tidy-typography.css, which gathers the useful parts into a default stylesheet: rendering and fallback rules, fluid sizing, vertical rhythm, wrapping, hyphenation, and OpenType detail.

Use it whole, or take the declaration that solves the particular irritation in front of you. I’ll walk you through all the details in this article and give you the full code at the end.

font-synthesis: none stops a browser from inventing bold or italic when the family lacks that face. The smoothing and text-rendering declarations are rendering hints, mainly for WebKit; results still vary by platform.

text-size-adjust disables mobile font inflation that can make a deliberately sized layout jump on rotation. font-size-adjust: from-font uses font metrics to reduce the apparent size change while a fallback swaps for a webfont.

(As with other snippets in this article, this ⁠:root block is a partial excerpt the final stylesheet combines it with additional custom properties defined later on.)

Viewport-based fluid type does not know how wide its element is. A heading in a narrow sidebar can therefore scale as though it fills the viewport.

container-type: inline-size makes the wrapper a container query, so its children can use cqi: a unit based on the container rather than the viewport. Replacing vw in clamp() lets the same heading scale appropriately in an article column or a 300px sidebar without component-specific media queries.

line-height includes space above the cap-height and below the baseline: half-leading. It becomes obvious when text must align tightly with another element. text-box-trim removes it, while text-box-edge measures from capital-letter caps to the alphabetic baseline. This is a metrics-aware alternative to font-specific negative-margin nudges.

Selectors Level 5 proposes :heading, matching h1 to h6 and [role="heading"]. It has not landed in browsers, so keep it behind an @supports selector() check.

Default heading wrapping can leave one short final line. text-wrap: balance distributes text across its lines and works best for blocks of up to about six lines. For paragraphs, text-wrap: pretty avoids a stranded final word without balancing the whole block. Both replace JavaScript orphan fixers. Editable text needs text-wrap: stable, which prevents each new word from reshuffling the paragraph.

hyphens: auto relies on the browser’s language dictionaries, so the document needs a correct lang attribute (for example ). Without one the browser has no dictionary to use and won’t be able to hyphenate text. Even with the correct lang, the defaults can leave tiny word fragments or several hyphenated lines in a row.

hyphenate-limit-chars controls the smallest breakable word and fragments; hyphenate-limit-lines prevents a ladder effect. hyphenate-limit-zone makes breaks less frequent, while hyphenate-limit-last protects the final word. Safari needs the prefixed equivalents, so declare both sets together.

Set code, variables, and headings to hyphens: manual: a broken code word can change meaning, and a hyphenated heading usually reads as an error.

Punctuation marks don’t carry the same visual weight as letters, so a quotation mark sitting at the start of a line can look slightly indented against the text below it. hanging-punctuation lets specified punctuation sit fractionally outside the text block’s edge.

The first value handles opening punctuation, and last handles closing punctuation at the end of a block, while allow-end lets punctuation hang past the right edge rather than force-wrapping. The result is a text block whose edges read as genuinely flush.

Underlines have a related problem. Browsers apply flat, one-size-fits-all thickness and position regardless of the font’s actual weight.

text-decoration-thickness: from-font reads the thickness the font’s designer intended. text-underline-offset gives descenders room to breathe, and text-decoration-skip-ink: auto interrupts the underline wherever a descender crosses it rather than drawing straight through the letter.

Variable fonts often include an optical size axis, or opsz. It lets a typeface use thinner strokes at large display sizes and thicker, more legible strokes when set small.

font-optical-sizing: auto lets the font make that adjustment based on its actual rendered size. The @supports block covers the older font-variation-settings syntax some browsers still need, while font-variant-ligatures and font-kerning enable the font’s own ligatures and kerning pairs.

Light text on a dark background genuinely reads heavier than the same weight on a light background. Where a font ships a GRAD, or grade, axis, the dark mode block dials that perceived weight down without changing glyph shape or width the way an actual weight change would. Fonts without a GRAD axis safely ignore the setting.

Mixing a monospace font into running prose often exposes a mismatched x-height at the same declared font-size. The size-adjust keyword, set once at the @font-face level, scales that font’s rendered output specifically, leaving the actual font-size value untouched.

Numbers in a table need identical widths so they stack into a clean column on the screen. Conversely, numbers in flowing text don’t need the same treatment and should sit naturally alongside the surrounding words.

The tabular-nums and lining-nums keywords give table digits a fixed width and full cap height. oldstyle-nums and proportional-nums let digits sit alongside lowercase letters without sticking up and disrupting the sentence’s texture.

font-variant-emoji makes the choice between full-colour and monochrome emoji explicit. Without it, the browser or platform gets to choose a fallback, and the result can vary more than you expect. Apply .emoji-color or .emoji-mono to containers where you want to enforce that choice, rather than setting it globally on the body.

Quote style and character order differ by language and region. English nests single quotes inside double quotes, British English reverses that convention, and French uses guillemets with a thin space.

The quotes property declares the order for each language through :lang(). The native q element then inserts the right pair through open-quote and close-quote. The same markup can render correctly in each locale without hardcoding characters into the content.

Wrapping the rules in :where() keeps them at zero specificity, so a project can override the quote style with one plain class.

Default sub and sup styling is synthesized. The browser just shrinks the text and shifts the baseline, rather than using glyphs actually designed for that position.

font-variant-position switches to the font’s own purpose-built sub and superscript glyphs where they exist, resetting vertical-align, font-size, and line-height so they don’t fight against the real glyph’s intended size and position. Most typefaces only include proper numerals rather than letters, so check the specific font before applying this broadly.

:where() always resolves to zero specificity, which is useful for a reset. Every rule here is an opinionated default, and wrapping it in :where() lets a project override a property with a plain class.

:is() solves a different problem: repetition. The heading selector list appears several times in a typographic stylesheet, and grouping it avoids the risk of forgetting a level.

Unlike :where(), :is() carries the specificity of its most specific argument.

Here’s the complete reset, combining the stable layers. Unsupported declarations are ignored by the browser, so the stylesheet can be adopted gradually.

Much of this CSS removes work that used to leak into other parts of a build. text-wrap can take over orphan handling from JavaScript, container query units keep a component’s type scale tied to its own width, and text-box-trim avoids the negative margins once used to compensate for leading. quotes, font-variant-position, and font-optical-sizing hand several smaller details back to the language and the font instead of a collection of one-off fixes.

Browser support will continue to change, especially for text-wrap and text-box-trim. Unsupported declarations are ignored, but that only helps when the unenhanced typography is acceptable in its own right.

Some of these rules are still too new for a baseline reset. A news article, a marketing page, and an editable product interface do not ask the same things of their text; the set that belongs in yours depends on the font, the content, and the browsers you still need to support.

Andy Clarke shows you how to create designs that people recognize and remember in his brand-new e-book, Standout Designs for the Web. Order it today!

"English nests single quotes inside double quotes, British English reverses that convention,"

"English nests double quotes inside single quotes, American English reverses that convention (like it usually does),"

It’s nowhere near that cut-and-dried, and double has historically and geographically been much more popular. For some reason, UK publishing mostly switched to single for the 1900s, but they seem to be returning to double these days. Very few other countries followed them to single—Canada and Australia, for example, stuck with double.

A couple of links to start with (and you can find plenty more elsewhere): https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quotation_mark#In_English, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quotation_marks_in_English#Quotations_and_speech

Overall, :lang(en) double-first with :lang(en-GB) single-first is a decent balance. If you had :lang(en) single-first, you’d want :lang(en-US, en-CA, en-IN, en-AU, en-NZ, …) for double-first.

A more accurate wording of the first sentence would be:

Quote style and character order differ by locale and house style.

I don’t understand the reason to use font-synthesis: none;. Isn’t synthesis just a fallback for when the author makes a mistake with the font faces or when the font file doesn’t load? Users’ fallback font-families likely have weight and style variants but probably not small-caps, maybe it at least should be allowed to be synthesized.

Instead of max-width: 65ch; being applied to p, li, dd, I’d consider applying it to p, dl, ol, ul. Limiting the width of the lists, rather than the list items, prevents longer items from “sticking out” relative to the paragraphs above and below, do to the items’ inline-start margins, padding.

The browser should automatically use French-style quotes (« ») within lang=fr; appears the British quote convention is not automatically used when lang=en-GB, at least not on my USian computer.

It might be worth noting more clearly that some of these are experimental/straight up don’t work today.

hanging-punctuation is Safari-only and doesn’t seem to support quotation marks in my testing, so the text is perhaps inaccurate on that point? Many of the hyphenation properties also only work in Safari, and require the prefixes described as “legacy” in the article.

It’s mentioned briefly in the intro that some stuff is experimental, but being more explicit in the sections about what does/doesn’t work currently would be great.

Definitely cool to explore the upcoming features, but feels a bit odd to frame it as a “reset” when it will likely change how text looks in the future in untested/unexpected ways, and includes superfluous code that won’t actually impact rendering today.

Limiting body, p, li and dd width is not the job of a reset. That’s a per-site choice and should be with other per-site css, not part of a reset block. And there’s lot’s of other very opinionated stuff as well. I’ll stick with my old friend Eric Meyer.

-webkit-font-smoothing: antialiased; is contentious (ill-advised); text-rendering: optimizeLegibility; is unwarranted; font-synthesis: none; has implications the typical user may not realise or like (though avoiding the need for synthesis is definitely preferable); font-size-adjust: from-font; has problematic side-effects, trading one problem for another that is in my opinion at least as bad; text-box is not widely supported and has layout implications that mean your layout will be somewhat broken in other browsers; Given the preceding ruleset, [contenteditable="true"] { text-wrap: stable; } doesn’t work if the content is within an inner p, li, dd (which it typically will be); Hyphenation is just all-round dodgy and generally better avoided; Hanging punctuation is contentious and current implementations are really not very pleasing; Turning contextual alternatives on generally is not necessarily a good idea—there’s a reason it’s opt-in and separated from the normal set of ligatures; font-kerning: normal; is ill-advised: let the browser decide, if it decides not to do kerning, it’ll be for a good reason; font-variant-position: [sub|super]; is a terrible idea when combined with font-synthesis: none, because you can’t know what font the text will be rendered in (no, your web font is not guaranteed to work; more people than you might realise block them and/or force the use of their own chosen fonts—I for one, it makes the web so much better), and the fallback font is unlikely to support the feature; and finally, :is(h1, h2, h3, h4, h5, h6), p, blockquote, figcaption, li is a completely useless example of :is().

Thank you for gathering these typography rules into one tidy reset — the section on font-size-adjust stood out to me, as fallback fonts so often change the apparent size of a page while a webfont loads. I also appreciated the honest note that text-box-trim and hanging punctuation are still unevenly supported, so the guardrails matter as much as the declarations themselves. It is a genuinely useful starting point that I will try on a small project first.

A reset that handles fluid type and vertical rhythm together is the part most resets skip. Hyphens in the same file is a nice touch.

I'd test the trimmed text beside an SVG icon with the webfont blocked, especially when the label wraps.

Thank you for pulling all of this together into tidy-typography.css, Stu. The section I keep coming back to is the one on rendering and fallback sanity. I spend a lot of time previewing font pairings, and the fallback swap is exactly where most sites lose their rhythm — a display face loads, the fallback is visibly narrower or taller, and the whole layout jumps. Your point about font-size-adjust: from-font reducing that apparent size change while a webfont swaps in is the clearest explanation of that fix I have read, and pairing it with font-synthesis: none so the browser does not invent a bold or italic face the family does not actually have makes the reset feel considered rather than just defensive.

The fluid typography section was the most useful part for me in practice. Using container query units so a heading scales to the column it occupies, instead of clamp() with vw scaling it as though it were always in a full-width article, solves a real problem I hit constantly: the same heading in a 300px sidebar should not behave like the same heading in the main column. The same goes for text-wrap: pretty and text-wrap: balance replacing the JavaScript orphan fixers I used to reach for, and the hyphenation tuning with hyphenate-limit-chars and hyphenate-limit-lines — I had never thought carefully about the ladder effect of several hyphenated lines in a row until this article. I also appreciated the honest framing that text-box-trim and hanging-punctuation are still unevenly supported, so they need @supports guardrails rather than blind inclusion.

One practical habit from my own work that fits well with this reset: when I test a new pairing, I preview it at small sizes with font-optical-sizing: auto on, because a face that looks crisp at display size can turn thin and hard to read at body size, and I check the same text with the webfont blocked so I can see the fallback in isolation. For anyone who wants a quick place to try out different styles side by side while testing a pairing, I keep a handy reference at https://fontsgenerators.net/ — it is useful for seeing how a style holds up before committing it to a stylesheet like this one. I will be trying tidy-typography.css as a base on my next project and keeping the experimental declarations behind @supports, exactly as you suggest.

#10129. Top 10 Black Friday Deals for Designers and Agencies in 2026

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A tool bought at up to 98% off this Black Friday lowers the cost of every client project built with it in 2027. The discounts below cover site builders, booking software, form and table plugins, animated sliders, a WordPress theme and an illustration library.

The offers arrive by the dozen, and few of them are aimed at web professionals. This list keeps ten, chosen for developers and designers who build client sites in WordPress and PHP, JavaScript included.

Each section covers what the product does, the features that matter on client work, a customer review where one exists and a link to the offer. A comparison table at the end puts all ten side by side.

Overview: Brizy AI & White Label gives agencies the tools to build and deliver client websites faster, manage projects, and offer website-building services under their own brand. Brizy AI generates complete, responsive websites from a text prompt or Google Business Profile, while its intuitive drag-and-drop editor lets designers customize every detail. With White Label capabilities, agencies can provide clients with a branded website-building experience without developing or maintaining their own website builder.

“Brizy’s website builder UI is one of the easiest to understand among all the major page-builder plugins; it is simple and efficient but contains everything you need to build professional websites.”

Overview: With IvyForms, a new WordPress form looks clean and responsive before any styling, and fields are dragged, resized, reordered and set in multi-column layouts on one screen. Deeper styling controls are there when a form has to match a client’s brand. The same builder handles a contact form and a multi-step workflow with conditional logic.

“A breath of fresh air for WordPress forms. It’s fast, modern, and just works. Setting up a new form took me less than two minutes. Definitely keeping this on my site.”

Every prompt returns four versions with different layouts and content structures, each in its own visual direction. Edits happen in a chat with the AI. Hosting and a domain are included.
