#10123. A Practical Guide to Browser Developer Tools for Debugging and Improving Websites

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Building a website involves more than writing HTML, CSS, and JavaScript. Developers also need to test how their code behaves in different browsers, identify layout problems, investigate JavaScript errors, and understand why certain pages take longer to load.

Browser developer tools make these tasks easier. They provide a way to inspect the code currently running in a browser without constantly changing files or guessing what might be wrong.

Whether you're developing a new website or maintaining an existing application, learning to use these tools can improve your debugging workflow and help you write more reliable code.

Let's explore some useful browser development tools and how to apply them to everyday web development tasks.

Browser developer tools, commonly called DevTools, are built-in utilities available in modern browsers such as Google Chrome, Mozilla Firefox, Microsoft Edge, and Safari.

They allow developers to examine a webpage's HTML structure, modify CSS properties, debug JavaScript, inspect network requests, and test responsive layouts.

You can usually open them by right-clicking a webpage and selecting Inspect. On Windows and Linux, the F12 key or Ctrl + Shift + I shortcut also opens DevTools in most browsers.

The following table highlights some commonly used features:

Understanding what each panel does makes it easier to select the right tool for a particular problem.

The Elements panel is one of the most frequently used features in browser DevTools.

It displays the Document Object Model (DOM), which represents the structure of the webpage currently loaded in the browser. Developers can select individual elements and examine the CSS rules affecting their appearance.

For example, imagine that a heading has more spacing than expected. Instead of immediately editing the stylesheet, inspect the heading and review its computed styles.

You might discover that the spacing comes from a margin inherited through another CSS rule or from a conflicting selector.

You can also experiment with CSS properties directly inside DevTools.

If the card appears too wide, you can temporarily change its width or padding in the Styles panel.

The browser immediately displays the result, allowing you to compare different values without repeatedly editing and saving your stylesheet.

However, these temporary changes do not automatically update your original CSS files. Once you've identified the correct solution, apply it to your project source code.

For more detailed CSS debugging techniques, developers can also read SitePoint's guide on CSS Debugging and Optimization: Browser-based Developer Tools.

JavaScript errors can prevent interactive features from working correctly. A navigation menu might stop opening, a form might fail to submit, or a button might not trigger the expected action.

The Console panel helps developers investigate these problems.

For example, consider the following JavaScript:

If the page does not contain an element with the ID submitButton, the selected value will be null. Calling addEventListener() on that value causes an error.

You can prevent this particular issue by checking whether the element exists:

The Console panel also allows developers to execute JavaScript expressions manually.

This returns the number of image elements currently present in the document.

You can use similar expressions to inspect elements, check values, and test small pieces of functionality.

Sometimes a JavaScript error is not immediately visible. A function may execute successfully but produce an unexpected result.

In such cases, the Sources panel in Chrome DevTools or the Debugger panel in Firefox can help.

A breakpoint pauses JavaScript execution at a specific line. Developers can then inspect variable values, examine the call stack, and execute the code step by step.

If the application displays an incorrect total, you can place a breakpoint inside calculateTotal().

When the function executes, the browser pauses and allows you to inspect the values of price, quantity, and total.

This is more useful than repeatedly adding console.log() statements throughout a large application.

Breakpoints are particularly helpful when debugging complex functions, asynchronous operations, and event handlers.

A webpage may contain several resources, including images, stylesheets, JavaScript files, fonts, and API requests.

If one of these resources fails to load, part of the website may stop working correctly.

The Network panel provides information about requests made by the browser.

It displays request URLs, HTTP status codes, response sizes, loading times, and other useful details.

For example, the following table explains some common HTTP responses developers may encounter:

Suppose an image is missing from a product page. Open the Network panel, reload the page, and filter the requests by images.

If the image returns a 404 response, check whether the file path is correct and whether the resource exists on the server.

The same process can help investigate failed API requests.

For example, if a frontend application requests product information from an API, the Network panel allows you to inspect the request method, response status, and returned data.

This helps developers distinguish between frontend problems and backend errors.

A website that looks correct on a desktop computer may not work properly on a smartphone.

Text can overflow, navigation menus can become difficult to use, and images may extend beyond their containers.

Browser DevTools include responsive design and device emulation features that allow developers to test different viewport sizes.

In Chrome, you can enable the device toolbar from the DevTools interface. Firefox provides a similar feature called Responsive Design Mode.

The desktop layout displays three columns, while smaller screens display one column.

You can test this behavior by changing the viewport width inside DevTools.

A useful responsive testing process includes:

Checking common mobile and tablet widths.

Testing both portrait and landscape orientations.

Checking whether navigation remains usable.

Verifying that buttons and links are easy to interact with.

Device emulation is useful during development, but it does not completely replace testing on physical devices.

A website can have correct HTML, CSS, and JavaScript but still provide a poor experience if it loads slowly or performs unnecessary work.

The Performance panel helps developers investigate browser activity over time.

It can record page interactions and provide information about scripting, rendering, painting, and other browser operations.

For example, a page might become unresponsive when a user opens a navigation menu. A performance recording can help identify whether a long-running JavaScript function is blocking the main thread.

Developers can also use tools such as Lighthouse to audit performance, accessibility, SEO, and other aspects of a webpage.

Instead of making random optimization changes, use the available measurements to identify the actual problem.

Some useful areas to investigate include:

Performance analysis should be repeated after making changes to determine whether the optimization actually improves the result.

These features are useful, but they can also introduce debugging challenges.

For example, an application might continue displaying outdated information because an old value remains stored in local storage.

The Application panel in Chrome DevTools allows developers to inspect and manage several types of browser storage.

If the application behaves unexpectedly because of stored data, inspecting local storage can help identify the problem.

Be careful when clearing storage because doing so may remove authentication sessions, saved preferences, or other application data.

Knowing individual DevTools features is useful, but developers benefit even more from using them in a consistent order.

The following workflow can make troubleshooting more efficient:

This process helps developers identify the cause of a problem before changing multiple parts of the application.

It also makes debugging easier when working with other developers because the issue can be reproduced and explained using specific observations.

Browser developer tools are essential for modern web development. They help developers inspect HTML and CSS, debug JavaScript, analyze network requests, test responsive layouts, and investigate performance issues.

The most effective approach is to select the appropriate tool for the problem instead of making changes based on assumptions.

Whether you're working on a small website or a complex web application, becoming familiar with DevTools can make your development workflow more efficient and help you build websites that are easier to maintain.

Rather than treating debugging as something that happens only after development, make it a regular part of your workflow. This allows you to identify problems earlier, verify your solutions, and deliver more reliable web experiences.

#10121. CSS-Tricks, Version 20

I’ve been bashing on updating this site’s design for the last couple of weeks. It’s high time things get a fresh coat of paint, right? The last version of CSS-Tricks was pushed five years ago. That’s ancient in web (and dog) years.

It’s not like the site has simply sat all that time. We pushed lots of changes and shipped new features, particularly since 2024, but we never really got the paint brushes out of storage.

Now, before there’s a chorus of “things don’t look thaaaat different” I want to say I’m rarely a fan of complete re-builds and tend to lean into iterative releases. And the new v20 theme is what I’d call an iterative rollup that’s worthy of a version bump.

Let’s go over the most notable updates to see why.

First and foremost, some of the biggest changes are what you don’t see. As you might imagine, this site has carried over plenty of pieces across 19 years. I decided to excavate the theme and definitely found some ghosts to bust.

For example, the global compiled JavaScript file retained some scripts that just weren’t being used anymore. Things like handling embedded versions of the newsletter from before we began self-hosting the posts. There were relics that catered specifically to the Flexbox Guide for handling toggles back before we had proper HTML

. There was a jQuery lazy-loader thing that is well-covered by WordPress core now. We had some scripts for serving third-party ads that are no longer needed now that we’re self-hosting ads. Things like that. The overall JavaScript was nicely trimmed from 151.6 KB (55.3 KB gzipped) to 133.8 KB (48.1 KB).

I also decided to formally retire the CSS-Tricks Forum. We put it in “archive mode” back in, like, 2018. None of those discussions are particularly valuable for today as they were over 10 years ago, so I deactivated the bbPress plugin that powered it and cleared out the database… and wow, that carved a whopping 400 MB from the ol’ database, not to mention it allowed me to drop several custom templates from the theme.

There were more ghosts busted along the way and, to be sure, there are still some lurking around. But it’s way less scary poking around the theme directory now, and further cleanup should be a lot easier from here on out.

Yeah, yeah, raise your hand if you’re still running Gulp. We are 🙋‍♂️. That’s not a bad thing in and of itself, but keeping it in a fixed state for all this time is not the greatest. I think we’re sporting a more modern workflow than before, even if there’s still room to grow. A smattering of the related commits:

For years, I endured the same errors every time I fired up a new terminal. Those are gone and things run super cleanly, not to mention quickly. Very happy here.

Oof, this is everyone’s favorite job, right?! Aside from being able to spot and fix a few unescaped custom field calls here and there, I decided to ask Claude to help me with a holistic audit. I prompted it to review recent WordPress security release notes and identify areas where the theme could be hardened to comply with what WordPress is doing.

Whoa, you used AI? Yeah, I sure did. But for things like this where it could be my little gopher and allow me more time to do what I do best: front-end. I’m still able to make most of the patches myself based on the output recommendations, but it’s nice to have a gut-check for things where I’m clearly no expert, like security.

I still use and like Sass very much. I mean, yes, I would like to be straight-up vanilla CSS everywhere, but I can’t tear myself away from partials in particular.

The previous theme had a fairly nice “flat” structure — very few partial directories and most things all in the same top-level source. Totally cool. But an organization nut like me (you should see my house) has to have a place for everything to live. It ain’t perfect, but I’m happy with what I was able to put together without leveling the building in the process.

There are some compromises. Like, /header should really be in /layout. The ordering is really important, though, and some of my ideals were simply not possible. I’m hoping to eventually work in some sort of Cascade Layers structure.

I’m pleased as punch to see the overall size of CSS drop in the process. It went from 180 KB (31.7 KB) to 169 KB (24.6 KB). Not hugely significant, but not not significant either. I had to do a couple tricky things to cheat my way there, like serving all the form styles I’m about to talk about into a separate forms.css file and serving it just on pages where WooCommerce, Restrict Content Pro, and Gravity Forms are enqueued.

Are you starting to notice a trend? We’re still talking about fairly invisible work. CSS-Tricks has so many nooks and crannies that it’s nearly impossible to make things perfectly consistent across the board. But because of those nooks and crannies, so many fixed values, magic numbers, one-off styles, and older utilities were hiding about. I decided to tackle a good chunk of that while reorganizing the Sass.

For example, there are now semantic design tokens that abstract away one-off colors. So many grays, let me tell you. Now there’s a maintainable palette that’s referenced throughout. Same with fonts, font styles, and font sizes. Same with paddings and margins. It feels a lot better to make a change at the “system” level with confidence that the change is taking effect everywhere it needs to.

But this is also where some clearly visible changes happened! Most notably, link styles are consistent all over the place with a small handful of intended and maintained variations. Not only is it more pleasing visually, but it improves accessibility throughout because the inherited styles default to proper visual cues that do not rely solely on color or opacity changes.

Oh, and my favorite piece of this? Forms and tables have both been redone. That’s tough to do in a WordPress theme where these components don’t always come from one source. For example, forms could come from any one of the Gravity Forms, WooCommerce, or Restrict Content Pro plugins, and they all have their own stylesheets. Resetting those allowed me to bring a certain level of harmony in all those instances.

OK, something even more visual. This is the first time CSS-Tricks has had more than one color scheme! It certainly could have been done a long time ago, but I’d also argue that the design hasn’t really called for a light/dark toggle over the last 8 years or so. It’s a nice blend of both light and dark elements that marry the best of both worlds no matter your theme preference.

But I had to take a stab, right? The site has predominantly been dark despite the content being against a white background, so I treated things as though a “dark” mode already existed. The result is a creamy light theme that is largely just a change in background and component styles. And I used some of what I learned from the recent “toggle” debate to handle system, dark, and light themes with a single toggle.

What hasn’t changed is the main content container you see in things like articles. That was already white, even in a “dark” mode, and I’m not confident that simply toggling it between white and black is the best approach. Something on the radar for sure.

Oh, and it’s all wired up to a view transition that slides the new theme over the previous one.

I hope you think the site’s header and footer are cute. Yes, cute. That’s what I was going for when adding a colorful series of dots as the backdrop.

Sorta like a subtle candy texture, right? Or a colorful group of stars? I dunno, but I’m calling it “Jawbreaker” for lack of a better term and because it reminds me of the jawbreaker candy I loved as a kid. Naming still sucks.

It’s all part of the same SVG. I really, really (really) wanted to random()-ly position dots around, but that would have taken a lot of empty individual elements (e.g., ) in the markup. Not my favorite. So, I generate the elements in PHP:

I really, really wanted to random()-ize all the things, but inlining random coordinates on each was the way to go. Same deal with the inlined styles that set a fill, and sizing for each circle in the texture, and variables that set a --delay and --dur on the subtle animation that twinkles them in and out.

I’ll have to look at generating the SVG using or whatever later since that pattern repeats in the footer, too.

That’s carried through to the 404 page as well:

If your browser supports border-shape and random() (only Chrome, I think), then the homepage gives you a blobby-blob shape behind the grid of author avatars. Hover it and it gets, you know, even blobbier.

It takes an existing pattern I’m calling .gradient-card:

…and sets a gradient on the component’s :before pseudo:

That sits underneath the :after pseudo that’s translucent, allowing the :before‘s gradient to show through a smidge:

The differing border-radius values allow :before to bleed outside, giving the impression of a border around the entire deal. That’s the basic shape. The border-shape is where things get blobby:

Those variables? They’re mapped to shape()s. I grabbed the first one from Temani Afif’s blob generator:

It’s called “static” because that’s the default state. Hover or focus on the component and it swaps out those points out with a “hover” version of the shape:

Those variables? They’re mapped to random() values:

So, the hover state is different on each page load. And we animate between the two:

I’m pretty stoked on this one. The Almanac has grown, evolved, and just plain gotten great the past few years. But the primary page for it… not so great. It was definitely overshadowed by work on the pages inside it and never revisited beyond clickable cards to get to each feature subset.

Now the page is a real index of the CSS features that gets you where you need to go quickly. Someone somewhere sometime did some sort of CSS periodic table. I forget where I’d seen that but the general idea sat with me and I thought I could pull it off. There’s nothing “tricky” about it but I love what the layout adds to the overall site.

This is another area that sorely needed love. Our guides have always been a vertical stack of items. The book effect was definitely cool, but I started believing that leaning on it as the main visual hook was holding things back from being more functional. With something like 27 guides, we’re talking about a lot of scrolling with no clear idea of what you’re going to see as you go.

Again, nothing “tricky” but I like the overall usefulness of the new layout. There’s a featured guide, a widget of newly added and updated guides, and a categorized grid of everything below that.

The pages that show all of an author’s published articles got a little spruce-up, too.

It’s a little change, really. The right sidebar was moved to the left and the author image and bio moved in, allowing the articles to creep up to the top. I hope to do more here since the small layout tweak opens up more opportunities.

Also need to do the larger Authors index, but another time.

This one’s just for those of us working in the WordPress editor. I don’t need a complete visual editor to get things done, but it is a nice affordance to have some of the front-end styles sneak into the editing UI for a better idea of what you’re working with.

Using Lighthouse to measure things like performance, accessibility, and best practices is woefully fraught with caveats, but there’s no reason to chuck it completely. I find them more fun than fact and I don’t mind the recommendations for improving things.

So, after a few runs and tweaks, here’s how things shook out theme-to-theme.

I’m happy here. There are some things I just don’t have the control to fix when it comes to performance, so seeing v20 come in consistent with v19 is just fine by me. I’m happier with the boosts to accessibility and best practices. SEO? Meh. Don’t really care unless it’s hurting more important things like accessibility.

Again, very happy. There’s little I can do with time to first byte but everything else seems to clock in at least marginally better. The total page weight is a clear win and that’s probably what I care most about here.

Oh gosh, there’s a lot more but they’re mostly minor things that don’t bubble up to the front end. Sort of the same way browser changelogs have some generic line of “bug squashing” or whatever. In this case, it’s things like better post queries and breadcrumbs and function management and naming and… you get it.

There’s definitely so much more to do. And plenty of things I’m sure I missed or broke along the way. That’s just life and those will have to come in another iterative release less worthy of a version bump. You can let me know what I muffed up in the comments.

I hope you at least like where this is going!

Andy Clarke shows you how to create designs that people recognize and remember in his brand-new e-book, Standout Designs for the Web. Order it today!

#10119. Agents are sessions, not requests: size GKE node pools for AI agent sessions

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Your company wants to move its coding agents from developer laptops to a shared GKE cluster, for persistence, scale, and governance. Each of those agent sessions stays open all day but works only part of it, and how you run them decides how much of your node bill pays for waiting.

By the end of this article, you will know why an agent session needs a different runtime than a web request, how Agent Substrate and AX run sessions on GKE, and how to identify cost optimization opportunities in your GKE node pools. This article assumes you know Kubernetes Pods and node pools. If not, check out the GKE overview.

A web request lasts milliseconds. The server answers it, keeps nothing, and serves the next one, so Kubernetes scales a web app by its traffic: more requests, more Pods.

An agent session works another way. A coding agent's workspace can stay open for the developer's whole workday: it keeps its memory and its files between prompts, and it works only after a prompt arrives.

How much of that day does the agent really work? Much less than it waits. In a July 2026 study of agent traces, Banruo Liu and colleagues found that in a typical session with several prompts, the agent waits for the user about 80% of the time. A turn, one prompt and the agent's whole response to it, takes about 6.5 minutes of agent work on average

Put those numbers on one developer's day. With 2 prompts an hour over an 8 hour workday, which is our assumption, the agent works 22% of the day and waits for nearly 78% of it. If each session holds a machine, you pay for that waiting time

Teams usually pick one of two answers, and both work well for web apps.

The first is to give each session its own Pod. The session keeps its state, but its Pod holds 1 vCPU and 1 GiB all day. For example, a c3-standard-4 node fits 3 such Pods after GKE's reservations, so in our cost model 100 coding agents need 34 nodes and about USD 1,288 a month, for agents that work 22% of the time.

The second is to scale the Pod to zero between prompts. The node's capacity is released, and files on a persistent volume survive, but the process and its memory do not, so the next prompt starts a new agent process.

Based on that, you want a third option: keep the session, and free the machine while the session waits.

Agent Substrate and AX are built for that: the developer's session stays open, and the sandbox that serves it gives its machine back while the session waits. Together they cover the parts of an agentic platform that are the same for most teams: the control plane, the runtime, and the sandbox. Agent Substrate answers where and how an agent runs. AX answers what the agent is.

Following the CNCF donation of gVisor, Agent Substrate, and OpenRL in November 2026, you can run Agent Substrate on GKE or on other Kubernetes clusters. Agent Substrate 1.0 is generally available on GKE.

One distinction helps from here on. The session is the multi turn interaction between the developer and the agent, with its conversation history. The compute that serves it is an isolated sandbox: an actor in Agent Substrate, or a Task in AX. Parking acts on the sandbox, and the session stays open.

Agent Substrate answers where and how an agent runs. It separates the stateful agent from the compute it runs on. An actor is the sandbox for one agent, and it holds the agent's running processes, files, and identity. A worker is a reusable sandbox drawn from a warm pool of Kubernetes Pods, and one worker hosts several actors.

An agent runs code that a model wrote, so anything in its environment can leak through a prompt injection or a bad command, API keys included. Gateways control the actor's inbound and outbound traffic and add credentials on the way out, based on the actor's identity, so credentials stay out of the agent's environment.

SuspendActor snapshots an idle actor to object storage and frees its worker, and the next request restores the actor on a warm worker with room for it. Agent Substrate snapshots memory by default and falls back to files when it cannot capture memory. Many parked sessions share a small warm pool, so in our cost model the same 100 agents need 16 nodes instead of 34.

AX answers what the agent is and what it knows. You declare a Task, which says what runs (image, command, and limits), and a Workspace, which says what the agent knows (repositories and files). AX runs each Task as an actor. ax suspend parks the task and keeps its /workspace. Today AX asks for a volume snapshot, so the files come back on resume and the agent's processes start fresh (runner guide).

AX passes suspend, resume, and delete to Agent Substrate. The AX roadmap plans to add budgets, policies, and gateways, which limit what an agent may do, and a record of what each task did.

Two details decide the bill before you size anything.

First, you decide when a sandbox parks. Agent Substrate does not suspend an idle actor by itself (#424), so your agent harness, or the service in front of it, calls the suspend API after an idle timeout. Until then, an idle actor keeps its worker. When idle actors hold every worker, a request for another actor waits up to 5 seconds by default, then fails with HTTP 503 (Agent Substrate docs).

Second, you decide how much CPU each sandbox is promised. An actor is sized by its CPU and memory limits, and each worker books those limits against the capacity you declare for its pool (API guide). Our cost model sets that capacity to what the node really has, so every actor keeps its full limit. You can declare more capacity than the node has, and then you overcommit CPU at the pool instead of in each Pod.

Our cost model prices parking for the same 100 coding agents, at Google Cloud on-demand list prices, as of November 2026, in us-central1.

With a 2 minute idle timeout, each session holds a worker nearly 29% of its open time. A warm pool sized for bursts (the 99th percentile of sessions holding a worker at once, if sessions act independently, plus 15% headroom) needs 15 nodes. Add one node that runs around the clock for the Agent Substrate control plane and its PostgreSQL database, and a job that scales the pool to zero after hours:

Parking saves nearly 40% of the node bill. The pool is full about once in 3,000 turns, so a prompt rarely meets that 503. In an hour at 2.5 prompts instead of 2, the same 15 nodes are full for 3% of turns, so size the pool for your busiest hour.

Keep in mind that parking pays most when your agents wait the most. At 2 prompts an hour, the saving is nearly 40%. At 4 prompts an hour, the agent works 44% of the hour, the pool grows to 27 nodes, and the saving is 4%. Above about 4 prompts an hour, one Pod per session becomes the cheaper option, so measure your prompt rate first.

Also, the pool size is yours to set. The autoscaling option in the GKE install guide follows worker Pods, not suspended actors, so scale the pool to zero after hours with a scheduled job: a pool that runs all 730 hours of the month costs nearly twice the one Pod per session bill.

Finally, the fleet size matters. The Agent Substrate control plane costs the same for 10 sessions or 1,000, so parking pays from about 37 coding sessions, or 13 for an assumed chat assistant that works about a minute an hour.

Overcommit changes the comparison. If your Pods request less CPU than their limit, Kubernetes places them by their requests. A Pod that requests 0.25 vCPU with a 1 vCPU limit packs 12 to a node (its 1 GiB of memory caps it there), so the same 100 coding agents fit on 9 nodes, fewer than our parked pool. Agent Substrate can overcommit too, through the capacity you declare on the worker pool, and our cost model does not price that case.

Overcommit has the same cost on both sides: agents share CPU and slow down when their neighbors are busy, during builds and tests. Compare like with like before you decide to park: Pods and actors with the same CPU guarantee.

Back to the shared cluster. Agents are sessions, not requests: size the warm pool for the sessions that hold a worker, not the sessions that are open.

We would park long lived sessions that wait most of the hour, and keep one Pod per session when developers prompt more than about 4 times an hour. Size the warm pool for working time plus a short idle timeout at your busiest hour, and compare with your current Pods at the same CPU guarantee before you park.

Thanks for reading! If you run agent sessions on Kubernetes today, how many prompts an hour do your users send? The prompt rate is the input we are least sure of. You can reach Ivan on LinkedIn or X with your numbers, questions, and corrections.

#10117. CSS Optimization: Is Using Declarations Just Once (UDJO) Still Effective Today?

CSS optimization is a deceptive topic. On the one hand, it feels like something simple that we should already have all the tooling for — especially when it comes to minification. On the other hand, the idea that we already exploited “everything” there is around CSS optimization may be considered naive. Such a claim looks hard to defend for a language that evolves almost daily.

In this article, I’d like to pick a technique that’s at least 18 years old — UDJO, or “using declarations just once.”

Aware of an older source? Please comment!

The technique is inspired by the principle of DRY, “don’t repeat yourself”, in that it works by identifying and reducing (if not eliminating) declaration repetition.

So, if there’s something like display: flex and it appears two or more times, it gets reconciled in one rule:

The outcome of the technique depends on how intelligently the deduplication is done and how long the selectors are. Intelligence determines if UDJO leads to an actual maintainability advantage — can the change of a declaration really be done to all the selectors attached? And the selector length influences how big file size savings are.

If you’re interested, my article on “DRY CSS” describes the mechanics in more detail. The question here is: Is UDJO still effective and still relevant today?

When we try to answer this question, however, we end up with a problem: When sites are complex and their style sheet declarations have not been de-duplicated before, UDJO can require a lot of work.

To save some time, I built tooling for this work. While it didn’t seem like it would immediately be as comprehensive as manually going through and testing style sheets, it seemed realistic enough that a tool could catch some repetition.

Skipping ahead, I created CSS Dedup not only to identify repetition, but also to deduplicate what could safely be optimized (CSS Dedup has some documentation on this but in essence, deduplication affects selector locations, which is part of how the cascade works). This, then, gives us some data:

For the first batch, I arbitrarily picked 20 websites to analyze. I then used CSS Dedup to check what the file size changes would be if we deduplicated what seemed provably safe, and also what seemed probably safe, but only if it led to file size savings.

I picked those two options to provide the “natural” result of deduplication, but also the one site owners may be most interested in to reduce repetition but also decrease transfer size. After all, UDJO as a CSS optimization technique can improve both maintainability and performance.

Important note: These values cannot be used to tell CSS quality—they depend on selector length and also on verifying if deduplication, in the respective case, actually leads to better maintainability. Also note that this leaves out changes after compression—this was not part of the experiment but would be fair to test, too.

For the question whether UDJO still works today, this should be a yes. It works as well as to even come with file size savings.

Maybe you’re curious, too, about what the situation looks like in our field, among web developers. Let’s take some samples on this, too.

It would be valuable to expand this analysis to take more samples and investigate the differences across samples, but there may be something to web professionals using shorter selectors so as to be able to benefit more from UDJO.

As mentioned at the beginning, UDJO’s effectiveness generally depends on the kind of repetition as well as on the length of selectors.

This can be managed, however: While tools like CSS Dedup make no difference between declaration use cases (and are therefore blunt), it is possible to configure them so as to reap the performance benefits.

As the data taken for this article suggest, file size savings may be achieved in most cases. Considering that this is based on safe changes, this means a low-cost performance optimization vector — especially when applied to minified output. It’s still a good idea to couch that against the use of further compression (e.g. gzip/Brotli), but at face value, deduplication shows how it can reduce the size of CSS.

For anyone generally interested in CSS optimization, maintainability, and performance, UDJO should probably be part of the overall approach to CSS. But since the technique has been niche and since tooling is new, I invite you to review, test, and explore this vector. It seems hard to argue that we have reached the ceiling of how far we can take CSS optimization.

and also to add that in the last weeks I’ve been testing some approaches to using CSS Dedup. It has proven quite effective, especially in the following setup: On static sites, reaping only savings via css-dedup CSS_FOLDER -p "(?

I’ll write more about this shortly (on my website)—and am curious about your thoughts and observations!

I’d be curious to see the minuscule performance implications, if any at all.

If up to 11% savings in a small sample are true – even pre-compression – then that’s not minuscule. Especially not with the average size of a stylesheet.

I’d like to see more sites being tested both pre and post compression.

#10114. Beyond 12 Columns: Designing Layouts With Personality

When I started designing websites thirty years ago, David Siegel had just published Creating Killer Web Sites, the book that popularised HTML tables and spacer GIFs for laying out web pages. CSS eventually gave us more control over layouts through floats and positioning. Neither was intended for page layout, and inconsistent browser behaviour could make using them frustrating. Still, that didn’t stop me and a generation of other designers from using floats and positioning.

When CSS frameworks began providing ready-made grid systems, things got a little easier. Blueprint CSS) offered a 24-column grid, while Nathan Smith’s 960 Grid System offered 12- and 16-column options. These frameworks gave designers and developers a shared structure, making layout planning and implementation considerably easier.

Twelve-column grids weren’t invented in the 2000s. Graphic designers had used column-based and modular grids for decades, and twelve is a convenient number because you don’t need to be a maths genius to divide it into halves, thirds, and quarters to create the 2-up, 3-up, and 4-up components we see everywhere online.

Frameworks like Bootstrap and Foundation followed, helping establish twelve-column grids as the de facto standard for page layouts. I get why that happened. But somewhere along the way, one layout option became the default starting point for almost everything.

CSS Grid changed what’s possible for page layouts, but I still see designers start by dividing a page into twelve equal columns, then deciding how to fit their content into them. The designs they end up with might be perfectly fine, but they can also look remarkably similar.

Still, I don’t think twelve-column grids themselves are the problem. It’s reaching for a familiar solution before considering what makes the company I’m designing for distinctive and what its website needs to communicate. So what happens when I start with those questions instead?

Before I decide how many columns a layout needs, I want to understand the company I’m designing for. What makes it different from its competitors? What does it want people to remember? Does it have a personality that could influence how its website looks and feels?

These questions matter because a layout does more than arrange content. The size, position, and relationships between elements influence how people experience a website. A restrained, orderly composition can communicate something very different from one that’s full of energy and movement. So to demonstrate what I mean, I’ll use Wheelman. It’s a fictional company I invented for my book, Standout Designs for the Web.

Pre-orders for Standout Designs for the Web are now open in the CSS-Tricks store.

Wheelman provides products and services for getaway drivers, including legal advice, robbery insurance, and an online shop selling break-in essentials. It also publishes a magazine with advice, news, and stories about life behind the wheel.

Wheelman isn’t an ordinary company, so why should its website look like an everyday collection of components? It takes its business seriously, but isn’t afraid to have fun with its design. It’s rebellious, but doesn’t want to look off-putting. Its audience is criminals, but they demand professionalism. Wheelman has plenty of personality to work with, and I want its designs to reflect that.

That doesn’t mean making Wheelman different for difference’s sake. Its customers still need to find what they’re looking for, understand what it provides, and buy its products and services. But I can make those experiences more memorable by choosing layouts that fit the company, rather than settling for familiar arrangements.

Let’s imagine I’m designing a new landing page for Wheelman’s online shop. It includes two products, one with a gigantic headline, product images, and the type of functionality and information you’ll find on most stores.

Before I decide how to arrange these elements, I want to look beyond them and consider how they relate. I ask myself questions like:

Only then do I start thinking about visual design questions: “Would a large photograph set the scene? Should I make the introduction more prominent? Can whitespace lead the eye and create spaces to pause? How can I bring images and text together into a considered composition?”

These questions help me understand what the layout needs to accomplish before I decide which grid might help me achieve it.

I could arrange everything in a single column for a straightforward reading experience. That would make responsive design considerably easier. But I won’t.

Wheelman has a rebellious personality, and I want its shop to reflect that. A more energetic layout seems appropriate, but I still need to establish a clear hierarchy between the products and their information. Two equal-width columns give both products equal prominence.

Changing their proportions creates a hierarchy, even when most elements stay the same size.

I could base those proportions on a twelve-column grid, but I won’t. Columns only divide space vertically, and I want a layout that adds another dimension.

Dividing a layout into columns is a useful starting point, but adding rows creates another dimension and opens up more creative possibilities. Where columns and rows intersect, they form rectangular or square units called modules.

Combining these modules creates larger spatial zones that can establish hierarchy, control whitespace, and add variety to a composition.

Using a modular grid, I can add drama, establish hierarchy by varying scale, and make the composition feel far less predictable. Starting with four columns and four rows gives me sixteen modules to work with, but I don’t need to fill them all.

I can create drama by spreading the large graphic headline and product image across four modules, while the product information and shopping tools occupy one module each.

The modular structure becomes even more apparent in the grid of alternative products. Most occupy just one module, while others fill two, either horizontally or vertically. Varying their sizes gives some products more prominence and makes the overall arrangement feel less predictable.

The result still feels like a product page, with a clear reading order and space for the content to breathe. But it also feels unmistakably like something Wheelman would publish.

I don’t expect this composition to work at every screen size, so I can adjust the size and quantity of modules to improve the reading experience.

And if one grid offers this much flexibility, imagine what combining two or more grids can do.

One thing I want from Wheelman’s website is variety. Its magazine, shop, and services serve different purposes, and I don’t want every page to feel like I’ve poured its content into the same template.

Wheelman needs layouts which feel organised without being predictable. Compound grids give me the structure I need while leaving room for less conventional arrangements.

A compound grid combines two or more grids of any type — column, modular, or hierarchical — on the same page. These grids can overlap, sit side by side, or be stacked one on top of the other. Combining grids creates possibilities which neither grid offers on its own.

I can start by overlaying a two-column grid with one containing three columns. Their combined grid lines create four columns of two different widths, giving me more flexibility without losing the underlying structure.

Those proportions offer more possibilities for arranging content. I might use a wider area for a photograph or headline, then place a pullquote into one of the narrower spaces. Another page could use the same underlying grid but combine its spaces differently to suit another story.

Combining grids with different column counts gets even more interesting as those numbers increase. In Standout Designs for the Web, I explore several combinations, including mixing four- and five-column grids to create different compositions for Wheelman’s pages while maintaining a connected visual structure.

The point isn’t to make every page look different for its own sake. It’s to give each section a layout that suits its content while keeping them all feeling like part of the same company.

Twelve-column grids are useful, and I’m certainly not suggesting you should stop using them entirely. But they’re only one option among many, and starting every design with the same structure limits your possibilities before you’ve even considered the story your design is telling.

There’s also nothing inherently wrong with the 2-up, 3-up, and 4-up patterns a twelve-column grid inspires. They can sometimes be exactly what’s needed. But reaching for them automatically can leave little room for the character and personality that make websites memorable.

Wheelman gives me plenty of reasons to choose different layouts. Its stories, products, and services don’t all need the same arrangements, and its personality gives me opportunities to make those arrangements more distinctive. The grids are simply tools that help me put those ideas into practice.

And this applies to much more than layout. The same thinking should influence the colours I choose, the typography I use, the images I commission, and the way I bring those elements together.

That’s why I wrote Standout Designs for the Web. I want to encourage designers to look beyond familiar solutions and make more deliberate decisions about colour, typography, imagery, and layout. Not to be different for difference’s sake, but to create websites that look and feel like they belong to the companies behind them. And no one else.

Pre-orders for Standout Designs for the Web are open! Everyone who pre-orders will also receive the Hardboiled Client Interview Guide for free — a practical companion to help designers ask better questions and discover what makes a company distinctive.

The book will be published on November 20, priced at $14.99 for the DRM-free EPUB and PDF editions.

Andy Clarke shows you how to create designs that people recognize and remember in his brand-new e-book, Standout Designs for the Web. Order it today!

#10113. How to Add Idempotency Keys to a Node.js API with PostgreSQL

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A mobile app sends POST /orders. The server creates the order, but the response is lost when the phone switches from Wi-Fi to 4G. The app sees a timeout and retries. Now the customer has two orders, and your support team has a refund to process.

Retrying is the right thing for a client to do. The problem is that POST is not idempotent: RFC 9110 defines GET, PUT and DELETE as idempotent methods, but not POST or PATCH. Repeating a POST can repeat its side effects.

An idempotency key fixes this. The client generates a unique key for each logical operation and sends it with every attempt. The server does the work once, stores the response against the key, and returns the stored response for any retry. The IETF HTTPAPI working group is standardizing this pattern as the Idempotency-Key HTTP header field, and this tutorial follows its recommendations.

We'll build it with Node.js, Express 5 and PostgreSQL. By the end you'll have a table that stores keys, a function that safely claims a key under concurrent requests, an Express route that replays stored responses, and a client that retries without creating duplicates.

The client's job is simple: generate a key, usually a UUID, and send the same key on every retry of the same operation. The draft defines the header value as a quoted string:

The server's job is harder, because every request falls into one of five cases:

To tell case 3 from case 4, the server stores a fingerprint of the request alongside the key. A matching fingerprint means a genuine retry; a different one means key reuse. Case 5 is the one most implementations get wrong, because it only shows up under concurrency. A database constraint handles it for us.

Create a project and install the two dependencies:

The key table records who sent the key, the request fingerprint, whether the work has finished, and the response to replay:

Two details matter here. The primary key includes client_id, so keys are scoped per client: two customers who happen to generate the same value can't see each other's responses. And locked_until lets the server recover a key whose request crashed halfway, which we'll use in Step 3.

The draft defines the header value as a structured field string, which means it arrives wrapped in double quotes. Many clients send it without quotes, so the parser below accepts both and limits the characters and length it will store:

The fingerprint has to be identical for identical requests, even if a client serializes the JSON body with its properties in a different order. Sorting object keys before hashing takes care of that. The hash uses SHA-256 from Node's built-in crypto module:

Including the method and path means a key reused on a different endpoint counts as a mismatch, not as a retry.

The tempting approach is to SELECT the key, and insert it if nothing comes back. Under concurrency that fails: two retries arriving a few milliseconds apart both see no row, and both do the work.

Instead, let the primary key decide. INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING either inserts the row or does nothing, atomically, even across many application processes. The PostgreSQL INSERT documentation describes this behavior in detail. If the insert returns a row, this request owns the key. If not, we read the existing row and decide which case we're in:

The UPDATE in the last step is also atomic. If two retries find the same expired lock, only one of them matches the locked_until < now() condition and gets the key. If the row disappears between the two queries, for example because it was released after a failure, the function reports in_progress and the client simply retries.

Set LOCK_SECONDS comfortably above the slowest time your operation takes. If the lock expires while the original request is still running, a retry can take over and run the work a second time.

Once a request owns the key, it creates the order and marks the key as completed. Both writes happen in one transaction, so there is no moment where the order exists but the key still says in_progress, or the other way round. Note that a transaction in node-postgres has to use a single client checked out from the pool, as the node-postgres transaction documentation explains:

releaseKey runs when the work fails. Deleting the claim lets the client retry with the same key instead of waiting for the lock to expire. It only deletes rows that are still in progress, so a completed response is never thrown away.

The route validates the body first. A request with invalid input never touches the key table, so the client can fix the input and resend with the same key. Error responses use the application/problem+json format from RFC 9457, which the idempotency draft also uses in its examples:

The server side only works if the client keeps the key stable. The most common client bug is generating a new key inside the retry loop, which turns every retry into a new operation. Generate the key once per logical action, before the first attempt:

The catch branch is the reason idempotency keys exist. After a timeout, the client can't know whether the order was created. With a stable key, retrying is safe either way: if the first attempt succeeded, the retry returns the stored order; if it didn't, the retry creates it. The random jitter spreads retries out, so many clients recovering from the same outage don't all hit the server at once.

In a browser or mobile app, store the key with the pending action, for example in the form state or a local queue. If the user taps "Place order" again after an error, reuse it. Only generate a new key when the user starts a genuinely new action.

Keys can't live forever, or the table grows without limit. The draft leaves the retention period to the server but says it should be published in the API documentation, so clients know how long a retry stays safe. Twenty-four hours covers most retry scenarios. A scheduled job can delete older rows in batches:

Run it every few minutes from cron or your job scheduler until it deletes zero rows. Deleting in batches keeps each transaction short, and the created_at index from Step 1 keeps the inner query fast. A retry that arrives after its key has expired is treated as a new request, which is why the retention period belongs in your documentation.

Start the server with node server.js, then send the same request twice:

The second request lists the properties in a different order, and it's still recognized as a retry because of the canonical fingerprint. Both responses carry the same order ID, and the orders table contains one row.

Now change the quantity while keeping the key. The server answers 422, because the key now belongs to a different request:

To see the concurrent case, add await new Promise((r) => setTimeout(r, 3000)); before completeKey in the route, restart the server, and fire two requests with a new key at the same time:

One request prints 201 and the other 409. Without the ON CONFLICT claim, both would have printed 201 and created two orders. Remove the delay when you're done.

This design guarantees that the database work in Step 4 happens once per key. Two situations need extra care:

Idempotency keys don't replace other safeguards either. A unique constraint on a natural business key, such as one payment per invoice, still catches duplicates that arrive with different keys, for example when a user submits the same form from two tabs.

With one table, an atomic claim and a transaction that stores the response next to the work, POST endpoints become safe to retry. Clients get a simple rule: generate one key per action, reuse it on every retry. The server takes care of the rest, including the concurrent retries that a SELECT-then-INSERT approach misses.

The same pattern works for PATCH endpoints and for webhooks you send to other systems. Start with the endpoints where a duplicate costs real money or confuses a customer, and publish your key format and retention period in the API documentation so client developers know what to send.

#10109. 10 Big Ol’ 2027 Black Friday Deals

Black Friday deals usually make me think of a big ol’ crowd of people trampling over one another for a TV in a Walmart parking lot. But turns out there’s a lot of deals for front-end too, specifically learning opportunities from course publishers. Thought I’d share those because (1) learning is still straight-up essential for what we do, (2) these really are the best courses in the business, and (3) it’s just plain good for all of us to support the huge effort these folks put into their work.

#10106. Staggering Scroll-Driven Animations with animation-range-start

The other day on the CSS-Tricks Slack channel, Danny Schwartz shared an interesting recent Master.dev article by Ashutosh Biswas on how to stagger CSS scrolling animations using a clever combo of several emergent CSS features and a lot of math, which Ashutosh admits is “a huge formula and can look intimidating.”

When Danny Slacked the link for the Frontend Masters article, he described it as “CSS on drugs.” Speaking of drugs, I am on heavy prescription painkillers while recovering from a serious injury, so that might not help my current comprehension skills. Nevertheless, after reading Ashutosh’s article a few times, here’s a meme showing how I’d feel using such an elaborate algorithm for something as mundane as staggering the animations of some characters as they scroll into view.

Don’t get me wrong. I know the complexity is because Ashutosh is going for a generic solution in which we can connect a “progress value to an animation, scroll progress, or any other input to drive the staggering.”

In fact, I can dream up a scenario in which this might be what we want: Imagine a CSS karaoke machine that displays a ball bouncing on text that incrementally highlights, based on the user’s progress through the vocals measured via speech recognition. Running that kind of animation “not as a collection of independently timed objects, but as a single predictable, holistic effect driven by one progress value” might be exactly what the doctor ordered. This would be true because the staggered animations could be interrelated, and the overall animation would be driven by a custom input that’s not built into the browser.

But unlike this hypothetical scenario, replacing the passage of time with scroll progress to control animations is already built into modern CSS. I contend that we’d need a better reason to roll our own implementation than the examples Ashutosh gives.

Ashutosh showcases a scroll-driven use case as “one of those ideal scenarios where the stagger formula shines.” But a red flag that we’re in danger of reinventing something we can get for free is that the spec for scroll-driven animations is cognizant that if we opt to base an animation on scroll progress rather than on time, then, as Ashutosh says, “the animation we are going to achieve is different from traditional ones because we don’t include time in our considerations.” This is no surprise to the creators of scroll timeline, and the MDN docs make Ashutosh’s point in different words when introducing scroll timeline.

The docs also point out that animation-delay is only meaningful when we are using the default time-based document timeline. Therefore, animation-delay does not affect scroll-driven animations. But this is why CSS introduced animation-range-start, which allows us to delay a scroll-driven animation relative to scroll progress and not time.

So, as esoteric and trippy as the math-heavy approach appears, that could be because we are doing something that should be simple in an unusual way.

Let’s see if I’m right by rewriting Ashutosh’s demo to remove reliance on custom CSS functions and use animation-range-start instead.

Ashutosh’s rework of Frontend Masters’ animated tally of donations in the site footer is a nifty, subtle use of scrollytelling that makes the user feel invested in revealing that number.

Sidenote: The only qualm I have about this use of scroll-driven animations for the donation tally is that it might be too subtle — if the user scrolls too quickly, the animated entrance of each digit might occur too quickly to notice. Therefore, the donation tally could be a good case study for comparing the merits of scroll-driven versus scroll-triggered animations as design options. However, that story will have to wait for another day, as the focus of this article is only to show that the staggered scrolling CSS animations can be greatly simplified.

In the video below, my eight-year-old narrates, as a reminder that simplicity is key to the web we’re choosing to leave to the next generation. He compares the complex math in the original demo source code with my animation-range-start fork. He shows the two versions working side by side, demonstrating that sometimes the only thing we lose by judiciously deleting complex code is a headache.

And for those using a Chromium-based browser, my fork of the Pen is below. You can peruse the CSS to see how it does away with the grand unified theory of staggered scrolling animations, and instead deals only with the problem at hand. Since I preserved the original functionality, you’ll need to scroll gradually to appreciate the staggered animations.

I once tackled a related problem with a mathematical formula in a previous article, which touched on how we can use sibling-index() to “increment animation-range-start to stagger the delay before characters fade in as the user scrolls.”

The truth is, if we refuse to fall into the coding trap of possibly abstracting the wrong thing, we can use one line of CSS to replace a formula that took 1.3k words in the Frontend Masters article for me to still not fully grasp:

The 2.5% increment for each sibling is because there are eight characters to stagger, and I wanted to choreograph the animations such that they are delayed in evenly-spaced increments till the last character makes its animated entrance when it reaches 30% of the containment range (10% + 20%).

It was inadvertently apt to describe the original post as “CSS on drugs.” Once you reinvent one built-in CSS feature, you can’t stop there. You find yourself having to recreate more stuff that CSS should be able to do by itself. Sure enough, even after we get through the custom formulas that make scroll timelines do what they already do, Ashutosh announces, “Unfortunately, we can’t use easing keywords or cubic-bezier() or linear() functions here. We are left on our own to create easing functions.”

We shouldn’t have to because, despite the word “timing” in the name of the animation-timing-function property, MDN is clear that “animation-timing-function has the same effect when creating CSS scroll-driven animations as it does for regular time-based animations.” I know from experience that scroll-driven animations work with animation-timing-function because I used scroll-driven animations with steps() to animate a sprite climbing a ladder in my CSS scrollytelling game.

The problem in the land of CSS on drugs is that after merging all the staggered animations into one, the easing applies to the entire master timeline rather than to each element. It’s not a flaw in CSS, but a logical consequence of the “holistic effect driven by one progress value.” Be careful what you wish for.

To be fair, over the years I have seen impressive results from developers reinventing things the browsers already do, usually because the reinvention gives us control we couldn’t obtain otherwise.

I mention this to show that reinventing something like scroll timelines isn’t an inherently bad idea if we know why we’re doing it. After all, scroll timelines are only a reinvention of what developers were doing for decades prior in JavaScript. Having said that, I believe my rewrite of the staggered scroll-driven tally demo shows that the original approach was overkill for that example. Do let me know in the comments if you see any advantage to the custom CSS functions version over using animation-range-start.

And yet the thought that stuck with me about the Frontend Masters post is that it opens the door to linking the progress of a CSS animation to any arbitrary input value that could be used in a conceptually similar way to scroll timelines.

This really appeals to me, because even when I first started writing about scroll timelines in 2024, I mentioned that “I would love to see other input controls that can manipulate animation timelines. Imagine text fields progressing animations as you fill them out.” Or how about an animation driven by how closely the new password you are typing matches a password scheme?

Sidenote: Adam Argyle’s Prop For That library, which Geoff recently wrote up, makes it more convenient than ever to expose validation information about a field via CSS custom properties.

I can add my hypothetical karaoke machine scenario to the wishlist of custom progress inputs for controlling animations. How about an animation driven by the user’s progress through a quiz or a shopping cart?

Custom animation timelines would open the door to all sorts of engaging user experiences—but the code Ashutosh has shared may be the closest we are likely to get to driving CSS animations using values other than time or scroll progress.

Let me know in the comments if you can think of other types of progress inputs that could drive CSS animations to create immersive, interactive experiences.

CSS-Tricks editor Geoff Graham sits down with author Lee Meyer to demo Google’s Prompt API and weigh it (good and bad) place in front-end development.

Andy Clarke shows you how to create designs that people recognize and remember in his brand-new e-book, Standout Designs for the Web. Order it today!

Staggering with animation-range-start is neat because each element can reveal on its own scroll offset without JavaScript or IntersectionObserver setup. One thing I’d consider is wrapping it in prefers-reduced-motion so sensitive users aren’t hit with moving content. Did you test fallbacks for browsers lacking support?

You are right, George, in a real production version it should definitely check for both prefers reduced motion and whether view timeline is supported. For what it’s worth this stuff is newly baseline since Firefox finally supports it. The other browsers have supported it for a while

There is a polyfill for older browsers https://github.com/flackr/scroll-timeline which does work quite well from my testing, although it can’t provide the performance improvements of native scroll timeline so it can get janky and it has some other limitations such as timeline scope not working the same

It is still better than nothing and uses the native version where possible

#10104. Securing Your Agents: A Hands-On Guide to Agent Anomaly Detection

As highlighted in our announcement post, we said that each new model generation lets agents do more on their own. However, the more an agent can reach, the more one bad decision can cost. You've probably seen a few of these in the news lately:

Because these events produced no system crashes or logging errors, the teams behind these agents had no way to know until the damage occurred.

Agent Anomaly Detection on the Gemini Platform watches for exactly these issues. It reads the traces and logs your agents already produce and flags sessions where an agent goes outside the job it was built for.

Before you point it at your production agents, you'll probably want to see it work in a test environment. In this hands-on guide, we’ll walk you step-by-step through setting up Agent Anomaly Detection and using it to capture your very first agent behavior anomaly. By the end of this post, you'll have:

To get started, open the companion notebook Detect anomalous agent behavior with Agent Anomaly Detection in a separate tab (available on Google Colab or GitHub). As you read through this guide, it will prompt you when to execute notebook cells or interact directly with the Google Cloud console.

If a step can be done in the UI console, this post shows that path, with screenshots. The notebook does the same thing through the Agent Anomaly Detection API, so by the end you'll also have the code to automate enablement and pull findings into your own tooling.

2. A place to run the notebook: Google Colab, Colab Enterprise, or a local Jupyter environment. The notebook installs the latest ADK for Python itself. Agent Anomaly Detection is compatible with agents running ADK 1.2+, though ADK 2.0+ is preferred for best results.

3. A Cloud Logging bucket and an Observability dataset in either US or EU multi-region. (Both resources need to be in the same multi-region)

4. In the notebook: run the Get started cells. They install the packages, authenticate you, take your project ID, and turn on the four APIs that Agent Anomaly Detection needs.

First, let’s create a Customer Support Agent called “anomaly-detection-refund-demo”. Our example agent is responsible for helping customers check their order history and request refunds. However, it has a deliberate flaw: the agent has access to an overprivileged fetch_records tool which can read any table, including a `payroll` table containing sensitive employee data. All three parts of the lethal trifecta are here: the agent can read private data, it takes input from people it can't vet, and it sends what it finds back in its reply. Tool permissions and guardrails should stop most of this, but they only block what someone thought to write down. Agent Anomaly Detection flags the sessions that get past them, usually within a few minutes.

The notebook has the full agent. The part that matters is the tool, which never checks the table name against an allowlist:

All data is canned and in memory, so nothing real is read or refunded. This tool is intended to be over-permissive to illustrate AAD functionality. Don't copy this tool into production.

The Step 1 cells deploy the agent to Agent Runtime. Two details in the deploy cell are what make the agent visible to Agent Anomaly Detection. First, it sets three environment variables on the deployment, which turn on telemetry and write prompt and response content to Cloud Logging, where detection looks for it:

Second, it sends the agent one test message right after deploying. Agent Anomaly Detection discovers agents through their telemetry, so an agent that has never served a request does not appear under Monitored agents.

The deployment takes about 5 to 10 minutes and prints the agent's resource name and the reply to the test message:

For more information on how to create and deploy an agent, take a look at these guides: Creating an Agent and Deploying an Agent.

In the console: The sample code we provide already enables logging and telemetry when deploying the agent. Verify this by navigating to the Agent Deployments page (Gemini Platform > Agents > Deployments) and clicking “Configure” next to the recently created anomaly-detection-refund-demo agent to open the Observability Service Configuration.

Great! You now have a working agent that is successfully configured and ready to be monitored by Agent Anomaly Detection.

Now let’s set up Agent Anomaly Detection to monitor your agent’s traffic.

In the console: navigate to the Agent Platform > Agents > Audit page and click “Enable”.

Choose your agent’s Logging Bucket and Observability Dataset, making sure they are in the same region (us-multi-region or eu-multi-region) enable “Automatically monitor new agents”, then click “Agree and continue” and Agent Anomaly Detection will be set up in your project.

If you'd rather do this in code, the first Step 2 cell in the notebook creates the same configuration through the API, using the default _Default bucket and _Trace dataset with automatic monitoring on.

Setup takes around 30 minutes. Once it’s done loading you’re ready to proceed to the next step! Because you enabled automatic monitoring and the agent already served a request in Step 1, setup picks it up right away. Check that it shows as Active under Settings > Monitored agents before you continue; sessions that start earlier aren't analyzed.

Now that the agent is being monitored, let's see Agent Anomaly Detection in action! The Step 3 cells send one normal conversation and one anomalous conversation, each in its own session. The normal conversation is a customer listing their orders and getting a refund for a broken lamp. The anomalous conversation simulates a user who finds the fetch_records flaw and uses it to pull payroll data:

Notice that the user brings almost nothing: a customer ID and one first name. Asking for a table that doesn't exist makes the agent reveal the existing tables, which is how they learn there is a payroll table within reach.

The notebook prints both transcripts with the agent's tool calls, and the session IDs you'll look up next.

Anomaly Detection will scan the sessions within 5 minutes. In the landing page, you should see that a couple sessions have been processed and one anomalous session detected.

Let’s take a look at the anomalous session in more detail. Click the session id to open the detail view or paste the anomalous session id into the search bar.

In our run, the top finding was Tool misuse at High severity, with 95% probability. Your detectors and severities may differ, because the models behind the analysis are non-deterministic. The recommended solution is to restrict the `fetch_records` tool to read from specific tables.

Click an invocation in the Session pane to see its analysis in Details. Each invocation is Medium on its own, but together they make the session High.

To stay informed without watching the console, connect Agent Anomaly Detection to your existing alerting workflows.

In Cloud Monitoring: Agent Anomaly Detection publishes two metrics: Detected events, which counts anomalies by detector and severity, and Monitored agents, which tracks your detection coverage. To view these metrics, click the arrow next to "View in Cloud Monitoring" on the Anomaly detection page and select a metric to open it in Metrics Explorer.

To be notified of new anomalies, go to Monitoring > Alerting, click "Create policy", and select the Detected events metric. Leave the filters empty so the alert covers every anomaly, including the demo's, then set the rolling window to 5 minutes with the delta function, and set the threshold to above 0. Once you know what's normal for your agents, add a severity filter (for example High or Critical) so you're only alerted on the findings that matter. A metric-absence policy on Monitored agents will also alert you if monitoring stops.

In Security Command Center: Each anomaly is also published as an AGENT_SESSION_ANOMALY finding in the Security Command Center if it is enabled for your organization. Click "View in Security Command Center" to triage these findings alongside your other threats, use "Set security marks" to tag and track them, and set up notifications for high-severity findings.

You’ve now seen Agent Anomaly Detection in action: it successfully flagged the session where our agent went off-script to read payroll records, while evaluating the benign session, and correctly not flagging an anomaly. The finding told you why it was flagged and how to fix the underlying issue, and alerts in Cloud Monitoring and Security Command Center ensure you are notified of future anomalies.

When you're ready to secure your own production workloads, follow this rollout plan:

To review the complete list of prerequisites or to request Private Preview access for your project, head over to the Agent Anomaly Detection documentation.
