#10102. How SD Card Photo and Video Recovery Works: A Practical Guide With a JPEG Carving Example

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

For photographers, videographers and content creators, an SD card is more than a storage device. It holds the raw material behind a finished project: wedding photographs, travel footage, product demos and drone recordings that may be impossible to recreate. Data loss can happen at almost any stage of that workflow, from a folder deleted to make space to a card formatted before the footage was backed up.

This article explains why files on a card become unavailable, what to do in the first minutes, how recovery tools find data, and how to compare options. It includes a small Python example that recovers JPEG files from a disk image, so you can see the mechanism for yourself. It also compares free open-source tools with paid graphical ones, using criteria you can apply yourself. Recovery is never guaranteed, and tool features change, so confirm details against each vendor’s current documentation.

Recovery is not just a matter of finding a deleted filename. Several different problems can make files unavailable, and they call for different approaches.

These steps cannot guarantee recovery, but they reduce avoidable risk.

Most tools combine two methods, and understanding them helps you read scan results.

Carving has a cost. Because file system information is ignored, names and folder structure are usually lost, which is why scan results often show generic names. Fragmented files can also be recovered incomplete, since the tool assumes the pieces are stored one after another. Free tools build on these same ideas. Wondershare Recoverit V15, which also offers a free version for Windows and Mac, adds a graphical interface, while PhotoRec and ddrescue stay closer to the command line.

The script below is a teaching sketch, not a recovery product. It reads a raw disk image, finds JPEG start markers, and writes out the bytes up to the last end marker before the next start. That detail matters because a JPEG can contain an embedded thumbnail with its own markers, so stopping at the first end marker would cut a file short.

A short test builds two real JPEGs, hides them in random bytes, and checks that both are carved and open as valid images:

Use it on an image rather than the card itself. On Linux you can make one with a read-only copy, for example with the GNU ddrescue tool, which is designed to copy data from failing media. Then run the carver against the image and save the output to a different drive. Real tools handle many more formats, trim files using header information, and deal with video containers, so treat this as a way to understand the idea.

The right choice depends on the problem, your platform and how comfortable you are with the command line. The table summarizes what each project’s own documentation states.

The order of steps matters more than the choice of tool. A cautious workflow on Linux or macOS looks like this:

Graphical tools follow the same logic and mainly change step 3, with thumbnails, filters and search that make large result sets easier to browse. Whether that is worth paying for depends on how many files you need to sift through and how comfortable you are on the command line. When comparing any tool, ask:

Features such as search by capture time, folder or AI-based tagging depend on metadata surviving the loss, and they narrow results without proving a file is intact. Treat them as convenience, not verification.

Recovering a video file does not necessarily mean recovering a playable video. A recording interrupted during writing can lack the structural information a player needs, and other files may be damaged in ways that block playback.

The distinction matters. Recovery retrieves missing or inaccessible data, while repair tries to fix damage that affects a file’s usability. Some cases need one, the other or both. Repair has limits too: if essential video data was overwritten or never recorded, software cannot recreate the original footage in full. After any recovery or repair, play the file all the way through and compare it with what you expect.

For a quick check of a recovered clip, ffprobe can show whether the container is readable:

An error or a missing duration suggests the file needs repair or is incomplete.

Often yes, if nothing has overwritten them. Stop using the card immediately and scan it or an image of it.

Not before recovery. Formatting changes file system structures and may make recovery harder.

Recovery retrieves missing data. Repair fixes a file that exists but will not open or play correctly.

Not always. PhotoRec and TestDisk are free and open source, while paid tools add a graphical interface and extras such as previews, filtering and video repair.

On a different drive from the card. Saving to the same card can overwrite data you still want.

SD card data loss creates two related challenges: getting files back, and working out which recovered files are useful. Stopping use of the card, working on an image and understanding the difference between file system recovery, carving and repair put you in a better position, whichever tool you choose. Graphical tools aim to make large result sets easier to navigate, and free tools such as PhotoRec and TestDisk remain solid options. Try your chosen tool on a copy first, verify what you recover, and keep a backup so you do not need to do this again.

#10101. When Everything Has Changed, What Do We Do Next?

It’s been a… year, huh? I think the most common emoji I’ve seen this year is “Face with Bags Under Eyes” (🫩) and that says a lot. One thing is for sure: the industry we make a living in is in flux, regardless of the outcomes of the AI bubble. Let’s take a look at how we can try and navigate that situation as best we can. But first…

Technology hyperscalers like Microsoft and Meta, along with AI startups like Open AI and Anthropic have transformed everything about tech and the job we do. This is true regardless of if they fail or somehow succeed in their quest to have everyone paying a subscription fee for their Large Language Model (LLM) technology, which is marketed to us as AI. I’ll refer to this technology as AI for the rest of this article for simplicity.

It’s not just tech jobs that have transformed either. Everywhere you go, you will see some slop, somewhere. Be it a flyer, website, a village poster or a food truck menu. Heck, people are even using LLMs to work out what’s special about their own children. The effects of this technology are far-reaching, way beyond our own industry.

The technology has hit us all in tech especially hard though. Mid-last year, I did some investigating because I suspected that so-called AI mandates were becoming increasingly popular and what I learned from conversations then feels like some sort of utopia compared to now. In the space between that piece being published and now, we went from token maxxing to companies realizing that actually, this technology is extremely expensive and not returning the promised outcomes.

The mandates have cooled, sure, but the demands to use AI to produce more volume, regardless of whether that volume is actually needed or not are still very much there. I’m in a fortunate position to have friendships with people all across the industry, from tech hyperscalers, to tiny little studios, and the stories I have heard from their work life are nothing short of horrifying. No one really seems to be happy with their work either.

This is such a long way away from even a handful of years ago where yes, there was a lot of problematic culture and outcomes from the tech industry, but people were generally quite happy with the work they were doing at least.

The sudden urge to sprint towards AI technologies over the last few years has had a devastating effect in so many places. The technology has encouraged suicide, devastated communities with the data center build-out and of course, displaced thousands of people’s careers. It’s worth noting that I’ve mentioned only a tiny sample of the harms there. If I listed them all, this article would be unbearably long and depressing.

Some harms I do want to touch on though are the effects on our communities in tech. The community spirit of open source and open knowledge was the foundation of the industry’s — especially the web industry’s — rapid success. At the root of everything there was people happy to share knowledge and build everyone up with them. Those people still do exist — myself included — but being one of those people is increasingly difficult.

As the numbers go up in the AI world, the numbers very much go down for independent educators, publishers and community conferences. There’s a developing and worrying culture of “I don’t need to skill up now because I can use AI for work”, so allow me to break down why that reductive view of your work and your career is harmful in the long term.

And don’t ever forget that! Let’s have a think for a second about what actually makes you a good developer, because typing characters in a text editor certainly isn’t it. What makes us good is experience, knowledge, nuance and above all else, creativity.

Look, I get it, AI really can feel like it’s a miracle machine, but it’s important to take a wider view. Along with the published studies on cognitive decline and the widening skills gap, it’s more important than ever to invest in your brain to keep your skills sharp because you’re not getting that repetitive reinforcement that writing code every day gives you anymore if AI tools do everything for you. There’s a reason that I often build most of my teaching around a linear build-along because the tangible nature of repeating patterns you’ve learned really helps that knowledge to sink in.

As always, this article is addressed to everyone, but I want to talk directly to founders (like me), decision makers, and managers for a second. A big part of our job, in fact, the most important part, is to ensure that our workers are looked after. I’m not just talking about compensation and perks here. I’m talking about our responsibility to ensure that our workers’ skills are kept sharp, their professional curiosities are satisfied and above all else that they are happy in the work they are doing. Our workers are much more than “number go up” vehicles.

You should be giving your developers more time to learn, rather than chasing endless volume. If AI enthusiasts are to be believed, there’s hours upon hours of time efficiencies created by this technology. Don’t fill that time with more volume. Instead, invest that time in developing your worker’s skills. It’s going to avoid burnout which yep, daily LLM use will likely cause in the long term.

One thing that is increasingly clear is that the health of the AI industry is not good. The bubble has already effectively burst in China and more worryingly for economy enjoyers, the “picks and shovels” — AKA the chips industry in Korea — has seen dramatic downturns. This is not how an “inevitable” outcome is supposed to happen!

The U.S. AI industry is on very shaky ground too. Circular financing, unbelievable levels of debt and a data center build out that simply cannot keep up with the required power levels to turn profits for hyperscalers and startups, mixed with a genuine hatred of the technology by the general public, creates enough risk in itself. Recent events, such as the Democrats dominating the midterms causing AI stocks to drop and as well as Nvidia’s stock because of its CEO being caught up in a spying scandal shine a light on the fragility of the industry. A healthy industry doesn’t work like this.

With all that in mind, what would be a really silly thing to do? Yeh, you guessed it: put AI at the heart of everything in your business. Sure, there’s open source models — which I think is the logical destination of the technology — but how many companies are going to be willing to maintain their infrastructure needs? Not many. The low barrier of entry of commercial AI technology is the selling point, let’s be honest.

Above everything, people need to be invested in, not pushed aside by technology like AI. As I mentioned earlier, it’s experience, knowledge, nuance, and creativity that sets humans far apart from AI. That technology does indeed keep evolving, but even the most ardent of AI supporters will concede that the rate of frontier model improvement is very much levelling out.

There’s never been a more important time to keep human skills sharp, so here’s some ideas on how to do that.

This is not just directed at individuals but also at founders, decision makers and managers. Our industry has been historically excellent at investing in skilling people up and we need to bring that back.

Give staff the time and budget to learn, to develop their skills and to engage with the community at conferences. You already know the positive outcomes that brings, so bring it back to your organization. At the absolute worst case, it’ll make the usage of AI in your organization more efficient and when the token subsidies inevitably end, more cost effective.

Again, token subsidies will inevitably end. So, getting thousands of dollars of worth from a $200 a month subscription will be something you’ll look fondly back on. When hallucinations from AI tools are suddenly costing thousands, does it make sense to force your staff to produce as much volume as possible? Of course not.

My advice is to re-evaluate those prior decisions and invest saved costs back into developing your workers instead, while there’s still a hope of them not burning out completely. Make sure you include your workers in these decisions and discuss the impacts of mandates on them. It’s important for you to understand the impact of these sort of decisions.

This advice is more aimed at developers, but stay paying attention, managers. As I said earlier, repetition (especially typing) reinforces new learning and existing knowledge. The slower pace also allows your brain to think, which from a quality of output perspective, is imperative. Doing none of that atrophies skills over time.

I recently read Jenn Schiffer’s great post. She did the Weird Web October challenge and wrote this:

[…] i encourage anyone who uses ai and/or vibe codes to consider doing an exercise like this without those tools as a way to challenge your brain and you can continue doing your regular work the way you do your regular work

I think that’s a very good idea, even if it’s messing around with a little side project — if you’ve got the energy from Producing Value all day, of course. Guess what bosses: this is another way you can make your staff happier, by giving them time to do this on the clock.

At the end of the day, you’ll miss the soul when it’s gone. It’s not gone yet though. Far from it. Independents like CSS-Tricks, my publication, Piccalilli, and Smashing Magazine are trying hard to keep the soul of our industry alive by sharing high quality educational content with the community.

Most of this content is free at the point of entry, but it certainly isn’t free to produce. We all pay writers fairly to share their knowledge with the community and we are all tiny organizations. We’re certainly not in this game to make huge profits, that’s for sure.

All of us could do more though, but funding is the ultimate barrier. For both companies and individuals, purchase courses, join the membership programs, and purchase ad space. With the right financial support, publications like ours can be a real force in pushing the good stuff back into our industry and bringing everyone up with us. That’s what we do it for.

Some course producers I recommend are Miriam, Sara, Mat, Scott, Brad and Ian, Josh, Ahmad, Christine, Vitaly, Ben and of course, myself! All of these will have generous Black Friday deals, so go ahead and ask your boss to invest in you.

Andy Clarke shows you how to create designs that people recognize and remember in his brand-new e-book, Standout Designs for the Web. Order it today!

It is a tough state of affairs, but if nothing else, at the end of the day we’ve got one of the best and most resilient communities around. Front-end is built on knowledge sharing, and as long as we hold each other up and strive for the future we want to see, we’re in it with a fighting chance. It has been hard, and it’ll continue to be hard, but the challenge is all the more reason to rise to the occasion and persevere.

I seem to be one of those ‘unique’ developers who has managed to avoid using AI entirely so far. I work for a conservation not-for-profit as the only IT guy (there’s only 5 of us in total). We regularly work with legally sensitive information (protected species records) and early on I had a long discussion with my manager about the dangers to information security of using AI, as well as the various ethical and environmental costs of the technology. My viewpoint is that AI is a tool and, like any other, the decision to use it needs to be made after carefully considering the pros and cons. At the moment, the cons seem to massively outweigh the pros and I see no sign of that changing in the near future; though I may well be wrong – I’m not an expert on machine learning after all.

Despite being ‘lucky’ in regards to management supporting my decision to abstain from AI use, I’m still not immune to the depression and burnout that this new technology has brought. I spend 50% more of my time patching websites than I did 2 years ago, and trying to find packages to use in projects is far more time consuming that it used to be due to all of the noise created by slop. Frankly, it’s sucked all the joy out of my work, and I’m just on the periphery.

I think your emphasis on the dangers of not learning anymore is a serious hazard. I started writing a book on Building Websites for Beginners some years ago, but have since neglected it due to health issues and house moves etc. Maybe taking up writing again is a way to alleviate the sense of hopelessness we’re all feeling?

This really resonated — especially the “build stuff by hand” part. I run a small tool site that I built by hand, and doing it the slow way is exactly how I learned how typography and fonts actually work. AI tools are fast, but they never teach you that. The point about supporting independents hit home too — small sites like ours are the ones keeping the web human. Thanks for saying it out loud.

Great article, your viewpoint on AI always has me hooked. I feel like you’re being slowly abandoned by your peers within the community, whether they want to or not. Look up any of the (my own opinion, granted) core community sites and they’re infested with AI content, articles and functionality. A lot of the leading voices and companies in the front-end dev community, who were once idealistic and morally guided, work for some of those companies you mention and are now mouthpieces for the tech. Or a peddling the tech themselves.

Just like the AI industry, I’m not sure what the future looks like for the web community. But it’s encouraging to see sites like this come back and continue to inform and educate.

It’s the reason why I brought my indie newsletter WDRL back to life this week. It’s important that we play with new technology on our own, that we explore the details, the hacks, the pitfalls of web development and share them on blogs. If we solely(!) rely on AI for everything, that’s going to backfire soon. Let’s revive the old community style!

I was SO happy to see your newsletter return!

Amazing and well written article. I agree with all the points you said, specially the burnout one. As a webdesigner I feel the pressure to deliver as fast as a machine and is getting me depressed and tired. Also I don’t find happiness in “validating” or “patching” AI slop code :(. This week I had to see a javascript code from Claude code that was was: let kids (was the name of the variable), that had nothing to do with schools or toys or whatever the heck the AI had in mind. I’m tired of being AI’s code babysitter, I’d rather write the code myself. Thank you for all the hard work you put on this website. Greetings from Portugal (hence my grammar errors :D )

I’ve never really left a comment on CSS-Tricks, so this is my first to say I really appreciated this article and your thoughts on it. It’s been a very tired few years, lay-off laden and hype driven. And it’s nice to see someone caring about the learning, as that’s what drove me into writing code and doing mathematics in the first place. It’s a good feeling to see someone understanding that, in a field that seems to care less and less about understanding itself–hard to think of it as a “science” these days if we do not learn.

If we ignore that AI is one of several threats to kill us all (apart from climate catastrophes, a possibly nuclear world war—famines and generally decreasing living standards in consequence—, the disintegration of our societies with widespread civil unrest, and other increasingly probable threats), then I believe one way of looking at AI is that professionally, we’re in the unfortunate situation that it uproots what we’ve learned and invested in.

It’s probably normal for many (anyone in the field for 3+ years) to feel uncomfortable and unhappy about this effect of AI—especially in a world that says “everyone on their own” and knows out-groups that can be neglected or worse. But no matter how normal, the discomfort and the unhappiness (and perhaps even anger) aren’t going to help anyone, and the sooner we get out of that zone, the better it is for us individually and collectively. That seems especially true because we have important problems to address with AI, problems “just don’t use it” won’t solve (it won’t—AI isn’t going anywhere).

In it with everyone else here, I’m betting on sucking it up, adapting, and doing one’s part to push this in a constructive direction. This is not meaning to give up—it’s pragmatism and redirection of energy, because the ride has only just begun. What’s to come probably needs all our attention and energy.

(What do I know though;—and with the best wishes to this great part of our community!)

#10098. scroll-state()

The CSS scroll-state() query, surprisingly, has nothing to do with whether you’re doom-scrolling in a state of existential dread. It does, however, allow you to change the CSS used based on scrolling behavior. Far from doom, that’s pretty neat-o speedo. (Okay, you’re right, there’s nothing neat-o about a speedo). You can do lots of things that used to require JavaScript, like revealing the navigation when a user scrolls up or animating in a back to top link once the user scrolls down.

While scroll-state(), with those slimming parentheses, looks like a function, it’s not technically one. It’s a container query (@container) like style(). Since scroll-state() is a query, the website is basically asking the browser whether a particular feature of a container has a specific value. If it does, the styles within the brackets will apply. If it doesn’t have that feature at all or has the feature, but the value is different, the @container query is false and the styles in the brackets will not apply.

In the following demo (which we’ll dissect in a bit), a “Back to Top” link slides in from the bottom as you scroll down the page.

scroll-state() has four possible features and each of those has a variety of possible values.

Asks if a container has been scrolled in a given direction:

Asks if a container can be scrolled in a given direction:

Asks if a sticky-positioned element is stuck:

Asks if a snap target has been snapped on a particular axis:

First let’s look at the scrolled feature. This is the demo we saw at the beginning where we animate a back-to-top link when we scroll down:

When the scroll container is scrolled toward the block-end (downward in this case) the translate applies and the .toplink element animates up into the visible area. Because we’re using a transition, when we scroll a different direction (up and down/block-start and block-end are the only options here), it nicely animates out.

While the scrolling direction in the demo above is pretty clear, what if this page was translated into a different language? Or we had a hero image that covered the entire viewport? One way we could indicate the scroll direction would be to have an arrow or other signifier animating and pointing in the correct direction. (I’m more a believer in constructing your page such that scroll direction is pretty obvious; however, I can foresee situations where an indicator was the best user experience or where translations/writing-mode changes could obscure what scrolling direction was appropriate.)

One nice thing about the scrollable feature is that when you scroll to the end of the scrollable area, it shuts off. So when we scroll to the block-end of the page (bottom of the page in this instance) scrollable: block-end is no longer true and our scroll indicator will revert to its default (in this case, display: none).

You could also combine the scrollable and scrolled features to remove the scroll indicator once the container has been scrolled either in the block-start or block-end direction. (We need both, because if we’re down in the middle of the page and we scroll up, scrolled: block-end will no longer be true and the scroll indicator will appear again.)

Another way we could enhance our demo is to have our navigation stick at the top while we’re scrolling down and change the look of the navigation once it’s stuck. The stuck feature must be on the element that gets stuck (as opposed to the scroll container, as was the case above). Also, because the container is set on the element that gets stuck, you cannot select the element itself from within the container query. You’ll need to position: sticky a wrapper element and “demote” all styles you want to change to the child.

The work of setting up the stuck feature happens in that .header-wrapper, but since we can’t apply styles to the container from within the container (not even with :has()). We need most of the styling to be in the

element.

Changing the size of an element in the header that affects the size of the header can lead to conflicting results. i.e. you scroll to a position where the header is stuck, which initiates the size change, but that reduces the size, which pulls it away from the stuck point, which cancels the size change which makes it stuck again, which initiates the size change and round and round we go. Even a small change can make it impossible to even scroll past the stuck point. Adding a transition can help with this and make scrolling work again, but you can still scroll to a place where the resizing happens in a loop. In the following demo, if you scroll down to a pixel or so past where the header touches the top of the viewport, you should see the stutter step.

There is one weird trick to fix this, though. No, really, there is! (shakes fist at the internet for making “one weird trick” an unbelievable phrase.) The one weird trick is to set a height on the element that’s getting stuck (.header-wrapper in the case above). Ideally, set it smaller than the final size of the header if you’re making the header smaller. This prevents the element from resizing because of a change in its content’s size. This does require the header content to overflow. If that’s a no-go for you, you’ll have to change its size with transformations. The transformed element has a sort of placeholder that stays in the DOM where the transformed element originally lived. This placeholder is what’s used to determine the sticky position and does not change based on what happened in the animation.

We can also add some styles when scroll snapping has been applied and that scroll has snappity-snapped (technical term). Similar to the stuck feature, snapped should be defined on the element that is snapped, not on the scroll container. If scroll snapping has not been applied to the element, then the snapped feature returns false and the container query does not apply.

First, we set up our scroll snapping in the scrolled container.

Note: As with most scrolling CSS (scrolling animations, etc.), if you’re using the document’s default scroll, the element is what’s being scrolled, not the .

The slide

s are what we’re going to snap to the size of the viewport:

As said above, the container type and name go on the snapped item. And then we modify descendants of the slide once it snaps:

Again, as with stuck, you cannot apply styles directly to the snapped element/the container, but only to the children of that element/container.

#10093. Andy Clarke answers six questions about Standout Designs for the Web

Web designer, author, and Stuff & Nonsense founder Andy Clarke has released Standout Designs for the Web, a new e-book that encourages designers to move beyond familiar templates and conventional layouts to create more personality-driven websites.

The book draws on Clarke’s thirty years of web design experience. It explores how storytelling, color, typography, imagery, and layout can work together to create websites that people recognize and remember. We asked Andy a few questions about the book and the ideas that went into it.

I’ve been designing websites for over thirty years, and I can’t remember a time when designers had more tools, techniques, and technology available to them. Yet, somehow, so many websites look remarkably similar.

I’m not suggesting every website needs a novelty design. Far from it. But I do think that — somewhere along the way—designers have become a little too comfortable with familiar patterns and predictable layouts.

I wrote Standout Designs for the Web to encourage designers to rediscover the creativity and personality that made me fall in love with the web in the first place.

Absolutely. Familiarity helps people understand how websites work, and there’s no reason to make something difficult to use just to be different. The problem is when familiarity makes every design look alike. When you visit a hotel website, an insurance company, or an online shop, you should be able to recognise something of the company behind it.

I call this sameness “commodity design,” and it’s perfectly fine when all you want is fine, but it does little to make a company recognisable or its website memorable. I’m not arguing against using conventions. I’m asking designers to make more thoughtful decisions about when to use them.

When I talk about storytelling, I don’t mean writing a lengthy company history or filling a website with marketing messages.

Every company has a story worth telling. It might be how it started, the people behind it, its personality, or something unexpected about what it does. Those details can influence everything from the words you write to the colours, typography, illustrations, and layouts you choose.

I spend a lot of time talking to clients because I’m looking for what makes them different. Sometimes, something they mention almost in passing becomes the inspiration for an entire design. The trick is recognising which details are worth developing and turning them into something visual.

Ha! Because I wanted a vehicle for my explanations and to have some fun. Wheelman is a fictional company for getaway drivers, and it gave me the freedom to explore ideas without worrying too much about the practical constraints of a real client project. Its characters, criminal connections, and rather questionable business activities gave me plenty of opportunities to experiment with personality.

But there’s a serious point behind the silliness. I use Wheelman to demonstrate how a company’s character can influence ordinary business pages, e-commerce, editorial content, and even transactional forms. If I can make a donation form for retired criminals interesting, designers have no reason not to bring more personality to everyday websites.

I think twelve-column grids have become the default setting for web design. They’re familiar and useful, but they’re only one way to organise content. When designers begin every project with twelve columns, they’re already limiting their possibilities before they’ve considered what the content needs.

In the book, I explore column, compound, and modular grids; ratios; symmetry versus asymmetry; and other ways to create rhythm and movement across a page. I’m not suggesting designers abandon column grids, but I want them to understand layout well enough to choose the right type and combine several—to make their layouts more interesting.

I’d like them to approach their next project with a little more curiosity. Before opening Figma or Sketch, choosing a typeface, or deciding on a layout, I’d like them to spend more time discovering what makes the company they’re designing for interesting.

Then I’d encourage them to question their first design decisions. Does this layout suit the content, or is it just familiar? Does this typography express the company’s personality? Can an illustration communicate something more effectively than an AI-generated image or a stock photo?

I don’t expect every website to be extraordinary, and I certainly don’t think every design needs to break conventions. But I’d like designers to feel more confident making decisions that give their work character.

You can purchase Standout Designs for the Web in the CSS-Tricks store.

Andy Clarke shows you how to create designs that people recognize and remember in his brand-new e-book, Standout Designs for the Web. Order it today!

Really interesting interview! I always enjoy seeing how experienced designers approach creativity, typography and visual storytelling on the web. A well-designed website is not just about appearance; it’s also about creating an enjoyable experience for visitors. Andy’s perspective on web design is inspiring, especially for people building and improving online platforms. Thanks for sharing this conversation!

#10092. What an AI Marketing Agent Actually Does, and How to Build a Guarded One

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

An AI marketing agent is a system that pairs a language model with access to real tools, such as a CRM, an ad platform API or a CMS, and lets it work toward a goal instead of following a fixed script. That is the core idea. Much of what gets labeled agentic marketing is either a wrapper around that loop or a scheduled job with a new name.

This article breaks down the mechanics in code terms, walks through a minimal guarded agent loop with tests, and gives a framework for deciding what to build, what to buy and what to keep away from any autonomous system. Product capabilities change quickly, so check each vendor's current documentation.

● An agent is a goal, a tool set and a loop. The model chooses the next action at runtime instead of following branches you wrote in advance.

● Guardrails belong in code. Spend caps, allow-lists and approval thresholds should never depend on the prompt.

● The prototype is the easy part. Validation, retries, logging and evaluation are where the weeks go.

● Match autonomy to reversibility. Let agents do reversible, measurable work and keep irreversible actions supervised.

● Treat vendor claims as claims. Ask for logs and test on your own data.

Many marketing features have been renamed in the last couple of years. Email sequencing, lead scoring and campaign tools are now often described as agents. The honest question is whether a given product is a real architecture shift or marketing automation with a chat box added. Both exist: some systems run a perceive-reason-act loop with dynamic tool selection, while others are if/else branches with a model writing the subject line.

Strip away the marketing language and an agent has three parts:

● A goal, expressed in natural language or as a measurable target.

● A tool set: functions the model is allowed to call, each described by a schema.

● A loop that runs until the goal is met, a limit is hit or a human steps in.

The reasoning step is a model call. You pass it the current state, the goal and the available tools, and it responds with text or a structured request to call a tool. Your code runs the call, feeds the result back and asks again. OpenAI's function calling guide describes this as a conversation between your application and the model, which is the primitive underneath most agent frameworks.

Compare that with a cron job. A cron job runs a rule such as pausing ads below a click-through threshold every morning. It cannot decide that cost per acquisition matters more than click-through this week. An agent can, because the branching is chosen at runtime by a model rather than at write time by you. A useful test: if you can draw the full decision tree before the system runs, it is automation. If the path depends on what the model decides after seeing the data, it is an agent.

● Perceive. Read current state from an ad platform's reporting endpoint, a CRM webhook or a database. Ordinary code, no model involved.

● Reason. One model call containing the goal, the state, a summary of past actions and the schemas of every tool the agent may use.

● Act. The model returns a structured tool call. Your code validates the arguments against the schema and your guardrails, then executes.

● Observe. Capture the result, including failures. A rate-limit error or validation error is information for the next step.

● Update memory. Record the action and outcome so the agent does not repeat itself.

● Repeat or stop. Loop until the goal is met, an iteration cap is reached or a guardrail escalates to a human.

Steps two and three rely on function calling, where tools are declared with typed parameters and the model returns matching arguments. The Hugging Face function calling guide shows the same pattern. For connecting tools across systems, the Model Context Protocol is an open protocol for integrating LLM applications with external data sources and tools. Instead of writing an adapter for every CRM and ad account, you point an MCP client at a server that exposes those tools with discoverable schemas.

Lead scoring and routing. The agent reads CRM state, enriches it with lookups, scores the lead and writes back an owner and next action. It is agentic when it chooses which enrichment tools to call based on what is missing, and not agentic when the score is a fixed model output piped into a rule.

Ad creative generation and testing. The agent produces variations from brand inputs, launches a structured test, reads performance data and shifts budget toward what works. This is among the clearest closed feedback loops because the outcome signal is numeric and fast.

IBM's overview of AI agents in marketing frames a similar progression, from scripted chatbots at one end to agents that decide, design workflows and use function calling at the other. Most products sit somewhere in between.

Vendors sometimes blur this line. Four criteria help even from the outside:

Practical tests: give it a goal it has no preset workflow for and see whether it composes a path or returns an apology. Change the data mid-run and check whether the next step differs. Ask for the tool call log, because a real agent produces one. Ask what happens when a tool call fails; a script throws, while an agent should retry or re-plan.

Three questions decide this, and none are about how clever the model is: can errors be detected quickly, can the action be reversed, and what does one bad decision cost?

● Reasonable to hand over: drafting copy variations, summarizing campaign performance, flagging data mismatches, writing internal briefs, pausing clearly underperforming ads.

● Keep supervised: budget reallocation across accounts, legal, medical or financial claims, pricing and discount changes, messages to named customers and final brand-voice sign-off.

A human approval step costs minutes. An unsupervised mistake in those areas can cost far more.

For many teams the practical answer is to build the orchestration that touches proprietary data and internal systems, and to evaluate off-the-shelf tools for narrow, high-volume tasks that other teams have already solved. What people underestimate when building is not the prototype, which can take an afternoon, but everything after: schema validation, retries, idempotency, rate-limit handling, prompt versioning, cost controls, an audit log and an evaluation harness.

Ad creative is a common candidate for the existing-tool route because volume is high and the loop is similar across businesses. Vendors in this space describe their products differently, so compare scope, integrations and pricing. As one example, Larven describes an AI marketing agent that runs Meta ads, pulls products and copy from a store such as Shopify, and connects to your own ad account. Treat any such description as the vendor's own, and verify it with a trial on your data before committing.

The goal: keep spend inside a daily cap while letting a model propose budget changes. The model is injected as a function, so you can wire in any provider's function-calling API or a stub for tests. The important part is that every limit is enforced in code. The model proposes, and your code decides.

"""A minimal guarded agent loop. The model is injected as a function, so you can swap in any provider's function-calling API, or a stub for tests.""" from dataclasses import dataclass, field @dataclass class Guardrails:     allowed_campaigns: set     daily_cap: float            # max total daily budget across campaigns     max_change_pct: float = 0.2 # larger single changes need a human     max_actions: int = 5        # per run @dataclass class State:     budgets: dict               # campaign_id -> daily budget     log: list = field(default_factory=list)     pending: list = field(default_factory=list) def check(call, state, g):     """Return 'run', 'approve' or ('deny', reason). Enforced in code, never by the prompt."""     if call["name"] != "adjust_budget":         return ("deny", "unknown tool")     cid, new = call["args"]["campaign_id"], call["args"]["new_daily_budget"]     if cid not in g.allowed_campaigns or cid not in state.budgets:         return ("deny", "campaign not allowed")     if new < 0:         return ("deny", "negative budget")     others = sum(v for k, v in state.budgets.items() if k != cid)     if others + new > g.daily_cap:         return ("deny", "daily cap exceeded")     old = state.budgets[cid]     if old and abs(new - old) / old > g.max_change_pct:         return "approve"     return "run" def run(model, state, g):     """model(state) -> a tool call dict, or None when it has nothing to do."""     for in range(g.maxactions):         call = model(state)         if call is None:             break         verdict = check(call, state, g)         state.log.append({"call": call, "verdict": verdict})         if verdict == "run":             state.budgets[call["args"]["campaign_id"]] = call["args"]["new_daily_budget"]         elif verdict == "approve":             state.pending.append(call)     return state

The tests use a stub in place of a model. They confirm that small changes run, large changes wait for approval, the daily cap and allow-list are enforced, an unknown tool is denied, and a model that never stops is halted by the action limit with everything logged:

from agent_loop import Guardrails, State, check, run G = Guardrails(allowed_campaigns={"a", "b"}, daily_cap=200) def call(cid, new): return {"name": "adjust_budget", "args": {"campaign_id": cid, "new_daily_budget": new}} def test_small_change_runs():     assert check(call("a", 105), State({"a": 100, "b": 50}), G) == "run" def test_large_change_needs_approval():     assert check(call("a", 150), State({"a": 100, "b": 50}), G) == "approve" def test_cap_and_allow_list_are_enforced():     s = State({"a": 100, "b": 90, "c": 10})     assert check(call("a", 115), s, G) == ("deny", "daily cap exceeded")     assert check(call("c", 11), s, G) == ("deny", "campaign not allowed")     assert check({"name": "delete_account", "args": {}}, s, G) == ("deny", "unknown tool") def test_loop_stops_at_action_limit_and_logs_everything():     s = State({"a": 100, "b": 50})     g = Guardrails({"a", "b"}, 500, max_actions=3)     run(lambda st: call("a", st.budgets["a"] + 1), s, g)   # a model that never stops     assert len(s.log) == 3 and s.budgets["a"] == 103 def test_denied_and_pending_actions_do_not_change_budgets():     s = State({"a": 100, "b": 50})     g = Guardrails({"a", "b"}, 500)     seq = iter([call("a", 150), call("a", 600), None])   # needs approval, then breaks the cap     run(lambda st: next(seq), s, g)     assert s.budgets["a"] == 100 and len(s.pending) == 1

Run them with pytest. In a real deployment, the model function would call your provider with the state, the goal and the tool schemas, then return the parsed tool call.

● It handles one tool. A real agent needs several, such as reporting, pausing ads and requesting approval.

● It keeps state in memory. Production systems need a durable store and idempotent actions so a retry cannot apply a change twice.

● The thresholds are placeholders. Set your own caps and percentages from your spend and risk tolerance.

● It validates the call but not the data. If upstream numbers are wrong, the agent will act on them.

Runaway spend. Budget APIs act instantly and changes compound. Without a hard ceiling in your own code, a reasoning error becomes a billing event.

Dirty upstream data. Stale CRM records, a broken product feed or a misattributed conversion. The agent can act confidently on wrong inputs and leave a tidy audit trail of well-reasoned bad decisions.

Missing audit trails. If you cannot reconstruct why an agent acted, you cannot fix or defend it. Deloitte's State of AI in the Enterprise reports that close to three-quarters of companies plan to deploy agentic AI within two years, and that only 21% of those companies report having a mature model for agent governance.

● Least-privilege credentials. A separate API key per agent, scoped to exactly what it needs, never a shared admin token.

● Hard caps in code. Daily spend ceilings and a maximum percentage change per adjustment, enforced outside the model.

● Human-in-the-loop thresholds. Define which actions always need approval.

● Log every tool call. Inputs, outputs, errors, model and prompt version, with timestamps you can query.

● Start read-only. Run in suggest mode and compare recommendations with what your team would have done.

A system that pairs a language model with real tools and lets it pursue a goal. It reads state, decides on an action, executes it, observes the result and decides again.

Automation starts from a trigger and follows branches defined in advance. An agent starts from a goal and selects tools at runtime.

Not on current evidence. They handle high-volume, measurable, reversible work well. Strategy, positioning and final brand approval still sit with people.

Function calling, where tools are declared by schema and the model returns matching arguments, and increasingly the Model Context Protocol for connecting tools.

It depends on how quickly errors are detected, whether actions are reversible and what a bad decision costs. Automate the cheap and reversible; supervise the rest.

Wrong tool calls, runaway spend, confident action on dirty data and brand drift at scale. Missing audit trails make all of them harder to diagnose.

An AI marketing agent is less magic than it sounds: a goal, a set of tools and a loop with limits. The model's intelligence matters, but the system around it decides whether it is safe to run. Define the tools narrowly, enforce limits in code, log everything, start read-only and widen autonomy only as the evidence supports it.

#10089. Dev Material Archive

A new resource resurrecting lost web-dev articles and posts:

This is an archive of written articles about web development, web design, and web accessibility that once existed on the World Wide Web and which would otherwise be lost to time. Publications close, SEO strategies change, rights lapse, standards alter, content management systems are migrated between, acquisitions happen, websites get redesigned.

Whatever the reason may be, so much high-quality web writing has been lost through the years.

I love the effort, which is ongoing. Link rot is a real thing. It’s links that point to either removed content or a page that’s changed URLs. Either way, it’s an awkward topic in our field. No one is obligated to archive and retain content, but doing so clearly benefits us all.

A big thanks to this site for helping preserve the things we care about. The same can said about Jay Hoffman and his long-running work on The History of the Web.

Andy Clarke shows you how to create designs that people recognize and remember in his brand-new e-book, Standout Designs for the Web. Order it today!

They aren’t directly navigable but you will stumble upon them in the https://dxsrc.org/topics/2000s/ and https://dxsrc.org/topics/2010s/.

I'd love the original publication date beside the archive date on each piece. Coming back to an old browser workaround is much more interesting when you can see which constraints the author was working around.

#10088. Gemini 4 Hydrogen: a faster model for general purpose robotics

Over the past month, Gemini 4 Argon and Gemini 4 Carbon brought frontier physical reasoning to robotics: understanding 3D scenes, reconstructing real environments in simulation, practicing tasks in sim before running them on hardware, and directly controlling robot arms from camera images. Last week, we introduced Gemini 4 Hydrogen, which brings strong spatial and embodied reasoning to a much faster model built for speed and efficiency.

To help developers put Hydrogen to work, today we're launching the Robotics plugin on Google Antigravity: a set of skills for hardware onboarding, simulation and robot control turning Antigravity into a one-stop robotics workbench. Together, they enable a developer to go from a robot in a box to one that can perform a working application in an afternoon. To install the plugin, open Antigravity, go to Customizations and search for Robotics on the Build With Google shelf.

Gemini 4 Argon and Carbon showed that a general model can drive a robot arm directly, and Gemini 4 Hydrogen preserves that ability. Give it a goal in plain language and a camera view, and it plans the task, writes and runs the control code, and keeps adjusting until the job is done. On the Artificial Analysis Robotics 0.1 benchmark, Gemini 4 Hydrogen controls a real 6-axis arm through eight everyday tabletop tasks, from pouring a drink to packing blocks into a box. Hydrogen scores 58, similar to Argon at a lower cost per token.

Hydrogen brings the specialized skills of our Gemini Robotics ER 2 (Embodied Reasoning) models into the core Gemini 4 model series, and it beats them on nearly every axis. On the public ERQA benchmark, Hydrogen outperforms Gemini Robotics ER 2 on spatial reasoning tasks such as multi-view correspondence and 3D trajectory reasoning. For developers using Gemini Robotics ER 2 today, we recommend trying out Gemini 4 Hydrogen for these workloads.

A fast, capable model is only half the story. Building a robot application has always meant gluing together drivers, calibration scripts, simulators, perception code, and a UI, which requires robotics expertise and a lot of time. The new Robotics plugin for Google Antigravity packages that work into skills Gemini can use on your behalf:

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Here's what that looks like end to end: set up a robot and build real applications.

Starting from an empty desk, a developer plugs an I2RT YAM arm and an external depth camera into a laptop and asks Hydrogen in Antigravity to bring it up. The plugin's Skills install the software, find the camera, calibrate the arm, and run test motions. The arm is calibrated and ready for tasks in about 10 minutes. A similar flow also brings up a low-cost SO-ARM101 arm and an industrial grade FR3 Duo from scratch to live waving.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Once the robot is live, you can use a simple prompt to create a basic task within just a few minutes:

Once the robot is live, the next step is building a simulation you can trust. With the Robotics plugin, MuJoCo runs directly inside Antigravity with dedicated creation, auditing, and rendering skills. This allows you to build and preview high-quality simulated scenes and assets—whether described, imported from links/documents, or uploaded via workspace photos—right alongside your code. You can then "vibe sim" by adjusting shapes, positions, and physical properties through chat to verify reaching and grasping before deploying to physical hardware.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

The workbench with Hydrogen and Robotics plugins lets you go from an idea to a repeatable working application in real world in a much shorter time, by iterating and validating it in simulation.

A developer starts with a whiteboard sketch: a conveyor belt carrying mixed snacks, with a robot arm picking and sorting them into bins. From one prompt of the task with images of a real scene, Hydrogen constructs an interactive MuJoCo simulation of the cell, testing and validating the grasping policy in sim across multiple runs. The developer then deploys the verified policy directly to the robot in the real world. When nuances emerge (such as tuning gripper closure force or refining sorting rules), the developer iterates in natural language, asking Hydrogen to adjust the controller to iterate. Within about an hour end-to-end, the arm reliably sorts snacks off the moving belt.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Hydrogen's efficiency makes it practical to run a multi-step robot application all day. In this example, a developer uses Hydrogen to build a small web storefront for a soap shop, then connects it to two YAM Pro arms. When an order comes in, the arms set out a packing bin, pick the ordered mint and citrus bars, and pull a printed thank-you note from a printer.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

With just a few prompts, the FR3 Duo simulates a chess board and then can play chess against itself or take on human opponents.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Gemini 4 Argon pushes the ceiling of physical reasoning. Gemini 4 Hydrogen makes those capabilities fast and affordable enough to use every day, and the Antigravity Robotics plugin puts them in a workbench where anyone can go from a robot in a box to a working application. We can't wait to see what you build.

You can share your own creations and use cases on the plugin’s GitHub discussions page. If you have any ideas, custom skills, or derived work you think would be helpful, please let us know—we’ll keep improving the plugins.

#10085. Using Fallbacks for a Robust Front-End

Anyone who has been working in web development for a while will be familiar with this now-legendary meme from xkcd. It illustrates just how fragile the technical infrastructure of a website or app can be.

That’s why it’s important to use fallbacks to build a robust front end, either to quietly and discreetly avoid the error or to communicate it to our users.

The issue of robustness is so important that it is actually one of the four principles of the Web Content Accessibility Guidelines (WCAG)! So how can we provide a modern front end without excluding users who use browsers and hardware that are only partially compatible or not compatible at all?

The answer lies in implementing multiple safety nets in HTML, CSS, and JavaScript, so that we are prepared for the worst-case scenario and can provide our users with the best possible experience.

The main difference between the use of fallbacks and progressive enhancement lies in the directions in which the two approaches aim.

While progressive enhancement is about automatically making certain features available as long as they are supported (forward), fallbacks are about what happens when something is not available or accessible (backward).

For example, we can offer a shiny new CSS feature that makes interacting with our website even more enjoyable. But if this feature isn’t available in some browsers, no one will notice, because a version that’s supported by older browsers is available instead. Progressive enhancement in action!

Another example: If JavaScript cannot be loaded for a critical application, we provide a fallback that either offers basic functionality or, at the very least, displays a message explaining what the problem is.

While one doesn’t necessarily exclude the other, their approaches are quite different. My personal recommendation is to always work with progressive enhancement as a guiding principle and to consider fallbacks whenever possible.

To evaluate new features intended for production, we must first determine the extent to which a feature is supported.

Since 2023, the Interop project has been in place, in which major browser vendors collaborate to release new features for the web platform more or less simultaneously. This serves as a general overview.

Related to this is the Baseline project, which shows, for specific features, the extent to which they are supported and how long they have been available. However, it is extremely important to note that it does not track browser support for accessibility features built into the web platform!

Another important resource for checking compatibility is Can I Use. Here, you can get a quick overview of browser support for various features.

Let’s start with the foundation of the World Wide Web: HTML. Modern tech stacks place a strong emphasis on client-side rendered JavaScript, which can offer many benefits to users in more complex applications. However, there can be just as many reasons why JavaScript might fail to load, so we need a strategy to address this.

In the event of a total failure, we should redirect users to an appropriate error page where they receive information about what went wrong and what not only they, but also we, can do to prevent this in the future. If you want to know how to write error messages that actually help users rather than frustrate them, check out Amy Hupe’s amazing article.

Because this kind of error feedback is so essential, the static site generator Astro, for example, offers the ability to add custom 404 and 500 error pages. You could discuss other possible error codes with your back-end team to implement additional safeguards.

But what if we also want to make individual sections or components more robust in case JavaScript isn’t available? For this, we can use the 

If JavaScript is not available, we will still display the components, but explain in simple and clear terms that they cannot be loaded at the moment and why that is the case.

We give users the option to reload that specific component so they can resolve the issue themselves. We also offer them the option to contact us directly via email if the problem persists.

Here, you could add a pre-written text to the  tag, which could include, for example, an internal error code specific to that component in the subject line and have a corresponding body text. This way, they would only need to click the button and could send the email directly using their email program of choice. Support would then have all the necessary information to resolve the issue without needing to ask for additional details.

No matter what your use case is, this pattern can be universally adapted and extended, and ensures that it works even if everything else goes wrong.

Let’s stick with our fictional dashboard component. Say we want to use the CSS anchor() function to display the data, which is fairly new and has solid, but not great, browser support.

Since this is a feature that can include multiple attributes, we need to check whether the ones we want to use are actually available. This is exactly where the @supports CSS rule comes into play! Here, it’s recommended to use the and operator: It should only be used if all attributes are available. If not, the more robust CSS with better browser support is used.

Here’s another fictional, simplified example:

This approach also serves as documentation: Once the feature has been around for a while and is more well-established, you can look directly for the @supports rule in your code base and check whether it is still necessary, along with its fallback.

Javascript offers us the ability to make our front end even more resilient with minimal effort. To do this, we can use the if…else statement to check whether a specific feature is available. For example, if we want to use the relatively new Navigation API, we could check for it as follows:

If we use a component-based approach, where HTML, CSS, and JavaScript are all contained in a single file, we can also use the conditional ternary operator to display this component or parts of it, or do nothing at all.

Our fictional dashboard component relies on data from the back end, and it only makes sense to display it once the data has successfully reached the front end. So, for example, we could do the following:

Depending on the use case, it makes sense to display a fallback or even nothing at all. You could also define the conditions more specifically, depending on how robust you want it to be.

My experience so far at various companies has been that the topics of fallbacks and robustness haven’t played as prominent a role as they really should have.

This is surprising, because as we’ve learned, it doesn’t take much to implement various safety nets on the front end, as long as you think ahead about what (shouldn’t) happen if something goes wrong.

HTML, CSS, and JavaScript provide us with specific elements and functions to ship robust code and thus avoid upsetting our users and, in the worst case, causing them to turn away from us.

#10083. Style Queries as Conditional Modifiers

This article is adapted from a lesson in Writing Resilient CSS, the newest module in Kevin’s course, CSS Demystified. The module covers configuration options with custom properties, math functions, relative colors, :has(), style queries, and more, all put to work in practical, reusable patterns. The course is 32% off until December 2, 2026.

Modifiers have been with us for a long time, often with modifier classes, but also with attribute selectors. Some common examples are things like different versions of alerts and buttons.

For a lot of use cases, either of those approaches work great, but when it comes to layout, a lot of the time we want the context of where the element is being used to control things.

A card is a nice example of this, where we might have several versions.

The issue here is, if I’m using .card--compact, I probably want to do that for all the cards within a section. That can be annoying to apply to each card, but that’s rendered moot since most people are doing some sort of looping in a template like this these days.

And this works great, as long as we always want the cards to be compact.

However, you might put this together and send it off to the design team for approval, only for them not to like that when the compact cards are lower down the page, the smaller padding isn’t needed to create visual separation between the two sections. Instead of helping with the hierarchy, it’s simply creating a strange visual inconsistency.

We could use something like a resize observer to try and deal with this, but we have a better tool at our disposal: Container Style Queries, which are now Baseline Newly Available.

While they all start with container, for the rest of this article I interchangeable use container query or size query for a container size query, and style query for what we’re talking about here.

Before we get into the good stuff, a quick look at the basics of style queries.

You’re probably familiar with size queries, which work similar to @media but look at the size of a container, rather than the viewport. Container style queries use the same @container at-rule as container size queries, but instead of looking at a size, they look at the value of a custom property.

This reads as, “if the nearest container has --theme: high-contrast, style the .card like this.”

Just like with size queries, style queries look at an ancestor, not the element itself. That means this won’t work:

The custom property needs to be set on something above the card. Sometimes this can be annoying, and it always tends to catch people out, but because of how I use them, I don’t find this to be much of a limitation.

if() is also on the way, and allows for style queries as well, but if() is able to see the value of custom properties on the element it’s being used. The big difference is it can only be used for a single property, while style queries can be used for rules with multiple properties.

Let’s take a look at a card again, first with a modifier like we might do now. To change it up, we’ll use an attribute modifier instead of a class.

Switching this up to use a style query, we could do something like this instead:

If we’re in the mindset of how we’re used to using modifiers, this feels like a lot more work with no real payoff, but now, anything that can set a custom property can switch it: a media query, a container query, a :has() selector, or a parent section. The styles for the alternative layout are written once, and there are lots of ways to switch them on, making it so much more versatile.

One easy win here is if you’re working with a CMS. Now, the author of the page can chose from an option, which just sets one custom property value for a section of the page, and the appropriate styles kick in.

Where I feel like this has the most potential is when dealing with conditional styles like the sidebar we looked at earlier, where we can enable the compact card layout however we want:

Here’s an overflow grid pattern I use quite a bit:

The problem is that it always overflows, even if I don’t have enough children to warrant it. Like here, the first row, the third card is just barely overflowing, making it awkward more than anything.

One solution is to use a container query and :has(), where we let the items fill the space when there are fewer than five of them, and there’s enough space:

And this works really well! But sometimes, we might need to make some bigger changes depending on where the elements are being used.

Let’s say, for example, our page has a hero component that goes to two columns on larger screens, with a heading on one side and a set of featured articles in an overflow grid on the other. The container query is making sure the overflow is on in the narrow space, but horizontal scrolling there seems a little awkward.

When the split happens, I’d rather the grid scrolled vertically instead, and we can leverage style queries to pull this off.

First, we give the overflow grid a configuration option for its direction, defaulting to columns:

Now the split can change when it goes to two columns:

And this works, but the result isn’t great. The grid is still sized for horizontal scrolling, so we end up with a tall, awkward column, and no overflow at all.

This is where style queries come in. The overflow grid can “listen” for the direction change and adjust its own styles to match.

The split section isn’t styling the overflow grid directly, which is possible, but always awkard, and leads to fragile code bases. Instead, we created some configurable settings on that grid, and simply adjusted them as needed.

I don’t think style queries mean it’s time to throw out modifier classes (or data modifiers). They still work great, especially since a style query can’t look at the element it’s styling. If I simply want my button to be an accent button, or a pill shape, then a modifier makes a lot of sense.

On top of that, a regular media query or container query, or even an intrinsic pattern might get the job done, and that’s great too.

Where style queries shine is when the condition and the styles need to live in different places, or when one condition needs to affect several things at once, like the split above. Instead of a layout reaching in and styling its children, it changes a few settings, and each component decides how to respond.

That’s a big part of what I think of as writing resilient CSS: components that keep working, and adapt sensibly, no matter where they end up.

This article is adapted from a lesson in Writing Resilient CSS, the newest module in my course, CSS Demystified. The module covers configuration options with custom properties, math functions, relative colors, :has(), style queries, and more, all put to work in practical, reusable patterns.
